February 5, 2026
                                               Abstract


This dissertation consists of an introduction and six chapters, covering a variety of combinatorial prob-
lems, with common themes running throughout.

   Chapter 2 is devoted to the study of partially ordered sets, or posets, in particular the structure of
chains and antichains in infinite posets. A poset P is said to satisfy the finite antichain condition, or
FAC, if it has no infinite antichain. It was conjectured by Aharoni and Korman in 1992 that any FAC
poset P possesses a chain C and a partition into antichains such that C meets every antichain of the
partition. Our main results are twofold. We provide a counterexample to the conjecture but, despite this,
we also prove that the conjecture does hold true for a broad class of posets. In particular, we prove that
the Aharoni–Korman conjecture holds for countable posets avoiding intervals I such that either I or its
reverse I ∗ is of the form x∈ω Qx , where each Qx is infinite and co-wellfounded. In pursuit of these
                          L

goals, we also investigate other facets of the structure of FAC posets. In particular, we consider strongly
maximal chains in FAC posets, proving some results, and posing several questions and conjectures.

   In Chapter 3, we address several related problems on combinatorial discrepancy of trees in a setting
introduced by Erdős, Füredi, Loebl and Sós. Given a fixed tree T on n vertices and an edge-colouring
of the complete graph Kn , for every colour, we find a copy of T in Kn where the number of edges in
that colour significantly exceeds its expected count in a uniformly random embedding. In particular,
this resolves a problem posed by Erdős, Füredi, Loebl and Sós by generalising their work from two
to more colours. Furthermore, if T has maximum degree ∆ ≤ εn for sufficiently small ε > 0 and
the edge-colouring of Kn is both balanced and “not too close” to one particular colouring, we show
that, for every colour i, there is a copy of T in Kn where colour i appears on Θ(n) more edges than
any other colour. Several related examples are provided to demonstrate the necessity of the introduced
structural restrictions. Moreover, when ∆ is a constant, we extend these results to sufficiently dense
host graphs in place of Kn . Our proofs combine saturation arguments for the existence of particular
coloured substructures and analysis of conveniently defined local exchanges. Using similar methods, we
investigate the existence of copies of a graph H with prescribed number of edges in each colour inside
2-edge-coloured dense host graphs. In particular, for a graph H with bounded maximum degree and
balanced 2-edge-colourings c of a host graph G with minimum degree at least (1 − ε)n for some ε > 0,
we show that, for any sufficiently large n and sufficiently small ε, there exists a copy of H where the
number of edges in the two colours differ by at most 2. Moreover, we completely characterise the pairs
(H, c) for which the difference of 2 cannot be improved. As a consequence, we refute a conjecture by
Mohr, Pardey, and Rautenbach.

   In Chapter 4, we are interested in finding a colour-balanced perfect matching within a colour-
balanced complete graph K2nk with a palette of k colours. An edge-colouring of a graph G is said to
vi


be colour-balanced if there are equally many edges of each available colour. While it is not necessarily
possible to find such a perfect matching, one can ask for a perfect matching as close to colour-balanced
as possible. In particular, for a colour-balanced colouring c : E(K2nk ) → [k], we seek to find a perfect
matching M minimising f (M ) := ki=1 |c−1 (i) ∩ M | − n . The previous best upper bound, due to
                                       P
                                                  √
Pardey and Rautenbach, was min f (M ) ≤ O(k nk log k). We remove the n-dependence, proving the
                                              2
existence of a matching M with f (M ) ≤ 4k for all k.
   In Chapter 5, we provide an extended abstract of a series of results centred around Rademacher
sums. The object of interest is P( ni=1 ξi vi ∈ S), where ξi are independent random variables sam-
                                  P

pled uniformly on {−1, +1}, each vi is a vector in Rd for some fixed d, and S is some set. We first
                                                                              Pn
consider anti-concentration probabilities of the form P(|X| ≥ x), where X =      i=1 ai ξi and ai are
                                      Pn
positive real constants normalised so i=1 a2i = 1. We determine the value of inf X P(|X| ≥ x) for
all values x ≥ 0, giving a partial answer to a question by Keller and Klein. In particular, for x = 1,
we prove the optimal lower bound P(|X| ≥ 1) ≥ 7/32, which improves on a sequence of results by
Burkholder, Oleszkiewicz, Hitczenko and Kwapień, and Dvořák and Klein, and confirms a 1994 conjec-
ture of Hitczenko and Kwapień.
    We then move on to consider the case of d ≥ 2, and investigate a 1945 conjecture of Erdős, which
                                                                      Pn
states that, for any unit vectors v1 , . . . , vn in R2 , the sum σ =  i=1 ξi vi satisfies ∥σ∥2 ≤ 1 with
                                                                                               √
probability Ω(1/n). While this conjecture is false for even n, Beck has proved that ∥σ∥2 ≤ 2 always
holds with probability Ω(1/n). Recently, He, Juškevičius, Narayanan, and Spiro conjectured that the
Erdős’ conjecture holds when n is odd. We disprove this conjecture by exhibiting vectors v1 , . . . , vn for
which ∥σ∥2 ≤ 1 occurs with probability O(2−n/2 ). On the other hand, an approximated version of their
conjecture holds: we show that we always have ∥σ∥2 ≤ 1 + δ with probability Ωδ (1/n), for all δ > 0.
This shows that, when n is odd, the minimum probability that ∥σ∥2 ≤ r exhibits a double-jump phase
transition at r = 1, as we can also show that ∥σ∥2 ≤ 1 occurs with probability at least Ω((1/2 + µ)n ) for
some µ > 0. Additionally, and using a different construction, we give a negative answer to a question
of Beck and two other questions of He, Juškevičius, Narayanan, and Spiro, concerning the optimal
                                                     √
constructions minimising the probability that ∥σ∥2 ≤ 2. We also make some progress on the higher
dimensional versions of these questions.
     Finally, we demonstrate that the parity of n continues to be a significant factor for dimensions d ≥ 3.
In particular, we prove that, for any d ≥ 3, there is some ε = ε(d) > 0 such that for any n ̸≡ d (mod 2)
and unit vectors v1 , . . . , vn ∈ Rd , there are signs η1 , . . . , ηn ∈ {−1, +1} such that ∥ ni=1 ηi vi ∥ ≤
                                                                                              P
√                                            √
  d − ε, and so P(∥ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn ∥ ≤ d − ε) > 0. This is in contrast to the case of n ≡ d (mod 2),
wherein the above probability can be zero.
     The final two chapters concern percolation on finite graphs. Percolation is a topic of central impor-
tance to probability theory, and we consider two different problems in this area.
     In Chapter 6, we consider the bunkbed conjecture, which has featured in the folklore of probability
                                                                                                           vii


theory since at least 1985, and concerns bond percolation on the product graph G□K2 . We have two
copies G0 and G1 of G, and if x(0) and x(1) are the copies of a vertex x ∈ V (G) in G0 and G1 respec-
tively, then edge x(0) x(1) is present. The conjecture states that, for vertices u, v ∈ V (G), percolation
from u(0) to v (0) is at least as likely as percolation from u(0) to v (1) . In this chapter we consider three
natural generalisations of the bunkbed conjecture; to site percolation, to hypergraphs, and to directed
graphs. Our main aim is to show that all these generalisations are false, and to this end we construct a se-
quence of counterexamples to these statements. However, we also consider under what extra conditions
these generalisations might hold, and give some classes of graph for which the bunkbed conjecture for
site percolation does hold.
    In Chapter 7, we work with percolation on vertex expander graphs. Given a graph G, the percolated
graph Gp is formed by retaining each edge independently with probability p. Collares, Diskin, Erde,
and Krivelevich initiated the study of large structures in percolated single-scale vertex-expander graphs,
wherein every set of exactly k vertices of G has at least dk neighbours before percolation. We extend
their result to a conjectured stronger form, proving that if p = (1 + ε)/d and G is a graph on at least
k vertices which expands as above, then Gp contains a cycle of length Ωε (kd) with probability at least
1 − exp(−Ωε (k/d)) as k → ∞.

   Beyond those I have directly worked with, the fantastic mathematical environment in Cambridge has

and problems, and thought-provoking discussions to be had. I would in particular like to thank Julian

Chapter 1


Introduction

1.1    The Aharoni–Korman conjecture

In Chapter 2 we provide a thorough resolution of the Aharoni–Korman conjecture, both providing a
counterexample to the conjecture, and proving that the conjecture does hold with one additional technical
assumption.
   Posets with no infinite antichains have a rich and intricate structure, but are relatively under-studied
as objects of interest in their own right. One of the most significant problems in this area is due to
Aharoni and Korman, who conjectured in 1992 [9] that any FAC poset P possesses a chain C and a
partition into antichains such that C meets every antichain of the partition. The problem arose naturally
from—and has strong links to—the study of covers and matchings in infinite hypergraphs.
   This conjecture is often referred to as “the fishbone conjecture” (due to how the structure of the
chains and antichains resembles the skeleton of a fish), and there have been many attempts to prove
the conjecture; indeed, it has been described as “one of the most attractive” conjectures in the area [6].
However, despite this attention, little progress has been made on this problem.
   We demonstrate why the conjecture has proven so resilient, and provide a counterexample to the
conjecture. However, we also note that our construction is somewhat complex. The main bulk of this
chapter goes some way to explaining why such complexity is necessary, by proving that the conjecture
does hold true for a large class of posets. In particular, for the Aharoni–Korman conjecture to fail, a
poset must contain an interval of a particular pathological type.
   This interval to be avoided can be thought of as a wellfounded sequence of co-wellfounded posets
(or the reverse of such), and we call a poset avoiding these intervals vacillating. This demonstrates that
the Aharoni–Korman conjecture is true for a large class of posets and, together with the counterexample,
provides a thorough resolution of the conjecture.
   In the process of investigating this conjecture, we consider another structure in posets which has to
date received little attention: the strongly maximal chain. In this case we give a positive result, proving
2                                                                                                Introduction


that such objects can be found in any countable FAC poset. We also hope to encourage more research in
the area by posing several open problems and conjectures.


1.1.1   The Aharoni–Korman conjecture

A subset X of a partially ordered set, or poset, P , is a chain if the elements of X are pairwise comparable,
and it is an antichain if its elements are pairwise incomparable. If P has no infinite antichain, then we
say that it satisfies the finite antichain condition, and for brevity we will refer to P as an FAC poset.
    In 1992, Aharoni and Korman [9] posed the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.1.1 ([9], Conjecture 4.1). If a poset P contains no infinite antichain then, for every positive
integer k, there exist k chains C1 , . . . , Ck and a partition of P into disjoint antichains (Ai : i ∈ I) such
that each Ai meets min(|Ai |, k) chains Cj .

    Conjecture 1.1.1—or sometimes the k = 1 case thereof—is referred to as the Aharoni–Korman
conjecture or the fishbone conjecture. We will use the former name, and work exclusively with the case
of k = 1.
    To give an example, if P is the poset on the set N × N with ordering given by setting (x, y) ≤
(u, v) if and only if x ≤ u and y ≤ v, then the k = 1 case of Conjecture 1.1.1 holds by taking
C = {(0, y) : y ∈ N} and Ai = {(x, y) ∈ P : x + y = i} for all integers i ≥ 0. However, this
partition into antichains is not unique. For example, we could instead take all antichains of the form
{(x, y), ((x + y)(x + y + 1)/2 + y, 0)} for x, y ≥ 0, and these are also pairwise disjoint, partition P ,
and all meet C.
    Our first main result is the following.

Theorem 1.1.2. There is a countable poset P for which Conjecture 1.1.1 is false.

    The poset P we construct is somewhat large and complicated. The rest of the content of this chapter
works towards proving that any such counterexample is necessarily large in a certain sense, by proving
that Conjecture 1.1.1 does in fact hold for a broad class of posets.
   We say that a chain C in a poset P is contiguous if there is no x ∈ P \C and y, z ∈ C with y < x < z
                                      L
and C ∪ {x} a chain. The linear sum i∈I Pi of a collection (Pi : i ∈ I) of posets indexed by a total
                          F
order I is the ordering on i∈I Pi wherein all elements of Pj are set to be greater than all elements of
Pi whenever j > i in I. Following standard set-theoretic notation, we write ω or N for the set of natural
numbers. In particular, we use N to refer to the (unordered) set of natural numbers, and ω to refer to
the poset naturals with the standard ordering. We write C ∗ for the poset equal to C with the ordering
reversed.
    We now state our second main result, before giving a more detailed overview of our contributions in
Section 1.1.3.
1.1. The Aharoni–Korman conjecture                                                                           3


Theorem 1.1.3. Let P be a countable FAC poset such that, for any contiguous chain C ⊆ P , neither
C nor C ∗ can be written as i∈ω Ci with each Ci infinite and co-wellfounded. Then there is a chain
                            L
                          S
C ⊆ P and partition P = i∈I Ai into antichains such that C meets every antichain Ai .

    We now review the existing literature on this topic, and then provide a more thorough overview of
our contributions.


1.1.2   Known results

Questions concerning the partition of posets into various structures can be traced back to Dilworth’s
theorem [42], originally published in 1950, which states that a poset P with width at most k has a
partition into at most k chains. As will become a theme throughout this introduction, the generalisation
to infinite posets presented some further challenges. Indeed, Perles [106] noted that the strongest “infinite
Dilworth’s theorem” one could hope for—that any poset of width at most λ can be partitioned into at
most λ-many chains—is false, as, for example, for any infinite cardinal κ, the poset κ × κ has no
infinite antichain, and thus width ℵ0 , but cannot be partitioned into fewer than κ-many chains. However,
Dilworth’s theorem can be generalised to infinite FAC posets P under the assumption that P has some
partition into finitely many chains, and this was proved by Abraham in 1987 [1].
    In 1976, Greene and Kleitman [65] generalised Dilworth’s theorem to families of antichains, proving
that, for any finite poset P and integer k, the maximal size of the union of k antichains of P is equal
                           P
to the minimal value of i≤m min(|Ci |, k) over all partitions {C1 , . . . , Cm } of P into chains. The
Greene–Kleitman theorem was then generalised to the infinite setting by Aharoni and Korman [9]. More
precisely, they proved the following theorem, which they described as “the ‘correct’ infinite version of
Greene-Kleitman’s theorem.”

Theorem 1.1.4 ([9], Theorem 3.1). Let P be a poset with no infinite chains and let k be a positive
integer. There exists then a partition (Ci : i ∈ I) of P into disjoint chains and k disjoint antichains Aj
(1 ≤ j ≤ k) such that every chain Ci meets min(k, |Ci |) antichains Aj .

    Having proved Theorem 1.1.4, Aharoni and Korman then asked about the dual version, interchanging
the roles of chains and antichains, which lead them to Conjecture 1.1.1, which we restate here.

Conjecture 1.1.5 ([9], Conjecture 4.1). If a poset P contains no infinite antichain then, for every positive
integer k, there exist k chains C1 , . . . , Ck and a partition of P into disjoint antichains (Ai : i ∈ I) such
that each Ai meets min(|Ai |, k) chains Cj .

    Conjecture 1.1.1 was later referred to by Aharoni as the “fishbone conjecture” (see for example [5]),
but is often called “the Aharoni–Korman conjecture”. The inspiration for the conjecture in fact came
from the consideration of strongly minimal covers and strongly maximal matchings in hypergraphs (see
4                                                                                               Introduction


for example [4]), and has close links to infinite graph theory as a whole. One such related area is the
generalisation of Menger’s theorem to infinite graphs. This problems has a rich history, and led to many
partial results [3, 8, 41] before the seminal paper of Aharoni and Berger [6] proved the full generalisation.
Indeed, in [6], Conjecture 1.1.1 was described as “one of the most attractive” conjectures in the area of
infinite matching theory.
    Aharoni and Korman also posed several other, more general conjectures in [9]. In 1995, Aharoni
and Loebl [10] resolved one of these conjectures, concerning perfect graphs, in some special cases.
Much more recently, the strongest of the conjectures from [9], concerning strongly minimal covers of
hypergraphs, has been disproved by van der Zypen [117].
    While there has been some progress towards proving Conjecture 1.1.1, the previous state of the art
was still a considerable way from a resolution of the conjecture. Indeed, the case of k = 1 and posets
of width at most 2 was dealt with by Aharoni and Korman themselves [9] by application of an infinite
version of König’s theorem due to Aharoni [2].
    The first partial result after the conjecture was introduced was due to Duffus and Goddard [45] who
proved that Conjecture 1.1.1 holds in two different cases. Firstly, they proved the conjecture for posets
of the form C × P , where C is a chain and P is finite (this result in fact first appeared in Duffus’ doctoral
thesis [64]). Secondly, they proved the conjecture for FAC posets with no infinite intervals. We will use
the second of these results as a black box in our proofs, so we now state it formally.

Theorem 1.1.6 ([45], Theorem 4.1). If P is an ordered set with no infinite intervals and no infinite
antichains then there is a partition of P into antichains and there is a chain of P that intersects every
member of the partition.

    Since then, Zaguia has recently proved a series of results, now collected into a single paper [121],
showing that Conjecture 1.1.1 is true when the FAC poset P is N -free (that is, if w, x, y, z ∈ P have
w ≤ y, x ≤ y, and x ≤ z, then some further comparison must hold between these four points), or has
locally finite incomparability graph. The incomparability graph of a poset P is a graph G with vertex
set P , and xy is an edge of G if and only if x ⊥ y in P . We will also find use for the latter of these two
results, which Zaguia deduced from Theorem 1.1.6. For ease of referencing the theorem, we now state
this result here as well.

Theorem 1.1.7 ([121], Theorem 6). If P is a poset whose incomparability graph is locally finite, then
there is a partition of P into antichains and there is a chain of P that intersects every member of the
partition.

    Taking a more broad view, beyond just partial results on Conjecture 1.1.1, FAC posets in general have
a rich structure, but have received only sparse attention. One topic of particular interest has concerned
completions of these posets, that is, extensions of the posets for which certain families of subsets are
1.1. The Aharoni–Korman conjecture                                                                         5


guaranteed to have suprema and infima. Duffus [46] studied several different extensions of posets, and
gave an example in which the MacNeille completion of a poset P (the minimal complete poset containing
P ) contains an infinite antichain even when P has no antichain of order 3. Some further results on this
topic were obtained by Lawson, Mislove, and Priestley [93].
    Another direction of research is the study of strongly maximal structures. Given a set family F, a set
A ∈ F is maximal if B ⊇ A and B ∈ F implies that A = B. Moreover, A is strongly maximal if, for
every B ∈ F, we have |A \ B| ≥ |B \ A|. For example, one could consider the family of chains of a
poset.

Definition 1.1.8. A chain C of a poset P is a maximal chain if no element of P \ C is comparable to
every element of C. C is a strongly maximal chain, or SMC for short, if for every chain D ⊆ P , we have
that |C \ D| ≥ |D \ C|.

    As was noted and discussed by Aharoni [4], the following theorem is a consequence of Theo-
rem 1.1.4.

Theorem 1.1.9. If P is a poset with no infinite chain, then P contains a strongly maximal antichain.

    Later, Aharoni and Berger [7] strengthened Theorem 1.1.9 by producing a weaker condition which
also implies the existence of a strongly maximal antichain. Strongly maximal chains, however, remain
largely unstudied.


1.1.3    Our contributions

Before considering Conjecture 1.1.1, we will first consider strongly maximal chains, or SMCs, in posets,
as in Definition 1.1.8. It is natural to consider the dual of Theorem 1.1.9, and thus arrive at the following
conjecture.

Conjecture 1.1.10. If P is an FAC poset, then P contains a strongly maximal chain.

    We note here that it is not difficult to construct a poset of width ℵ0 (and having antichains of that
size) with no strongly maximal chain. While we are not able to resolve Conjecture 1.1.10 in full, we can
prove a partial result: in Section 2.3, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1.11. Any countable FAC poset has a strongly maximal chain.

    The proof of Theorem 1.1.11 will also serve as a warm-up for some of the techniques we will use to
attack Conjecture 1.1.1.
    The remainder of our results are in pursuit of determining for which posets Conjecture 1.1.1 holds.
As is the case in all work on Conjecture 1.1.1 to date, we focus on the case of k = 1. To ease the
discussion of these results, we make the following definition.
6                                                                                               Introduction


Definition 1.1.12. Given a poset P , we say that a subset C ⊆ P is a spine of P if C is a chain and there
is a partition of P into antichains (Ai : i ∈ I) such that C meets every antichain Ai .

    In this language, the Aharoni–Korman conjecture states that every FAC poset has a spine. While
Theorem 1.1.2 demonstrates that Conjecture 1.1.1 is false in general, we present the following class of
posets, which we can prove contain spines.

Definition 1.1.13. We say that a poset P is vacillating if there is no contiguous chain C in P for which
either C or its reverse C ∗ can be written as i∈ω Ci , where each Ci is infinite and co-wellfounded.
                                             L

    For example, any finite union of wellfounded and co-wellfounded posets is vacillating, as is the
Cartesian product ω × ω ∗ . However, the lexicographic product of ω and ω ∗ is not vacillating, as it can
by definition be expressed as i∈ω ω ∗ .
                             L

    With this definition in hand, we can restate Theorem 1.1.3 as follows.

Theorem 1.1.14. If P is a countable vacillating FAC poset, then P contains a spine.

    Our proof of Theorem 1.1.14 proceeds in several steps, which will be outlined in detail at the start of
Section 2.4.
    Returning to the statement of Conjecture 1.1.1, while we believe that our results may extend to larger
values of k, resolving the k = 1 case of the conjecture seems to be by far the most important point, and
so we have expended little effort attempting to generalise our results to larger values of k.
    We also remark here that our proof of Theorem 1.1.2 given in Section 2.5.2—that Conjecture 1.1.1
is false—is largely self-contained, and does not rely on the results of previous sections.
    Before proceeding further, we say a few words about our proof techniques. The more serious proofs
presented here rely on a proof technique which we develop, and will refer to as using “replacements”.
These techniques are used on three occasions: to find η-strongly maximal chains in Section 2.3, to find
alternating-maximal chains in Section 2.4.1, and to find reduced chains in Section 2.4.2.
    The intuition captured by these replacements is that we wish to find a chain which is strongly maximal
with respect to some particular property. For example, η-strongly maximal chains are chains in which
one cannot remove a single point and insert in its place a copy of Q. To find such a chain, we would
like to argue that if we keep applying such replacements, removing a point and inserting a copy of Q,
we must eventually “exhaust” the poset P , as it has no infinite antichains. This intuitive approach is
formalised by considering a partial order on chains of P , where two chains are comparable if one can be
reached by a sequence of replacements from the other.
    While the proofs themselves are essentially appeals to Zorn’s lemma, it is, in the author’s opinion,
the definitions of the partial orders where the real work is happening. Indeed, in all three cases, we will
define a particular partial order on a family of chains of a poset P (e.g. on maximal chains), and will in
general say that one chain C can be “replaced” by D if there is a function f : C → D which will be in
1.2. Discrepancies of spanning trees                                                                     7


some sense order-preserving and injective. The other conditions on the function f will be set up so that
this replacement operation will be a partial order and infinite chains will have upper bounds, allowing us
to apply Zorn’s lemma.



1.2     Discrepancies of spanning trees

Discrepancy theory is a relatively recent and very active subfield of mathematical optimisation and algo-
rithm design. Its goal is to study the existence and the properties of unbalanced substructures of some
weighted or coloured mathematical structure. The roots of this subject may be found in the pioneering
work of Weyl [119]; relations to classical mathematical fields like number theory, combinatorics, ergodic
theory and discrete geometry were exhibited shortly after that. For a broad introduction to the subject,
we recommend the outstanding books of Beck and Chen [19], Matoušek [99] and Chazelle [37].
    The work presented in Chapter 3 contributes to a line of research initiated by Erdős, Füredi, Loebl
and Sós [53] who showed that there is a constant c > 0 such that, for every tree T on n vertices and
maximum degree ∆ and every 2-colouring of the edges of Kn , for some c > 0 one can find a copy of T
where one of the colours is given to at least c(n − 1 − ∆) more edges than the other colour. Alternatively,
in the language of discrepancy, the above result claims that some copy of T has discrepancy at least
c(n − 1 − ∆).
    In this 2-colour setting, the discrepancy of the tree can be viewed as the ‘imbalance’ between the two
colours, that is, the difference of the sizes of their pre-images. However, there is more than one natural
way to adjust this notion to more colours. In this chapter, we consider several of them.
    Our results go in two different directions. First, for any edge-colouring of Kn with r ≥ 2 colours, we
generalise the Erdős–Füredi–Loebl–Sós theorem by exhibiting copies of a tree T where the number of
edges in some colour is significantly over-represented. Later, we extend our study to approximately bal-
anced edge-colourings of dense host graphs (Kn being a particular example) and find copies of T where a
predetermined colour class contains significantly more edges than any other colour class. Along the way,
we exhibit several curious pathological constructions which indicate that certain structural constraints
we rely on are actually needed. These results are described in Sections 1.2.1 and 1.2.2.
    In a different direction, for a given graph H with constant maximum degree, under certain constraints
on a 2-colouring of the host graph G, we exhibit copies of H with a prescribed number of edges in each
colour. The relevant results are described in Section 1.2.3.


1.2.1   Maximising the number of edges in some colour

Our first result generalises the main theorem of Erdős, Füredi, Loebl and Sós [53] to r ≥ 2 colours,
thereby resolving a problem raised in the same paper.
8                                                                                            Introduction


Theorem 1.2.1. There exists a universal constant c > 0 such that the following holds. Fix an integer
r ≥ 2 and an r-colouring of the edges of Kn . Then, for every tree T on n vertices with maximum degree
∆, there exists a colour i ∈ [r] and a copy of T in which the number of edges of colour i is at least

                                        n−1   c
                                            + 2 (n − 1 − ∆).
                                         r   r

    In fact, we derive Theorem 1.2.1 from a more general result, where we generalise the main result
of [53] in two ways: we consider colourings in r ≥ 2 colours and, for each of the colours involved,
we find a copy of T where the number of edges in that colour significantly exceeds its expected count
in a uniformly random embedding. Theorem 1.2.1 follows from the next result applied to some most
commonly met colour.

Theorem 1.2.2. There exists a universal constant C > 0 such that the following holds. Fix an integer
r ≥ 2 and an r-colouring of the edges of Kn . For every colour i ∈ [r], denote by mi the number of
edges of colour i. Then, for every i ∈ [r] and every tree T on n vertices with maximum degree ∆, there
is a copy of T with at least
                                                                      2
                                                                m∗i
                                                            
                                   2mi
                                        + C(n − 1 − ∆)
                                    n                           n2
                                               
edges of colour i, where m∗i := min mi , n2 − mi .
                                           


    Note that Theorem 1.2.2 can be used to find copies of the tree T where the colour i is under-
represented instead: indeed, one can group the colours in [r] \ {i} together into a new super-colour
and apply Theorem 1.2.2 for that super-colour.
    The topic of discrepancy in edge-coloured graphs has received considerable attention in the last few
years. Balogh, Csaba, Jing and Pluhár [13] studied the discrepancy of spanning trees and Hamilton
cycles in 2-colourings of graphs with high minimum degree. Notably, they showed that every graph on
n vertices with minimum degree at least (3/4 + ε)n with any ε ∈ (0, 1/4) contains a Hamilton cycle
with discrepancy at least εn/32. This result was later generalised independently by Freschi, Hyde, Lada
and Treglown [56] and Gishboliner, Krivelevich and Michaeli [60] to more colours. In particular, for
every r ≥ 2, both works showed that every r-coloured graph on n vertices with minimum degree at least
(1/2 + 1/2r)n + d contains a Hamilton cycle where some colour is given to a least n/r + cd edges where
c = 1/6r2 in [56] and c = 2 in [60]. Moreover, [60] further generalised the results on spanning trees
from [13] by exhibiting weak connectivity conditions for the host graph which are sufficient to provide
the existence of a spanning tree with high discrepancy for any number of colours (for a suitable extension
of discrepancy to the multi-colour case).
    Gishboliner, Krivelevich and Michaeli [59] also studied r-colourings of the Erdős-Rényi random
graph G(n, p) above its sharp threshold for Hamiltonicity pHam = (log n + log log n)/n. They proved
that, with high probability, for every r-colouring of the edges of G(n, p) with n(p − pHam ) → ∞,
1.2. Discrepancies of spanning trees                                                                         9


there is a Hamilton cycle with at least (2/(r + 1) − o(1))n edges in the same colour. A similar re-
sult holds for perfect matchings. In the 2-colour setting, the discrepancy of powers of Hamilton cycles
was further examined by Bradač [25]. Balogh, Csaba, Pluhár and Treglown [14] studied the case of
Kr -factors and obtained a discrepancy version of the Hajnal-Szemerédi theorem by identifying the min-
imum degree threshold for the existence of a Kr -factor with high discrepancy. Bradač, Christoph and
Gishboliner [26] then generalised their result and, for any graph H, found tight minimum degree con-
ditions for the existence of an H-factor with linear discrepancy. Krishna, Michaeli, Sarantis, Wang
and Wang [89] generalised graph discrepancy in the 2-colour setting by introducing high-dimensional
discrepancy where every edge is associated to a unit d-dimensional vector for some d ≥ 1. They in-
vestigated high-dimensional discrepancy in a setting where the host graph is an n-vertex tree T and the
subgraphs of interest are all subtrees of T . Finally, the discrepancy of tight Hamilton cycles in k-uniform
hypergraphs was the main focus of a recent work by Gishboliner, Glock and Sgueglia [61].


1.2.2     Maximising the number of edges in a given colour

While Theorem 1.2.2 guarantees the existence of a copy of T where a certain colour is over-represented,
it does not give any information about the proportion of edges in any other colour. The following natural
question arises: if the edges of G are approximately evenly distributed among all colours, does there
necessarily exist a copy of the tree T where a fixed colour is met more frequently than any other colour?
Our next result shows that this is not the case, even when restricted to trees with maximum degree
∆ ≤ εn for any small ε > 0.
    We say than an r-colouring of a graph is balanced if the number of edges in any two different colours
differs by at most 1. Also, for an integer ℓ ≥ 1, a balanced ℓ-caterpillar is a tree where every vertex
lies within distance 1 of a central path of length ℓ, and the degrees of the vertices in this path differ by at
most 1.

Proposition 1.2.3. There is a constant ε0 ∈ (0, 1) such that, for all 0 < ε < ε0 and integers r ≥ 1/ε5 ,
the following holds. There is n0 = n0 (ε, r) such that, for all n ≥ n0 , there is a balanced r-colouring of
Kn such that, in every copy of a balanced ⌈1/ε⌉-caterpillar on n vertices in Kn , the number of edges of
colour 1 is smaller than the number of edges in one of the colours 2, . . . , r.

    While the above result only holds for a large number of colours, it turns out that a similar result holds
for every r ≥ 3 and all trees with maximum degree at least 23n/24 + O(1).

Proposition 1.2.4. There is a constant C > 0 such that the following holds. For every integer r ≥ 3
there is an integer n0 = n0 (r) such that, for all n ≥ n0 , there is a balanced r-colouring of Kn such that
for every tree T on n vertices with maximum degree ∆ ≥ 23n/24 + C and every copy of T in Kn , the
number of edges of colour 1 is smaller than the number of edges in some colour among 2, . . . , r.
10                                                                                             Introduction


     Propositions 1.2.3 and 1.2.4 make it clear that additional conditions are necessary if we aim to find
a copy of a tree T in an arbitrary balanced r-colouring of Kn where colour 1 is met substantially more
often than any other colour. A natural restriction, consistent with the previous results, is to impose an
upper bound on the maximum degree of T . However, even for spanning paths, certain almost balanced
colourings can still behave pathologically.

Example 1.2.5. Fix r = 4, n = 2N and let V1 , V2 be a partition of the vertices of Kn into two equal parts.
Colour all edges within V1 (resp. V2 ) with colour 1 (resp. colour 2), and assign colour 3 (resp. colour 4)
to ⌊N 2 /2⌋ edges (resp. ⌈N 2 /2⌉ edges) between V1 and V2 . In this almost balanced 4-colouring of Kn ,
every Hamilton cycle contains the same number of edges of colour 1 and of colour 2. As a result, the
difference between the number of edges of colours 1 and 2 in every Hamilton path is as most 1.

     Curiously, the construction in Example 1.2.5 turns out to be the unique obstruction in a certain sense.
To make this statement precise, we introduce the notion of an η-standard edge-colouring of a graph G on
n vertices.

Definition 1.2.6. For every η > 0, an r-colouring of G is η-standard if, for every partition V1 , V2 of its
vertex set with |V1 | − |V2 | ∈ {0, 1} and every integer i ∈ [2, r], G[V1 ] contains at least ηn2 edges of
colour different from 1 or G[V2 ] contains at least ηn2 edges of colour different from i.

     In essence, η-standard colourings are those that are “not too close” to the colouring described in
Example 1.2.5, although the second colour is not necessarily colour 2. In particular, it is not hard to
check that all balanced colourings in r ̸= 4 colours are η-standard for all sufficiently small η.
     For any tree T on n vertices with suitably bounded maximum degree, the next theorem ensures
that, in any almost balanced η-standard colouring of Kn , there is a copy of T where colour 1 is met
substantially more frequently than any other colour. More precisely, we say that an r-colouring of a
graph G is ε-balanced if each colour i is given to a (1/r ± ε)-proportion of the edges of G, that is, a
proportion in the interval [1/r − ε, 1/r + ε].

Theorem 1.2.7. For every integer r ≥ 3 there is η0 = η0 (r) > 0 such that, for all η ∈ (0, η0 ), the
following holds. There is ε = ε(r, η) > 0 and an integer n0 = n0 (r, η) such that, for every n ≥ n0 and
every tree T on n vertices and maximum degree ∆ ≤ εn, every ε-balanced η-standard colouring of Kn
in r colours contains a copy of T where colour 1 is given to at least (η 2 /106 r3 )n more edges than any
other colour.

     It is natural to wonder whether Theorem 1.2.7 extends to dense graphs in place of complete graphs.
The following example, inspired by a construction in [13], shows that Example 1.2.5 is not the only
exceptional construction in case the minimum degree of the host graph drops below 3n/4.

Example 1.2.8. Let n = 4N and partition the vertex set of Kn into two parts V1 and V2 of size N and
3N , respectively. Let G be a graph obtained from deleting all edges connecting two vertices of V1 as well
1.2. Discrepancies of spanning trees                                                                     11


as the edges of an (N − 1)-regular graph on the vertex set V2 (note that this is possible since 3(N − 1)N
is even). In particular, G is a 3N -regular graph. Then, colour all edges incident to V1 blue and all other
edges red. One can easily check that a spanning path of G contains either 2N − 2 blue edges (if it starts
and ends in V1 ), or 2N − 1 blue edges (if it starts in V1 and ends in V2 ), or 2N blue edges (if it starts
and ends in V2 ), so the difference between the number of red and the number of blue edges is bounded
independently of N in each case.

    While it would definitely be of interest to determine if 3n/4 is a barrier for the existence of obstruc-
tions qualitatively different from Example 1.2.5, our approach allows us to treat host graphs of minimum
degree at least (1 − ε)n for small ε > 0 depending on r, η and the maximum degree of the tree.

Theorem 1.2.9. Fix integers r ≥ 3 and ∆ ≥ 2. There exists η0 = η0 (r) > 0 such that the following
holds. There is ε = ε(r, ∆, η) > 0 and an integer n0 = n0 (r, ∆, η) such that, for every n ≥ n0 ,
every graph G on n vertices and minimum degree at least (1 − ε)n, and every tree T on n vertices and
maximum degree at most ∆, every ε-balanced η-standard colouring of G in r colours contains a copy of
T where colour 1 is given to at least εn more edges than any other colour.

    We note that our proofs are sufficiently flexible to derive analogues of our main results for graphs
containing many vertices of degree one or many adjacent pairs of vertices of degree two (such as sparse
random graphs) instead of trees. Nevertheless, for the sake of clean statements, we focus on trees.


1.2.3    k-sum subgraphs of dense 2-edge-coloured graphs

The rest of Chapter 3 is dedicated to finding copies of a graph H with a specified number of edges in each
colour within a suitably dense 2-edge-coloured host graph. For a 2-edge-colouring c : E(G) → {−1, 1}
(where we call colour −1 red, and colour 1 blue) and a subgraph G′ ⊆ G, define c(G′ ) = e∈E(G′ ) c(e).
                                                                                       P

For an integer k, a subgraph G′ ⊆ G is called k-sum if c(G′ ) = k.
    For a k-sum copy of a graph H in a host graph G to exist, several elementary conditions must be
satisfied:

    • k ≡ |E(H)| ( mod 2)) and |k| ≤ |E(H)|,
    • H is a subgraph of G, and
    • c must be sufficiently balanced (for example, if |k| ̸= |E(H)| and c is monochromatic, then there
        is no k-sum copy of H).

Taking these restrictions into account, it is natural to ask if, for integers k and ∆ > 0, and graph H
with maximum degree at most ∆ and a number of edges both large compared to k and respecting the
parity of k, there is always a k-sum copy of H in approximately balanced colourings of sufficiently dense
host graphs. We begin with a couple of motivating examples showing that this may be ruled out by an
additional number-theoretic constraint.
12                                                                                              Introduction


Example 1.2.10. Fix an integer n = 4m + 6 and partition the vertices of a graph Kn into two sets V1
and V2 such that |V1 | = 2m + 1 and |V2 | = 2m + 5. Colour all edges between V1 and V2 in red and all
other edges in blue. Then, every Hamilton cycle in Kn has discrepancy congruent to 2 (mod 4). Indeed,
given a Hamilton cycle of Kn , the number of red (resp. blue) edges in this colouring is 2ℓ (resp. n − 2ℓ)
for some integer ℓ ≥ 0, so its discrepancy is always congruent to n modulo 4.

     Before presenting the second example, we remark that it refutes the following conjecture of Mohr,
Pardey and Rautenbach [101].

Conjecture 1.2.11 (Conjecture 5 in [101]). Fix integers k, n ≥ 1 such that n2 and (k − 1)n/k are both
                                                                             

even integers. If T is a tree of order k, c : E(Kn ) → {−1, 1} is a 0-sum labelling of Kn , and n is
sufficiently large in terms of k, then Kn has a 0-sum T -factor, that is, there is a 0-sum spanning forest F
of Kn whose components are all isomorphic to T .

Example 1.2.12. Fix n = 16k 2 and m = 8k 2 − 2k for some odd k. Partition the vertices of Kn into sets
V1 and V2 such that |V1 | = m and |V2 | = n − m. Colour all edges between V1 and V2 red and all other
edges blue. In particular, the number of red edges is
                                                                   
                                        2           2            1 n4      2
                      m(n − m) = (8k − 2k)(8k + 2k) = 64k − 4k =      .
                                                                 2 2

Let S be a star on 8 vertices. We show that no S-factor in Kn is 0-sum. Fix an S-factor H and let
H1 and H2 be the subforests containing all stars with centers in V1 and V2 , respectively. Let s1 , s2 be
the number of stars in H1 , H2 and ℓ1 , ℓ2 be the number of red edges in H1 , H2 , respectively. Then,
8s1 − ℓ1 + ℓ2 = m, so ℓ1 + ℓ2 is even. Moreover, s1 + s2 = n/8 = 2k 2 and, since k is odd, we obtain
that s1 + s2 ≡ 2(mod 4)). As a result,


c(H) = (m − s1 − ℓ2 ) + (n − m − s2 − ℓ1 ) − (ℓ1 + ℓ2 ) = n − 2(ℓ1 + ℓ2 ) − (s1 + s2 ) ≡ 2(
                                                                                         (mod 4)).


This shows that there is no 0-sum S-factor in this graph.

     In fact, the absence of 0-sum copies in these examples reflects a more general phenomenon. Its
essence is captured by the following definition and the subsequent proposition.

Definition 1.2.13. For an integer n ≥ 1 and a spanning subgraph G of Kn , a colouring c : E(Kn ) →
{−1, 1} is called bipartite if there is a partition (V1 , V2 ) of the vertices of Kn such that either c−1 (1)
or c−1 (−1) is equal to the edge set of G[V1 ] ∪ G[V2 ]. Then, the sets V1 , V2 are called the parts of c.
Moreover, for an n-vertex graph H and G, c as above, the triplet (H, G, c) is called inert if all degrees
in H have the same parity and the colouring c is bipartite.

     Note that, in each of Examples 1.2.10 and 1.2.12, (H, Kn , c) is an inert triplet. This is not a coinci-
dence, as all inert triplets share the following property.
1.2. Discrepancies of spanning trees                                                                      13


Proposition 1.2.14. For every n ≥ 1, every inert triplet (H, G, c) and every copy H ′ of H in G, c(H ′ )
has the same residue modulo 4.

    More interestingly, inertness turns out to be the only obstacle for finding k-sum copies of a graph
subject to some natural conditions, as demonstrated by the following theorem.

Theorem 1.2.15. For every integer ∆ ≥ 1, there are constants ε = ε(∆), ν = ν(∆), α = α(∆) > 0 and
integers n0 = n0 (∆) and m0 = m0 (∆) such that the following holds for all n ≥ n0 and m ≥ m0 . Let
H be an n-vertex graph with m edges and maximum degree ∆, let G be an n-vertex graph with minimum
degree at least (1 − ε)n, and let c be a ν-balanced 2-edge-colouring of G. If the triplet (H, G, c) is inert,
set a = 4; otherwise, set a = 2. Then, there is an integer b ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3} such that, for every integer
k ∈ [−αm, αm], there is a copy H ′ ⊆ G of H with c(H ′ ) = k if and only if k ≡ b (mod a)).

    Note that, in the case of a non-inert triplet (H, G, c), Theorem 1.2.15 ensures the existence of a copy
H where the number of edges in the two colours differ by at most 2. Before outlining our proofs, we note
that the problem of finding spanning copies that are nearly colour-balanced has been addressed in several
papers. The earliest work in this setting is due to Caro and Yuster [29], who sought minimal-weight
copies of a graph H when the edges of the host graph is coloured with (a small set of) integers. Caro,
Hansberg, and Montejano [30] later specialised and refined these results to the setting of {−1, +1}-
colourings and H being a clique. The same three authors subsequently extended this work to several
other similar settings, such as unavoidable chromatic patterns, balanceable graphs and omnitonal graphs
[33] and amoebas [35]. Their work on balanceable graphs and omnitonal graphs was further extended
by Caro, Lauri and Zarb [32], while Caro, Hansberg, Lauri, and Zarb [34] investigated the conditions
under which a zero-sum spanning tree can be guaranteed. The specific case of perfect matchings has
been studied in [50, 74, 86, 104], while factors of P3 ’s and P4 ’s were considered by Mohr, Pardey and
Rautenbach in [101]. Finally, the case of spanning forests of Kn with fixed isomorphism class was
investigated in [80, 101, 105].


1.2.4   Brief outline of the proofs

Our proofs of Theorems 1.2.2, 1.2.7 and 1.2.9 rely on a switching technique that allows us to increase
or decrease the number of edges in a copy of T with a given colour via small local changes to the
embedding. More precisely, given a tree T on n vertices randomly embedded in the complete graph
Kn , we find some special pairs of edges uv, wz in T (described in the beginning of Section 3.3) which
can be exchanged with the edges uz, vw outside T such that the resulting graph remains a copy of T .
In the proof of Theorem 1.2.2, these switchings are used solely to increase the number of edges of a
fixed colour. However, a more careful treatment is needed in the proof of Theorem 1.2.7 where we
need to show that, for every i ∈ [r] \ {1}, a significant number of switchings increases the number of
14                                                                                              Introduction


edges of colour 1 but not the number of edges of colour i. As pointed out in Example 1.2.5, this is not
always possible; however, by counting suitably coloured 4-cycles in edge-colourings of Kn , we show
that this example is the only obstruction. Additional complications arise in Theorem 1.2.9 where edges
outside the host graph cannot be used. This proof can be seen as a modified version of the argument
showing Theorem 1.2.7 where some additional vertex exchanges are used to avoid the non-edges of G.
The construction showing Proposition 1.2.3 uses a colouring of a blow-up of a small complete graph
and is inspired by the finite projective plane PG(2, p). In contrast, Proposition 1.2.4 employs a more
straightforward colouring of the same blow-up.
     We turn to the complementary k-sum part. Our proof of Theorem 1.2.15 combines ideas from previ-
ous sections with new structural insights. First, unless (H, G, c) is an inert triplet, we find and reserve a
small part of the graph G (later called a 2-gadget), which will enable us to adjust the sum of the graph by
exactly +2 or −2. We then embed the graph H uniformly at random in Kn . We show that, with positive
probability, this random embedding contains

      • Ω(m) disjoint sets of vertices that allow us to change c(H) by ±4 (we call one such set of vertices
        a gadget),
      • a number of edges outside G bounded from above by a small constant times n,
      • and close to the expected number of edges in each colour.

Then, we exchange some pairs of vertices to obtain a specific configuration in the reserved part of G and
to remove any missing edge in the embedding. This process is carefully executed to ensure that

      • most of the gadgets remain untouched, and
      • the number of edges in each colour is not altered by much.

We finish the proof of Theorem 1.2.15 by triggering some of the untouched gadgets to produce an em-
bedding of sum precisely k. Finally, the proof of Proposition 1.2.14 relies on a simple degree-counting
argument in and between the parts of a bipartite colouring.


1.3      Colour-balanced perfect matchings

In Chapter 4 we find perfect matchings in complete graphs equipped with k-edge-colourings such that
the induced colouring of the perfect matching is very close to balanced. The problem of finding colour-
balanced perfect matchings can be considered a special case of zero-sum Ramsey theory, which has
received significant study in recent years. While work was initially concerned with zero-sum embeddings
over finite groups, Caro and Yuster [29] initiated the study of zero-sum embeddings over Z. Several
variants of this problem have since been studied; see for example [31, 30].
     Embedding perfect matchings in this context was first considered by Caro, Hansberg, Lauri, and Zarb
[34]. They asked whether every two-edge-colouring of a complete graph on 4n vertices with equally
1.3. Colour-balanced perfect matchings                                                                 15


many edges of each colour also contains a perfect matching with equally many edges of each colour.
This question was solved, affirmatively and independently, by Ehard, Mohr, and Rautenbach [50], and
by Kittipassorn and Sinsap [86]. Beyond solving this problem, Kittipassorn and Sinsap asked about
the generalisation of this problem to more than two colours. In particular, they [86] asked whether
every colour-balanced k edge-colouring of the complete graph K2kn admits a colour-balanced perfect
matching. By calling a colouring colour-balanced, we mean that there are equally many edges of each
colour (as is standard), and we also use the notation [k] := {1, 2, . . . , k}.
    Pardey and Rautenbach [104] resolved this question in the negative, giving a colour-balanced three-
colouring of K6 which admits no perfect matching with one edge of each colour. However, they also
relaxed the question to ask merely for an almost colour-balanced perfect matching, and proposed the
following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.3.1 ([104, Conjecture 1]). If n and k are positive integers, and c : E(K2kn ) → [k] is
colour-balanced, then there is a perfect matching M of K2kn with


                                               f (M ) ≤ O(k 2 ),                                   (1.3.1)


where

M with f (M ) ≤ 2, they could prove the following theorem.

[k], there is some perfect matching M of K2nk satisfying

the deterministic case presented here. In particular, a random (not necessarily balanced) colouring of a
complete graph contains a colour-balanced perfect matching with high probability.

Using the above definition will allow for us to improve a matching by changing only two edges at a time,

In particular, one of the key insights is that the above sum of squares can be written as a sum of “weights”
of edges in M , where the weight of an edge is the number of edges in M with the same colour.


1.4     Rademacher sums
                                                                                             Pn
Throughout Chapter 5 and this introduction, a Rademacher sum is a sum of the form                 i=1 ξi vi , where
the ξi are independent Rademacher random variables—that is, drawn uniformly from {−1, +1}—and
the vi are vectors in Rd for some fixed dimension d.
     In Section 1.4.1, we will be concerned with the case of d = 1, and the sum will be normalised to
have standard deviation equal to 1. In Sections 1.4.2 and 1.4.3, we will be concerned with the higher
dimensional case of d ≥ 2, and will primarily be working in the case wherein all vi are unit vectors.
     In the interest of brevity, we do not include full proofs of the results in this chapter, and present an
overview of the material. In particular, while we state all of the main results precisely and in full, we
give only outlines of proofs, and skip over many technical details. Full proofs can be found in the papers
[77, 78, 81] from which the work is drawn.


1.4.1    Anti-concentration of Rademacher sums over R
                                                                                                  Pn
The first problems we consider in this chapter concern random variables of the form X =              i=1 ai ξi , for
some n ∈ N, where ai some positive reals. We consider bounds on the tail probabilities P(|X| ≥ x). In
particular, letting X be the set of all Rademacher sums over R of variance 1 (i.e. with the normalisation
Pn      2
  i=1 ai = 1), we prove the following.

Theorem 1.4.1. For any x ≥ 0, the infimum


                                         f (x) := inf P(|X| ≥ x)

is attained by a Rademacher sum of the form              √1 ξi for some n ∈ {1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 7}.
                                                   i=1     n
1.4. Rademacher sums                                                                                       17


    We prove this result via the more precise Theorem 5.1.1, which confirms a simulation-inspired con-
                                                                                           √      √
jecture of Lowther [98], namely that f is piecewise constant with discontinuities at 0, 1/ 7, 1/ 5,
   √      √
1/ 3, 2/ 6 and 1.
    One case which has attracted particular interest is that of x = 1. Indeed, the problem of determining
f (1) has a significant history, and was first considered in 1968 by Burkholder [27], who proved that
f (1) > 0. This was then improved in 1994 to f (1) ≥ e−4 /4 by Hitczenko and Kwapień [70], who
furthermore conjectured that f (1) = 7/32. In 1996 the lower bound was improved by Oleszkiewicz
[103] to 1/10, which stood as the best bound until the problem was recently picked up by Dvořák and
Klein [47], who proved f (1) ≥ 3/16. We note here the following consequence of Theorem 1.4.1.

Theorem 1.4.2. For any X ∈ X , P(|X| ≥ 1) ≥ 7/32.

    This proves the conjecture of Hitczenko and Kwapień [70], effectively concluding this line of results.
We would like to remark that the problem of determining f (1) is a natural counterpart to Tomaszewski’s
conjecture, which is concerned with the concentration inequality instead of the anti-concentration in-
equality, stating that inf X∈X P(|X| ≤ 1) = 1/2. Tomaszewski’s conjecture received significant atten-
tion since it was posed in 1986 (see for instance [20, 24, 23, 48, 82]), until it was recently resolved by
Keller and Klein [85].
    Our proof expands on the methods of Dvořák and Klein [47] developed for the proof of the weaker
bound P(|X| ≥ 1) ≥ 3/16, which itself used some of the ideas from Keller and Klein [85].


1.4.2   Reverse Littlewood–Offord problems

We now consider Rademacher sums weighted with vectors in Rd for some d ≥ 2.
    In their seminal work of 1943, Littlewood and Offord [97] examined signed sums of complex num-
bers with unit norm and, in particular, the probability that these sums lie within an open ball of unit
radius. This research laid the groundwork for what is now known as Littlewood–Offord theory, which is
broadly concerned with bounds on the probability that the random signed sum ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn falls
within a target set S, where v1 , . . . , vn are fixed vectors and ξi are independent Rademacher random
variables, that is, ξi are uniformly distributed on {−1, +1}.
    Littlewood and Offord, motived by the problem of estimating the number of zeros of random polyno-
mials, considered the case where each vi is a complex number with norm at least 1, showing that the prob-
ability that ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn lies within any open ball of radius 1 is at most O(n−1/2 log n). While this
result was sufficient for their purposes, the best possible result was found in 1945 by Erdős [52], who used
                                                                 n
                                                                     −n
Sperner’s theorem to show that the probability is at most ⌊n/2⌋      2 , attained when v1 = · · · = vn = 1.
    In his influential paper of 1945, Erdős [52] posed two conjectures. The first of these asked for a
generalisation of the problem of Littlewood and Offord to an arbitrary Hilbert space, and was resolved
by Kleitman [87]. Erdős’ second conjecture is the following.
18                                                                                               Introduction


Conjecture 1.4.3 (Erdős). Let x1 , . . . , xn be unit complex numbers. Then the number of sums ni=1 ξi xi
                                                                                               P
                         Pn                                     n
with ξi ∈ {−1, +1} and      i=1 ξi xi ≤ 1 is greater than c2 /n for some absolute constant c > 0.


     Questions of this kind have been recently termed ‘reverse’ Littlewood–Offord problems, as the goal
is to show a lower bound on the number of signed sum ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn that lie in a specified set, rather
than an upper bound.
    It turns out that Conjecture 1.4.3 is false as stated, which can be seen in R2 by taking an odd number
                                                                                          √
of copies of (1, 0) and of (0, 1). Indeed, this forces all the sums to have norm at least 2. This observa-
tion, which was attributed to Erdős, Sárközy, and Szemerédi by Beck [18], was also made by Carnielli
and Carolino [28]. Both groups conjectured from this example that Conjecture 1.4.3 should hold if the
                         √
radius 1 is replaced with 2. This corrected version of the conjecture of Erdős was proven by Beck [18]
in 1983, who moreover obtained the analogous result in every dimension.

Theorem 1.4.4 (Beck). For any d ≥ 1, there is a constant cd > 0 depending only on d such that the
following holds. Let v1 , . . . , vn be vectors in Rd with ∥vi ∥ ≤ 1 for each 1 ≤ i ≤ n. If ξ1 , . . . , ξn are
independent Rademacher random variables, then

Narayanan, and Spiro [69] rediscovered this result for d = 2 with an alternative proof. While they noted
                  √
that the bound of d on the radius of the ball in Theorem 1.4.4 is optimal, this is not the end of the
                                                                                         √
story for Conjecture 1.4.3. Indeed, when d = 2, the example that showed that a radius of 2 is required
only works when n is even. Encouraged by the possibility that this is the only obstruction that prevents
concentration inside the unit disk, they conjectured [69, Conjecture 4.1] that the original conjecture of
Erdős holds when n is odd. Indeed, they had already noted in [69] that their pairing technique could
                                                                                          √
be used to show P(∥ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn ∥ ≤ r) ≥ Ω(1/n) for some r slightly smaller than 2, but that
new ideas would be required to get close to 1. In our first result, we provide an approximate version of
Conjecture 5.2.1, showing that for any r arbitrarily close to 1, a lower bound of order 1/n still holds.

    Theorem 1.4.7, together with Theorems 1.4.5 and 1.4.6, showcases a surprising change of behaviour
that occurs when considering the radius to be exactly 1. This double-jump phase transition, reminiscent
to the one that occurs with the size of the largest component of the Erdős-Rényi random graph [54],
illustrating the richness of phenomena exhibited by the reverse Littlewood–Offord problem.
    We also consider generalisations of these results to higher dimensions. In short, very little is known,
though we collect together what results we can. In particular, we pose the following question, which,

1.4.3       Vector balancing

                                                   i=1


In particular, we may take ε = 2−100 d−80 .

        We remark that, for n ≡ d (mod 2), there are choices of v1 , . . . , vn for which P(ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn ≤
√
    d − ε) = 0 for any ε > 0. Indeed, let e1 , . . . , ed be an orthonormal basis for Rd , and let v1 , . . . , vn
consist of an odd number of copies of each ei (whence the parity condition on n). We may thus see that
Theorem 1.4.11 demonstrates that the parity of n is significant in any number of dimensions.
   The value of ε given in Theorem 1.4.11 is surely far from optimal. Indeed, it is known that the upper
        √
bound of d − 1 from Question 1.4.8 holds in two dimensions [69]. However, even proving this for the
case of four vectors in three dimensions seems to be non-trivial.1



1.5        Generalisations of the bunkbed conjecture

We now move on to the final two chapters of this thesis, which concern percolation on graphs.
        In 1985, Kasteleyn posed the bunkbed conjecture, which then circulated as an intriguing problem in
percolation theory (see [84, Remark 5]). This problem concerns percolation on the product graph G□K2 ,
which consists of two copies G0 and G1 of G with edges between copies x(0) and x(1) of x ∈ V (G)
in G0 and G1 (respectively) also being present. The conjecture states that, for vertices u, v ∈ V (G),
percolation from u(0) to v (0) is at least as likely as percolation from u(0) to v (1) .
        Interest in the problem has waxed and waned over time, but, especially in recent years, the conjecture
has attracted significant attention. Indeed, the work presented in Chapter 6 has since been built on
to produce a counterexample to the bunkbed conjecture; see Section 1.5.1 and [63]. A feature of the
problem which has made it particularly appealing is its apparent simplicity and how, as has been noted
by several authors, it appeared to be intuitively clear.
    1
    Though, as this problem can be seen as a problem of 12 real variables, it can be checked (and has been checked) to be true
by a suitable computer search.
22                                                                                                   Introduction


     We now provide some definitions and state our main results before returning to the history of the
problem. While most work on the topic is concerned with simple graphs, we work in a more general
setting, and thus define the bunkbed graph in several cases, as follows.

Definition 1.5.1 (Bunkbed graph). For Q a graph, digraph, or hypergraph, we can define the bunkbed
structure Q̃ as follows. Take two copies, Q0 and Q1 , of Q, labelling the copy of vertex v and edge e in
Qi as v (i) and e(i) respectively. We will call such edges e(i) horizontal edges. E(Q̃) then consists of
E(Q0 ) ∪ E(Q1 ) along with an edge between v (0) and v (1) for each v ∈ V (Q), which will be called a
vertical edge. In the case of directed graphs, this vertical edge will be bidirectional. Q0 will be called
the lower bunk and Q1 the upper bunk of Q̃.

     We will be concerned in this chapter with percolation on the bunkbed graph under various uniform
models of percolation. We will consider two types of model: site percolation and bond percolation.
In both cases, we will ultimately take a family E(Q̃) of subsets of E(Q̃), which we will sample from
uniformly at random to produce some set X ⊆ E(Q̃) of edges which will be retained. We now define
these concepts formally.

Definition 1.5.2 (Percolation model). If Q is a graph, digraph, or hypergraph, then a percolation model is
a family E(Q̃) is a family of subsets of E(Q̃). We perform percolation by selecting an element X ∈ E(Q̃)
uniformly at random, and then retain precisely those edges in X. For two vertices x, y ∈ V (Q̃), we write

                                         E(Q̃)                       E(Q̃)
                                  P(x −−−→ y)         or       P(x ←−→ y)


for the probability that there is a directed path of retained edges from x to y in the former, or a bidirec-
tional path in the latter case, in Q̃ under model E(Q̃). When Q is a hypergraph, a path in Q is a sequence
of vertices in which consecutive vertices are both members of some hyperedge in Q.

     We can then use this definition to define site percolation as a special case of bond percolation. We
will only consider site percolation on graphs, so for simplicity we only define this case.

Definition 1.5.3 (Site percolation model). If G is a graph, then a percolation model E(G̃) is said to be a
site percolation model if, for every X ∈ E(G̃) there is some set U ⊆ V (G̃), which we shall refer to as
the set of open vertices, such that X consists of precisely those edges with both ends in U .

     We consider site percolation as a special case of our general (bond) percolation model for conve-
nience; it will be useful for us to still have all vertices present, even if they are in no edges.
     We remark that in much other work on the topic, percolation is phrased in terms of sampling edges
independently at random with some probability p. Our sampling as above is equivalent to this in the
case that E(Q̃) is the powerset P(E(Q̃)), and p = 1/2. This restriction to p = 1/2 turns out to not be
important, as will be discussed later.
     We now provide a very general statement of the bunkbed conjecture in terms of a general model,
1.5. Generalisations of the bunkbed conjecture                                                           23


before we proceed to consider several special cases.

Definition 1.5.4 (Bunkbed conjecture for a general model E). Let Q be a graph, hypergraph, or digraph,
E(Q̃) be a model of percolation on Q̃ as defined above. The bunkbed conjecture for model E states that
for any Q, and any vertices u, v ∈ V (Q), we have

                                        E(Q̃)                  E(Q̃)
                                P(u(0) −−−→ v (0) ) ≥ P(u(0) −−−→ v (1) ).


Let BBC(E(Q)) denote the above statement.

    There are several different statements which are referred to as ‘the bunkbed conjecture’, and they
are used somewhat interchangeably in the literature; Rudzinski and Smyth [113] detail a few of these
statements. One such form of the conjecture, which fits the notation we have described above, concerns
the following model of percolation.

Model 1.5.5 (E0 (G, T ), the standard bunkbed model). For an undirected graph G and a fixed set T ⊆
V (G), a set X ⊆ E(G̃) of horizontal edges is chosen uniformly at random, and retained along with
precisely those vertical edges v (0) v (1) such that v ∈ T . Equivalently, each horizontal edge of G̃ is
retained independently at random with probability 0.5.

    In this setting, BBC(E0 (G, T )) is equivalent to many statements referred to as ‘the bunkbed conjec-
ture’. When stated, the conjecture is often given in a slightly more general form, retaining each horizontal
edge of G̃ with probability p for some fixed p ∈ (0, 1), as in for example the work of Häggström [66, 67].
However, as proved by Rudzinski and Smyth [113, Corollary 1.7], the case for any specific probability
p implies the general case, and so the truth of BBC(E0 (G, T )) would suffice to prove the more general
conjecture.
    The conjecture BBC(E(G)) is false for general percolation models E; this is unsurprising as our
definition has no requirement of symmetry, and so permits many pathological models. However, we will
show that BBC(E(G)) is also false for several highly symmetric models E, some of which have been
conjectured to be true by various authors. This is particularly surprising as much of the same intuition
which tells us that BBC(E0 (G, T )) should be true also applies to these other—false—cases.
    The conjecture has attracted significant attention over the last few years, being resolved in progres-
sively more general special cases. One initial sequence of results by de Buyer and then van Hintum and
Lammers [39, 40, 118] resolved the case of the complete graph. This was then further generalised by
Richthammer [112] to a broader class of highly symmetric graphs. Linusson [95, 96] took a different
route, and both considered generalisations of the conjecture and related the conjecture to a problem con-
cerning randomly directed graphs. This work was then built upon by Hutchcroft, Nizić-Nikolac, and
Kent [83], who resolved the conjecture in the case of general G and probability p ↑ 1. A different angle
on the p ↑ 1 limit was later considered by the author [73], proving the conjecture holds in this case by
24                                                                                              Introduction


working with graph cuts.
     We now turn our attention to more general models; it is natural in an attempt to prove the bunkbed
conjecture to try to generalise the statement of the problem, in the hope that this may be conducive to an
inductive proof. Indeed, upon attacking the conjecture BBC(E0 (G, T )), a generalisation to hypergraphs
soon arises as a tempting possibility. Likewise, directed graphs allow for the possibility of attaching
‘gadgets’ to a graph to easily generalise the conjecture further, and on generalising to site percolation,
one no longer needs to consider paths which return to an already-visited pair of vertices in the bunkbed
graph. However, our main result is the following, which says that all of these generalisations are in fact
false.

Theorem 1.5.6. The generalised bunkbed conjecture is false for site percolation, hypergraphs, and di-
graphs. More precisely, there are counterexamples to the statement BBC(E(Q)) for each of the models
S1 (G), S2 (G, T ), H4 (H, T ), H5 (H), D6 (D, T, F ), and D7 (D) to be discussed in Sections 6.3 and 6.4.


1.5.1    Subsequent disproof of the bunkbed conjecture

Before proceeding, we note that a few months after the initial release of this chapter as a preprint on arXiv,
the work presented here was used by Gladkov, Pak and Zimin [63] to produce a counterexample to the
bunkbed conjecture. In particular, they started from the hypergraph construction shown in Figure 6.5,
and, by replacing each hyperedge with a suitably constructed graph, were able to produce a graph on
which the bunkbed conjecture fails.
     While it is impossible to directly simulate site percolation with bond percolation—or indeed bond
percolation on a hypergraph by bond percolation on a simple graph (as was shown by Gladkov and
Zimin [62])—they were able to simulate a modified hypergraph percolation model due to Wierman and
Ziff [120]. By then correctly tuning the parameters of this model of percolation, a counterexample to the
conjecture was found.
     The relation of [63] to the work presented in Chapter 6 is discussed further in Section 6.6.



1.6      Long cycles in percolated vertex expanders

For a graph G and a probability p ∈ [0, 1], the percolated graph Gp is the subgraph of G formed by
retaining each edge of G independently with probability p. The most general form of the question we
consider here is: what are the threshold values of p at which various structures emerge in the graph Gp ?
And how do these threshold values depend on the structural properties of the host graph G? When the
underlying graph G is the complete graph Kn , this model of percolation recovers the well-known Erdős–
Rényi random graph G(n, p), and in this situation questions of phase transitions have been subject to a
long line of research, beginning with the seminal 1959 paper of Erdős and Rényi [51]. However, while
1.6. Long cycles in percolated vertex expanders                                                                             25


the structure of the Erdős–Rényi random graph is generally well-understood, this is not the case for
more general percolated graphs. Indeed, structure which is not present in G cannot persist in Gp , and
so assumptions on G are required as well. For example, Gp cannot have large connected components if
these are not present in G.
      A natural condition that may be placed on G is that of a minimum degree constraint. Indeed, Kriv-
elevich and Sudakov [92] showed in 2013 by analysis of a depth-first search (DFS) algorithm that if G
has minimum degree at least d, then Gp contains a path of length Ω(d) with high probability provided
that p = (1 + ε)/d. This also provided a new, simple proof of a classic result of Ajtai, Komlós, and
Szemerédi [11] concerning long paths in G(n, p). Note that this is the best value of p that one could
hope for: if G has maximum degree d and p < (1 − ε)/d, then the size of the largest component in
Gp is, with high probability, logarithmic in |G| (see for example Theorem 2 of [44] and the references
therein). Krivelevich and Sudakov [92] moreover noted many applications of their analysis of a DFS to
objects such as digraphs, pseudorandom graphs, and positional games. A more subtle analysis allowed
Krivelevich and Samotij [91] to find cycles of length Ω(d) under the same constraints.
      Expansion conditions at a larger scale were considered by Diskin, Erde, Kang, and Krivelevich [43],
who showed that one may with high probability find components of size k/2 in Gp with p = (1 + ε)/d
provided that every set of size k has edge-boundary (in G) of size at least dk. They moreover noted
that this condition does not suffice to find a long path, as, for example, the graph Kd,d10 has good edge-
expansion for sets of size up to d9 but no path of length longer than 2d. A way around this issue was
suggested by Collares, Diskin, Erde, and Krivelevich [38], who considered vertex-expansion rather than
edge-expansion. Indeed, they proved the following result.

Theorem 1.6.1 ([38], Theorem 2). Let k, d ∈ N, and let G be a graph on at least k vertices such that
every S ⊆ V (G) with |S| = k satisfies |N (S)| ≥ kd. Let ε > 0 be a sufficiently small constant
and let p = (1 + ε)/d. Then G contains a path of length at least ε2 kd/10 with probability at least
1 − exp(−Ωε (kd)).

      The asymptotic notation Ωε above indicates that the implicit constants depend only on ε. We use
N (S) to denote the set of points in V \ S which are adjacent to some element of the set S. Moreover,
it may be noted from the proof of Theorem 1.6.1 that the precise conditions on the value of ε are that
ε ∈ (Cd−1 , 0.1) for an absolute constant2
      The notion of vertex-expansion in Theorem 1.6.1 differs a little from the standard form of vertex-
expansion. Indeed, the standard definition is that a (k, a)-vertex-expander is a graph wherein every
S ⊆ V (G) with |S| ≤ k has N (S) ≥ a|S|. For ease of discussion, and following the notation of [90],
we make the following definition.

  2
      The lower bound on ε in terms of d and the constant C was not made explicit in [38], but follows easily from the proof.
26                                                                                            Introduction


Definition 1.6.2. A graph G is an ({k}, d)-vertex-expander if every S ⊆ V (G) with |S| = k has
|N (S)| ≥ dk.

     For further details on expander graphs and the connection between (k, a)-vertex-expander graphs
and ({k}, d)-vertex-expander graphs, we refer the interested reader to the survey of Krivelevich [90].
     It was conjectured in [38] that, under the assumptions in Theorem 1.6.1, there should also be a cycle
of length Ωε (kd). In G(n, p), inferring the existence of a long cycle from a long path can be done with an
entirely standard sprinkling argument. However, in our setting this may be more difficult as, for example,
the girth of G could be far larger than dk. Indeed, our main result is the following, which resolves this
conjecture.

Theorem 1.6.3. Fix d > 1 and ε ≥ ε0 (d) > 0 such that ε ≤ 1/8, let k ∈ N satisfy k ≤ ε−2 d, and let
the graph G be an ({k}, d)-vertex-expander. Then, if p = (1 + ε)/d, then Gp contains a cycle of length
at least Ωε (kd) with probability at least 1 − exp(−Ωε (k/d)).

     As in Theorem 1.6.1, the asymptotic notation Ωε simply hides implicit constants which depend only
on ε. We will in fact prove a refined version of this result, Theorem 7.2.1, which contains more precise
information about the dependencies and constants involved. A thorough outline of our proof will be
provided in Section 7.1.1, but a brief summary is that we—like much of the previous work in this area—
make use of a modified DFS algorithm to explore the graph. This algorithm allows us to find a long path
in Gp , and, moreover, use the vertex-expansion condition to find many edges of G which connect vertices
far apart on this path. Then, applying the technique of sprinkling, we prove that, with high probability,
some of these edges are present in the percolated graph, producing a long cycle, as required. We will
