conclude the chapter with some open problems and suggestions for directions of future work.
Chapter 2

The Aharoni–Korman conjecture

The work presented in this chapter is drawn from a paper [72] of which I am the sole author.


2.1    Introduction

In this chapter, we consider the Aharoni–Korman conjecture, which we now recall.

Conjecture 1.1.1 ([9], Conjecture 4.1). If a poset P contains no infinite antichain then, for every positive
integer k, there exist k chains C1 , . . . , Ck and a partition of P into disjoint antichains (Ai : i ∈ I) such
that each Ai meets min(|Ai |, k) chains Cj .

    In particular, we focus on the case of k = 1. Recalling the definition of a spine, Definition 1.1.12,
and the abbreviation that an FAC poset is one satisfying the finite antichain condition, i.e. with no infinite
antichain, Conjecture 1.1.1 states that any FAC poset has a spine. Our first main result is the following.

Theorem 1.1.2. There is a countable poset P for which Conjecture 1.1.1 is false.

    The construction of the counterexample posited by Theorem 1.1.2 is presented in Section 2.5.2.
    To state our second main result, we must first recall the following key definition.

Definition 1.1.13. We say that a poset P is vacillating if there is no contiguous chain C in P for which
either C or its reverse C ∗ can be written as i∈ω Ci , where each Ci is infinite and co-wellfounded.
                                             L

    We remark here that the name “vacillating” comes from the observation that a vacillating poset must
alternate between wellfounded and co-wellfounded sections, and cannot contain any denser structures
(e.g. the rationals). We may now recall our second main result as the following.

Theorem 1.1.14. If P is a countable vacillating FAC poset, then P contains a spine.

    However, before dealing with Conjecture 1.1.1 directly, we consider strongly maximal chains, which
we may recall are defined as follows.
28                                                                         The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


Definition 1.1.8. A chain C of a poset P is a maximal chain if no element of P \ C is comparable to
every element of C. C is a strongly maximal chain, or SMC for short, if for every chain D ⊆ P , we have
that |C \ D| ≥ |D \ C|.

     We record the following observation, the proof of which is simple, and not given here.

Observation 2.1.1. If C is a spine of a poset P , then C is also a strongly maximal chain in P .

     The above observation allows us to consider the existence of strongly maximal chains as a reso-
lution of a weaker version of the Aharoni–Korman conjecture. Our work on SMCs both serves as an
introduction to the ideas used in the proof of Theorem 1.1.14 and provides a positive result without the
requirement that P is vacillating. Indeed, we prove Theorem 1.1.11, which we now restate.

Theorem 1.1.11. Any countable FAC poset has a strongly maximal chain.

     We now work through some basic definitions and results in Section 2.1.1, and then in Section 2.1.2
we give an outline of the rest of the chapter.


2.1.1    Definitions and basic results

We now recall some fundamental facts and definitions that we will make use of throughout the rest of this
chapter. We are concerned here with posets P containing no infinite antichain, although their width—the
supremum of the sizes of antichains in P —may be infinite.
     For any order X, we will write X ∗ to denote the reverse ordering. If chains C, D ⊆ P have C ⊆ D,
then we will say that C is cofinal in D if for all y ∈ D there is x ∈ C such that y ≤ x. We will say that
C is a final segment of D if C is contiguous in D and if, whenever x, y ∈ D have x ≤ y and x ∈ C, then
we have y ∈ C. The terms coinitial and initial segment are then the dual statements, with the ordering
reversed. If X, Y ⊆ P are arbitrary subsets of P , then by X < Y , we mean that for all x ∈ X and
y ∈ Y , we have x < y.
     We will denote the up-set of x—the set of all elements above x—as (≥ x) := {y ∈ P : y ≥ x}; the
down-set (≤ x), along with sets (> x) and (< x) are defined similarly. We write x ∥ y to mean that x
and y are comparable, and x ⊥ y to mean that x and y are incomparable, and the sets (∥ x) and (⊥ x)
are the subsets of P of all elements comparable and incomparable to x respectively.
     One final piece of notation that we will use concerns the limit of a sequence of sets: if X1 , X2 , . . .
are subsets of P , then

                                    ∞ \
                                    [ ∞
                       lim Xn :=              Xn = {y ∈ P : ∃N ∀n ≥ N y ∈ Xn }.
                      n→∞
                                    m=1 n=m


In words, this limit is the set of those points that are in every Xn for sufficiently large n.
     We now discuss a property of posets to which we will give particular attention.
2.1. Introduction                                                                                           29


Definition 2.1.2. A poset P is said to be scattered if it does not contain a poset order-isomorphic Q as a
sub-order.

    Scattered posets have been studied extensively. A useful property of scattered posets is that we can
find covers.

Definition 2.1.3. For elements x, y ∈ P , we say that x covers y, and write x ⋗ y, if x > y and there is
no z ∈ P with x > z > y.

Fact 2.1.4. If P is scattered, then for any x, y ∈ P with x < y, there are u, v ∈ P with x ≤ u < v ≤ y
such that v ⋗ u.

    Another fact that we will use extensively is the following.

Fact 2.1.5. If P is an infinite FAC poset, then P has an infinite chain.

    This follows easily from basic infinite Ramsey theory. In particular, if x1 , x2 , . . . is an infinite
sequence in an FAC poset P , then we can then find a sequence n1 < n2 < · · · such that either xn1 ≥
xn2 ≥ · · · or xn1 ≤ xn2 ≤ · · · .
    Throughout, we will write ω for the order type of the naturals and the first infinite ordinal, ζ for the
order type of the integers, and η for the order type of the rationals.
    Given two posets P and Q with orders ≤P and ≤Q respectively, the Cartesian product poset P × Q
is is the poset on the Cartesian product set P × Q, with order ≤ given by (p, q) ≤ (p′ , q ′ ) if and only if
p ≤P p′ and q ≤Q q ′ . The lexicographic product P ×lex Q is also a poset on the Cartesian product set
P × Q, except now the ordering ≤lex is given by (p, q) ≤lex (p′ , q ′ ) if and only if p <P p′ , or p = p′ and
q ≤Q q ′ . P ×lex Q can also be written as the linear sum p∈P Q.
                                                         L

    For an arbitrary poset P , a subset X ⊆ P is said to be convex, or an interval of P if for all x, y ∈ X,
if z ∈ P has x < z < y, then z ∈ X. We will use the notation I(P ) to represent the set of all intervals
of P . There are several particular types of intervals that we will consider here, for elements x, y ∈ P
with x < y. The first is the open interval


                                     (x, y) := {z ∈ P : x < z < y},                                   (2.1.1)


and the second is the closed interval


                                     [x, y] := {z ∈ P : x ≤ z ≤ y}.                                   (2.1.2)


We can also take the closed interval of a set without endpoints, resulting in the convex hull of the set:


                            Conv(X) := {x ∈ P : (∃y, z ∈ X) y ≤ x ≤ z}.                               (2.1.3)
30                                                                        The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


The third is the non-standard interval which we refer to as the wide interval, defined as follows.


           ⟨x, y⟩ := {z ∈ P : ∀w ∈ P (w > y =⇒ w > z) ∧ (w < x =⇒ w < z)}.                           (2.1.4)


Likewise, we define the wide interval ⟨X⟩ for an arbitrary subset X ⊆ P to be


           ⟨X⟩ := {z ∈ P : ∀w ∈ P (w > X =⇒ w > z) ∧ (w < X =⇒ w < z)}.                              (2.1.5)


For example, if P is the diamond poset on four elements {a, b, c, d} with a < b < d and a < c < d, then
the wide interval ⟨{b}⟩ is equal to {b, c}. Note that the containments (x, y) ⊊ [x, y] ⊆ ⟨x, y⟩ always
hold. We will say that an interval I of P is proper if there are x, y ∈ P with x < I < y.


2.1.2    Chapter outline

Before we prove any of our main results, we develop the tools which we use to prove them. These tools
consist primarily of a particular form of poset completion—that is, an extension of a poset P to a larger
poset so that certain subsets of P are guaranteed to have infima and suprema—which we introduce in
Section 2.2. The definitions and preliminary results that we require to use this structure are split into
several subsections, and an more thorough outline of the contained contained therein is given at the start
of the section.
     We then proceed to consider strongly maximal chains in Section 2.3, working first in the case of scat-
tered posets, and then generalising to all countable posets. In Section 2.4 we then prove Theorem 1.1.14,
which again takes place over several stages, in which we find successively stronger structures within the
poset. As before, a more thorough outline of this proof is given at the start of the section.
     In Section 2.5, we then provide several examples to illuminate some of the definitions and results
given throughout the chapter. In particular, the counterexample required by Theorem 1.1.2 is given in
Section 2.5.2. In Section 2.6, we state several open problems and possible directions for future research.
Finally, to aid the reader, we include in Section 2.7 a summary of the notation and terminology used in
the chapter.



2.2     Poset extensions and chain completeness

In this section, we introduce the tools and machinery that we will be using to prove our main results. The
key object introduced here, described in Section 2.2.2, is H(P ), which can be thought of as similar to a
chain completion of P ; while H(P ) is not in fact a chain complete poset, if C ⊆ P is a chain then H(C)
is complete, and naturally embeds into H(P ). We will in particular be concerned with chains which are
contiguous with no maximal element (or, similarly, with no minimal element), which we will refer to
2.2. Poset extensions and chain completeness                                                            31


as increasing chains (or decreasing chains respectively). We now provide an overview of this section,
which is somewhat technical, before proceeding to the technicalities themselves.
   We first lay some groundwork in Section 2.2.1, defining an equivalence relation between increasing
chains. Two chains will be considered equivalent if, in essence, elements of one chain are incomparable
with only finitely many elements of the other, and vice-versa. With this equivalence relation in hand,
in Section 2.2.2 we will then consider the quotient of the set of increasing chains by this equivalence
relation; we will use this set to augment P and thus form our chain extension H(P ). We also demonstrate
how H(P ) has a natural order (the domination order) which agrees with the order on P .
   In Section 2.2.3, we define a new partial order ⊵ on H(P ), in particular on the nonprincipal elements
(those in N (P ) := H(P ) \ P ). This order, which we call reduction, is similar to the notion of one
chain being cofinal in the other, but has some further complexities which allows us to conclude that,
under certain conditions (which will be discussed further in Section 2.4), the partial order ⊵ is in fact a
wellfounded partial order.
   We then introduce in Section 2.2.4 two notions of bicomparability between nonprincipal elements of
H(P ), and prove some lemmas concerning these properties. In particular, this section gives a first hint
as to why the condition of a poset being vacillating is relevant to the problems at hand.
   Finally, in Section 2.2.5, we state and prove two important lemmas concerning chains and antichains
of nonprincipal elements of H(P ). In particular, we show that chains of elements of N (P ) under the
domination order can be found among the limit points of a chain of principal elements of P , and also that
we cannot have an infinite set of nonprincipal elements in N (P ) which form a chain under reduction but
an antichain under domination.


2.2.1   Chain equivalence

When considering an increasing chain C in a poset P , we will in general only be interested in proper-
ties which hold when passing to an arbitrary final segment of C; any finite region of the chain which
is in some sense “unusual” can be skipped over. For this reason, we first define a very strong sense
of similarity—mutually finite incomparability—between two chains, and then define our equivalence
between chains C and D to hold when there are final segments of these chains with mutually finite
incomparability.

Definition 2.2.1. Chains C and D in a poset P are said to have mutually finite incomparability if they
are both maximal chains in the poset Conv(C ∪ D) ⊆ P , and, moreover,


             ∀y ∈ D |{x ∈ C : x ⊥ y}| < ℵ0         and    ∀x ∈ C |{y ∈ D : x ⊥ y}| < ℵ0 .


   Note in particular that if C and D have mutually finite incomparability and x ∈ C \ D, then there
is some y ∈ D which is incomparable to x, as otherwise D would not be a maximal chain in the poset
32                                                                         The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


Conv(C ∪ D). Definition 2.2.1 intuitively says that C and D can only deviate from each other on
small scales; the differences are local. As indicated above, we can now use the notion of mutually finite
incomparability to define our notion of equivalence of chains.

Definition 2.2.2. Chains C and D in a poset P are said to be equivalent as increasing chains, and we
write C ≃+ D, if either C and D have maximal elements which are equal, or neither C nor D has a
maximal element and there are final segments C ′ ⊆ C and D′ ⊆ D with mutually finite incomparability.
Similarly, C and D are equivalent as decreasing chains, written C ≃− D, if they have either minimal
elements which are equal or initial segments with mutually finite incomparability.

     Given the name ‘equivalent’, it should come as no surprise that ≃+ and ≃− are equivalence relations
on the set of chains of P . To prove this fact, we first need to establish some properties of chains with
mutually finite incomparability. We will show that if C and D have mutually finite incomparability, then
the ‘large scale’ structures of C and D are identical. To this end, we define the following equivalence
relation, which allows us to consider the structure of a chain.

Definition 2.2.3. Given a chain C in a poset P , define the symmetric relation ∼C on C by setting x ∼C y
if and only if the interval (x, y) ∩ C is finite.

     It is immediate from definition that, for any chain C, the relation ∼C is in fact an equivalence relation
on elements of C, and that each equivalence class of C is either finite, or isomorphic to one of ω, ω ∗ and
ζ. The following lemma shows that mutually finite incomparability ‘respects ∼C -equivalence classes’.
     For x ∈ C, we denote the ∼C -equivalence class of x by [x]C

Lemma 2.2.4. If C and D have mutually finite incomparability, then there is a bijection f : C / ∼C →
D / ∼D such that, for any x ∈ C and y ∈ D with x ⊥ y or x = y, f sends [x]C to [y]D . Moreover,
a ∼C -equivalence class X ∈ C / ∼C has a maximal element if and only if the ∼D -equivalence class
f (X) has a maximal element, and similarly for minimal elements.

Proof. Firstly, note that, for all x ∈ C, either x ∈ D or there is some y ∈ D with x ⊥ y, as otherwise
D ∪ {x} would be a chain in Conv(C ∪ D) and strictly larger than D, contradicting the definition of
mutually finite incomparability. Note further that, if x ∈ C and y, z ∈ D with x ⊥ y and x ⊥ z, then we
have y ∼D z. Define the function g as follows.
                                    
                                    x    if x ∈ D,
                                    
                          g(x) :=
                                    y    if x ∈
                                               / D and y = min{z ∈ D : x ⊥ z}.
                                    


Note that {z ∈ D : x ⊥ z} is a finite subset (in fact, an interval) of the chain D, so does indeed have a
minimum. We may now define f ([x]C ) := [g(x)]D ; we claim that this is well-defined. We must show
that if x ∼C y, then g(x) ∼D g(y).
2.2. Poset extensions and chain completeness                                                             33


    Indeed, assume for contradiction that the interval I := (x, y) ∩ C is finite, but J := (g(x), g(y)) ∩ D
is infinite. Every element of the infinite set J \ I is incomparable to a finite non-zero number of elements
of C, and, moreover, of I. Therefore some element of I is incomparable with infinitely many elements
of J, contradicting the mutually finite incomparability of C and D. Thus f is a well-defined function.
    We now show that f is injective and surjective. Injectivity follows from an argument similar to that
one showing that f is well-defined. For surjectivity, take some y ∈ D; we show that f hits [y]D . Indeed,
either y ∈ C, in which case f ([y]C ) = [y]D , or there is some x ∈ C such that x ⊥ y, in which case
g(x) ∼D y, and so f ([x]C ) = [y]D . Thus f is a well-defined bijection satisfying the conditions of the
lemma.
    It therefore remains to prove the final part of the lemma. By symmetry, it suffices to take some ∼C -
equivalence class X with a maximal element x, and show that Y := f (X) also has a maximal element.
Assume for contradiction that Y does not have a maximal element. The point x is incomparable with only
finitely many points of Y , and so there is some y ∈ Y with y > x. But then y is totally comparable to
X, and we know that y is also totally comparable to every other ∼C -equivalence class. This contradicts
the maximality of C in Conv(C ∪ D), and so proves the lemma.

    With Lemma 2.2.4 in hand, it is now straightforward to prove that ≃+ and ≃− are equivalence
relations.

Lemma 2.2.5. The relations ≃+ and ≃− are equivalence relations.

Proof. By symmetry, it suffices to consider ≃+ . Reflexivity and symmetry are clear, so it suffices to
prove transitivity. Let C, D, and E be chains in P , each with no maximal element, such that C ≃+ D
and D ≃+ E (the case in which they have maximal elements is trivial). We thus know that there are final
segments C ′ ⊆ C, D′ , D′′ ⊆ D, and E ′′ ⊆ E such that C ′ and D′ have mutually finite incomparability,
as do D′′ and E ′′ . We may therefore apply Lemma 2.2.4 find bijections


                        f : C ′ / ∼C ′ → D′ / ∼D′     and    g : D′′ / ∼D′′ → E ′′ / ∼E ′′


satisfying the conditions of Lemma 2.2.4. We may also assume due to symmetry that D′ ⊆ D′′ , and thus
consider the function


                                      h := g ◦ f : C ′ / ∼C ′ → E ′ / ∼E ′ ,


where E ′ ⊆ E ′′ is chosen so that h is also a bijection. Note that E ′ is a final segment of E ′′ .
    It suffices to prove that if X ∈ C ′ / ∼C ′ and Y ∈ E ′ / ∼E ′ have Y = h(X), then X and Y have
mutually finite incomparability. If X and Y are both finite, then this is immediate, so we may assume
that X and Y are both infinite, and thus we know from Lemma 2.2.4 that X and Y are order-isomorphic.
34                                                                       The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


     The lemma thus reduces to proving that, if X and Y as above each have no maximal element and
x ∈ X, then there is y ∈ Y with y > x. Indeed, take z ∈ f (X) minimal so that z > x, which exists as
(⊥ x) ∩ D is finite and f (X) has no maximum element. Then take y ∈ Y minimal so that y > z, which
exists for the same reasons as before. We find that y > x, as required, thus proving the lemma.

     Now that we know that these relations are in fact equivalence relations, we are ready to define our
chain completion H(P ).


2.2.2    Chain extension and the domination order

The intuition behind the poset H(P ) is that it is simply the set of chains of P quotiented by the relation
of equivalence as defined in Section 2.2.1. However, this intuition runs into a problem in that we have
not one, but two equivalence relations; ≃+ and ≃− .
     Once we have defined the two quotient sets, we will have to carefully glue the two resulting structures
together along the ‘principal’ elements, and define an order on the whole structure taking into account
both the increasing and decreasing chains.

Definition 2.2.6. Given a poset P , let F (P ) be the set of all contiguous chains of P . We then define the
sets of increasing and decreasing chains as


                         G+ (P ) := F (P )/ ≃+       and   G− (P ) := F (P )/ ≃−


respectively. Elements of G+ (P ) and G− (P ) are thus equivalence classes of contiguous chains of P .

     Note that if an equivalence class X ∈ G+ (P ) contains a chain C with a maximal element x, then it is
immediate from Definition 2.2.2 that all chains in X have x as a maximal element. In particular, the chain
{x} is an element of the equivalence class X. For this reason, we refer to such elements of G+ (P ) (and
the similar elements of G− (P )) as principal elements, and the other elements as nonprincipal elements.
     There is now a natural equivalence between principal elements of G+ (P ) and principal elements of
G− (P ); for X ∈ G+ (P ) and Y ∈ G− (P ), write X ≃prin Y if there is some x ∈ P such that {x} ∈ X
and {x} ∈ Y .

Definition 2.2.7. Let G(P ) := G− (P ) ∪ G+ (P ), and then define the set


                                          H(P ) := G(P )/ ≃prin .


Furthermore, define N (P ) to be the set of nonprincipal elements of H(P ); those equivalence classes
of chains in G+ (P ) with no maximal element, or in G− (P ) with no minimal element. We will write
N+ (P ) and N− (P ) for the nonprincipal increasing and decreasing chains in N (P ) respectively, so
N (P ) = N+ (P ) ∪ N− (P ).
2.2. Poset extensions and chain completeness                                                               35


    While H(P ) is technically a set of equivalence classes of equivalence classes of chains, we will
abuse notation and identify the principal elements of H(P ) with the corresponding elements of P (they
are in natural bijection). Following this abuse of notation, we may write the following.


                                    H(P ) = P ∪ N+ (P ) ∪ N− (P ).                                   (2.2.1)


    We will furthermore generally refer to nonprincipal elements of H(P ) only by a representative chain.
In particular, we will write, for example, “C ∈ N+ (P )” to mean that C is a representative chain of an
equivalence class X ∈ N+ (P ) such that {X} ∈ H(P ). If we wish to emphasise that we are considering
an equivalence class rather than a chain, then we will use square brackets: C will be a chain and [C] an
equivalence class.
    Now that we have defined H(P ), our next task is to give it the structure of a poset. We will define an
ordering ≥ for all elements of H(P ), and then show that it agrees with the ordering on P on the principal
elements.

Definition 2.2.8. For C, D ∈ H(P ), we say that C dominates D, and write C > D, if there are nonempty
final/initial segments C ′ ⊆ C and D′ ⊆ D (final or initial depending on whether C and D are increasing
or decreasing chains respectively) satisfying ∀x ∈ C ′ ∀y ∈ D′ (x > y).
    By C ≥ D, we mean that C > D or C ≃ D (i.e. C ≃− D or C ≃+ D).

    Note first that it is clear that domination is indeed a partial order. Secondly, if C and D are both
principal, and equivalent to {x} and {y} respectively, then we can take {x} and {y} as their respective
final/initial segments, and see that the ordering in Definition 2.2.8 does agree with the order from P .
    Domination will be the standard order that we use on H(P ); if we refer to an ordering of elements
of H(P ) without further comment, the domination order is implied.
    Now that H(P ) has been given the structure of a poset, we can prove the following important lemma.

Lemma 2.2.9. If P is a scattered poset, then so too is H(P ).

Proof. Assume for contradiction that H(P ) contains a copy of the rationals. It is a standard result that if
the rationals are finitely coloured, then one colour class must itself contain a copy of the rationals. This
can be seen to follow from Fact 2.1.4 (if there was no monochrome copy of Q, one finds x1 ⋗ y1 in the
poset restricted to the first colour class, and then x2 ⋗ y2 with x1 > x2 > y2 > y1 in a second colour
class, and so on.)
    Note that, due to equality (2.2.1), we may assume without loss of generality that N+ (P ) contains a
copy of the rationals: let f : Q → N+ (P ) be an order-preserving injection. Now take X, Y ∈ N+ (P )
with X < Y . It is the immediate from the definition of the domination order that there is x ∈ P with
X < x < Y . Thus for any p, q ∈ Q with p < q, there is some xp,q ∈ P such that f (p) < xp,q < f (q). A
simple inductive construction then produces a copy of the dyadic rationals, and thus a copy of Q, inside
36                                                                        The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


P . This contradicts the fact that P is scattered, therefore proving that H(P ) is scattered.

     We now give one further definition concerning nonprincipal elements before we continue.

Definition 2.2.10. A nonprincipal increasing chain C ∈ N+ (P ) is an accumulation point of N (P ) if,
for any D ∈ N (P ) with D < C, there is some E ∈ N (P ) with D < E < C, and similarly (with orders
reversed) for elements of N− (P ). An element of N (P ) which is not an accumulation point is called an
isolated point.

     We now prove that, if C is a chain, then H(C) is complete. It is worth noting that, for a general poset
P , H(P ) need not be chain-complete; if C and D are non-equivalent chains in P with C cofinal in D
(e.g. C has order type ω but D has order type ω 2 ), then [C] and [D] are both minimal upper bounds for
C, but they are incomparable. It is easy to see, however, that for any chain C, [C] is an upper bound for
C, and there are no strictly smaller upper bounds for C. It is for this reason that we refer to H(P ) as a
chain extension, rather than a chain completion.

Lemma 2.2.11. For any poset P and chain C ⊆ P , the poset H(C) is complete. Moreover, if D ⊆ C is
a chain with no maximum in D, then sup(D) ∈ N+ (C).

Proof. It suffices to prove the second statement, as then by symmetry every sub-chain of C has an inf
and a sup. Given a chain D ⊆ C containing no maximal element, we claim that [D] ∈ N+ (C) is a least
upper bound for D.
     Indeed, given any element x ∈ D, there is a nonempty final segment of D which is entirely above
x, so [D] is certainly an upper bound for D. Take now an arbitrary chain E ∈ H(C) such that [E] is an
upper bound for D. If E is principal, then it is immediate that [E] > [D], so we may assume that E is
non-principal.
     If E ∈ N− (C), then for every x ∈ D, there is some Ex ≃− E with an initial segment entirely above
x. Thus E is entirely above x, and so E is entirely above D, i.e. [E] > [D], as required.
     Otherwise, E ∈ N+ (C). As [E] is an upper bound for D, for every x ∈ D there is a chain Ex ≃+ E
with a final segment entirely above x; thus a final segment of E is entirely above x. Therefore either a
final segment of E is entirely above D, and E > D in the domination order, or D is cofinal in E, and,
as they are both contained in the same chain, they are thus equivalent. In either case, we see that [D] is
indeed the least upper bound for D, completing the proof of the lemma.

     We now work towards defining our second partial order on H(P ), which will be one of our key tools
in eventually finding a spine in P .


2.2.3    The reduction order

Before giving the definition of reduction, we need to define a particular subset of H(P ), consisting of
those nonprincipal elements which are in some sense minimal. For example, the only infinite increasing
2.2. Poset extensions and chain completeness                                                               37


chains in a copy of ω are cofinal subsets. However, there are also more complicated possibilities. For
example, in the poset ω ×lex ω ∗ , consisting of ω-many copies of ω ∗ , the only infinite increasing chains
are also cofinal, and so, if we are concerned with contiguous chains, this poset is also in a sense minimal.
This leads to the following definition.

Definition 2.2.12. An element of N+ (P ) is said to be an atomic increasing chain if it does not admit an
order-preserving injection from the poset ω + 1. We write N+0 (P ) for the set of atomic increasing chains.
Similarly, a decreasing chain is atomic if it does not admit an order-preserving injection from the poset
(ω + 1)∗ , and we write N−0 (P ) for the set of such posets.

    Note that the notion of an atomic chain is well-defined in that, if chains C, D ⊆ P have C ≃+ D
and C contains no copy of ω + 1, then there is a final segment D′ of D for which contains no copy of
ω + 1. Indeed, this follows easily from Lemma 2.2.4.
    Now that we know that N+0 (P ) and N−0 (P ) are well-defined, we would like to prove that these
objects are non-empty for nontrivial P . To do this, we will perform an induction on scattered chains, by
appealing to the following classical result of Hausdorff [68].

Theorem 2.2.13 ([68]). Let B be the class of well-orderings and reverse well-orderings. The class of
scattered linear orderings is the least class which contains B, and is closed under lexicographic sums
with index set lying in B.

    We are now ready to prove that nontrivial scattered posets always contain atomic chains.

Lemma 2.2.14. If P is a scattered poset containing an infinite increasing sequence, then N+0 (P ) ̸= ∅.

Proof. Note first that a scattered poset P contains an infinite increasing sequence if and only if it contains
some chain with this property. It thus suffices to prove that N+0 (C) ̸= ∅ for any scattered chain C which
admits an embedding of ω.
    We induct on chains by means of Theorem 2.2.13; let B be as defined therein. For the base case,
we must prove the result for chains C which are wellfounded or co-wellfounded. If C is wellfounded
and infinite, then we can simply take the chain consisting of the bottom ω elements of C, which clearly
admits no embedding from ω + 1. If C is co-wellfounded, then by definition C does not admit an
embedding of ω, and so the condition holds vacuously.
   For the inductive step, we take P = i∈I Ci , where I ∈ B and each Ci either has N+0 (Ci ) ̸= ∅
                                        L

or does not admit an embedding of ω. If any Ci has N+0 (Ci ) ̸= ∅, then we can find these elements in
N+0 (P ), which is thus nonempty. We may therefore assume that no Ci admits an embedding of ω, i.e.
they are all co-wellfounded.
   If I is also co-wellfounded, then so too is P , and we are (vacuously) done. Thus assume that I is
wellfounded and infinite. We claim that X := j∈J Cj is an element of N+0 (P ), where J consists of the
                                              S

bottom ω elements of I. Indeed, this poset admits an embedding of ω: take one element from each Cj .
38                                                                       The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


Furthermore, this poset admits no embedding of ω + 1; if it did, then some Cj would have to admit an
embedding of ω, a contradiction. Thus, in all cases, either P is co-wellfounded or N+0 (P ) is nonempty,
as required.

      We are now ready to give the definition of our new ordering.

Definition 2.2.15. For chains C, D ⊆ P , we say that C reduces to D, or that D is a reduction of C, and
write C ⊵ D, if the following holds. If C ∈ N+0 (P ), then D ∈ N+ (P ) and C is cofinally above D, that
is,


                                         ∀y ∈ D ∃x ∈ C (x > y).


If C ∈ N−0 (P ), then D ∈ N− (P ) and C is coinitially below D, that is,


                                         ∀y ∈ D ∃x ∈ C (y > x).


Otherwise, there is a function f : N (C) → N (D) with all the following properties.

      • f is order-preserving,
      • f is injective,
      • f is continuous (i.e. commutes with sup and inf),
      • if C ∈ N+ (P ) then f has cofinal image in N (D), otherwise f has coinitial image in N (D), and
      • for all ε ∈ {−, +} and X ∈ Nε0 (C), we have f (X) ∈ Nε (D) and X ⊵ f (X).

We say that f witnesses the fact that C ⊵ D.

      The following remark highlights a potentially unintuitive point about Definition 2.2.15

Remark 2.2.16. Note that, for C, D ∈ N− (P ), if C ⊵ D, then for every element of D there is a smaller
element of C; in particular, if C < D, then C ⊵ D (as opposed to the case of C, D ∈ N+ (P ), where
C > D implies C ⊵ D). This convention on the ordering allows for more natural statements, and
statements which still hold after reversing the order of P . An example of such a statement is “if C ⊵ D
and D ̸⊵ C, then there is an x ∈ C incomparable to infinitely many elements of D.”

      We also take note of the following observation, which follows immediately from Definition 2.2.15.

Observation 2.2.17. If C ⊵ D, and f : N (C) → N (D) witnesses this fact, then for every X ∈ N (C),
we have that X ⊵ f (X).

      We will consider the reduction order in more detail in Section 2.4.2, and it will be proved therein
that, under certain conditions, ⊵ is a wellfounded partial order. Here, we prove only that reduction is a
preorder.

Lemma 2.2.18. The order ⊵ is transitive.
2.2. Poset extensions and chain completeness                                                                  39


Proof. Take chains C, D, and E in the poset P such that C ⊵ D and D ⊵ E. We must show that C ⊵ E.
    If C is not atomic, then there are functions f : N (C) → N (D) and g : N (D) → N (E) witnessing
that C ⊵ D and D ⊵ E respectively. It is then immediate that the function g ◦ f : N (C) → N (E)
is order-preserving, injective, continuous, and has cofinal/coinitial (as appropriate) image in N (E). It
remains to prove that if X ∈ Nε0 (C), then g ◦ f ∈ Nε (E) and X ⊵ g ◦ f (X). Note that this is precisely
the original statement of transitivity when C is atomic, and so we now consider this case.
    If C is atomic, then assume without loss of generality that C ∈ N+0 (P ); we must show that C is
cofinally above E. If D ∈ N+0 (P ), then D is cofinally above E, and C is cofinally above D, and the
result is immediate. Therefore assume that D is not atomic, and so there is a function g : N (D) → N (E)
as above.
    Take a point z ∈ E. As the image of g is cofinal, there is some Z ∈ N+ (E) with Z > z and
Z ∈ im(g). Let Y ∈ N+ (D) have g(Y ) = Z, and let D′ ⊆ D be the final segment of D defined by
D′ := (> Y ) ∩ D. Applying Lemma 2.2.14, we know that N+0 (D′ ) ̸= ∅, so take Y ′ ∈ N+0 (D′ ), and
let Z ′ := g(Y ′ ). As g is order-preserving, we thus see that Z ′ > Z, so we may take z ′ ∈ Z ′ such that
z ′ > Z. We know that Y ′ is cofinally above Z ′ , and thus there is y ′ ∈ Y ′ satisfying y ′ > z ′ > z. Finally,
C is cofinally above D, so there is x ∈ C with x > y ′ , whence x > z, and so C is cofinally above E, as
required.

    In the next section, we consider situations in which C ⊵ D and D ⊵ C, and thus work towards our
eventual goal of proving that ⊵ is, in some situations, a wellfounded partial order.


2.2.4   Bicomparability

We continue our consideration of orders on H(P ) with the following definition.

Definition 2.2.19. Chains C, D ∈ N+ (P ) are said to be bicomparable if we have


                        ∀x ∈ C ∃y ∈ D (x < y)         and   ∀y ∈ D ∃x ∈ C (y < x),


i.e. C is cofinal in D and D is cofinal in C. If this is the case, then we write C ≶ D. The definition for
C, D ∈ N− (P ) is as expected; C is coinitial in D and D is coinitial in C.
    We say that the chains are strongly bicomparable if C ⊵ D and D ⊵ C.

    Note that strong bicomparability implies bicomparability, but the converse does not hold in general.
    We now consider sufficient and necessary conditions for chains to be strongly bicomparable. Along
with proving that ⊵ is indeed a partial order in certain conditions, the following results shed a little
light on why vacillation is a condition that we are considering: it is under this condition that ⊵ becomes
antisymmetric (after quotienting out by ≃).
40                                                                     The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


Proposition 2.2.20. If P is scattered and chains C, D ⊆ P have C ≃+ D, then there are final segments
C ′ and D′ of C and D respectively that are strongly bicomparable. Equivalently, if C ≃− D, then the
chains have strongly bicomparable initial segments.

     Proposition 2.2.20 follows immediately from the definition of equivalence and the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2.21. If P is scattered and chains C, D ⊆ P have mutually finite incomparability and no
maximal elements, then C and D are strongly bicomparable.

Proof. Due to symmetry, it suffices to prove that C ⊵ D. Applying Lemma 2.2.4, we find an order-
preserving bijection, i.e. order-isomorphism f : C/ ∼C → D/ ∼D . This may then be extended to an
isomorphism, which we also call f , from N (C) to N (D), which we claim witnesses C ⊵ D.
     The only non-trivial condition that we need to check is that, if X ∈ N+0 (C), then X ⊵ f (X). Due to
mutually finite incomparability, for all y ∈ f (X), there must be some x ∈ X with x > y, as otherwise
y would have (⊥ y) ∩ X infinite, a contradiction. This immediately shows that X ⊵ f (X), and proves
the lemma.


     For the converse to hold, we require that P is vacillating; see Example 2.5.2 for an example of when
this can fail if P is not vacillating.

Lemma 2.2.22. If P is scattered and vacillating, and chains C, D ⊆ P are increasing and strongly
bicomparable, then C ≃+ D. Equivalently, if the chains are decreasing and strongly bicomparable,
then C ≃− D.

Proof. First assume that C is not atomic. Thus D is also not atomic and there are functions f : N (C) →
N (D) and g : N (D) → N (C) witnessing C ⊵ D and D ⊵ C respectively. Consider the function


                                         h := g ◦ f : N (C) → N (C).


Our goal is to prove that h is the identity function.
     Take some X ∈ N+ (C). Then h(X) ∈ N+ (C) is cofinally below X, and so, as they are comparable,
h(X) ≤ X. Similarly, if Y ∈ N− (C), then h(Y ) ≥ Y .
     Assume for contradiction that there is some X ∈ N+ (C) such that h(X) ̸= X (the case for N− (C)
is entirely similar). We thus know that h(X) < X. We now claim that the interval (h(X), X) ∩ H(C)
is disjoint from N− (C). Indeed, if Y ∈ N− (C) had h(X) < Y < X, then h(Y ) ≥ Y , so Y < X but
h(Y ) > h(X), contradicting the fact that h is order preserving.
     Moreover, h is injective, and so h(h(X)) ̸= h(X), and thus h(h(X)) < h(X). Repeating the above
process, and letting X0 := X and Xn+1 := h(Xn ) for all n ≥ 0, we find that X0 > X1 > X2 > · · · ,
2.2. Poset extensions and chain completeness                                                               41


and


                                    Conv{Xn : n < ω} ∩ N− (C) = ∅.


However, this contradicts the vacillation of P , and so h is indeed the identity function. Similarly, we find
that f ◦ g = idN (D) , and so f and g are mutually inverse bijections.
      It therefore suffices to prove the result for atomic chains. Moreover, we only need to consider chains
which are atomic both as increasing and decreasing chains, i.e. do not admit embeddings of either ω + 1
or (ω + 1)∗ . Such chains are precisely sub-orders of ζ, the order-type of the integers.
      Let C and D be strongly bicomparable and order-isomorphic to subsets of the integers. Then C has
a maximal element if and only if D does, so assume that neither chain has a maximal element, and take
x ∈ C. As D ⊵ C, there is some y ∈ D with y > x, and so it is immediate from symmetry that C ≃+ D.
The situation for decreasing chains is entirely similar, and thus the lemma is proved.

      We close this subsection by combining the above results into the following remark.

Remark 2.2.23. If C ≃+ C ′ , D ≃+ D′ , and C ⊵ D, then we know from Proposition 2.2.20 that there
are final segments of C and C ′ that are strongly bicomparable, and likewise for D and D′ . Thus, as we
know from Lemma 2.2.18 that ⊵ is transitive, we know that there are final segments C ′′ of C ′ and D′′ of
D′ with C ′′ ⊵ D′′ .
      Moreover, in the case wherein P is vacillating, the relation on increasing chains given by final seg-
ments being bicomparable is an equivalence relation, and it follows from the above and Lemma 2.2.22
that it is the same relation as ≃+ . Thus it makes sense to talk about ⊵ as a relation not only on chains, but
on equivalence classes of chains, and N (P ), which we will do from now on without further comment.


2.2.5     Nonprincipal chains and antichains

Finally, we conclude Section 2.2 with two more basic, but essential, results about H(P ). The first says
that, under some mild assumptions, chains in N (P ) occur within H(P )-completions of chains in P .

Lemma 2.2.24. Let P be a countable scattered poset, and let D, D′ ⊆ N (P ) be two chains consisting
only of nonprincipal elements, with D ⊆ D′ and all elements of D isolated in D′ . Then there is a chain
C ⊆ P such that, for any Y ∈ D, there is an X ∈ N (C) with X ≃ Y .

Proof. We construct a chain C as required; we will say that C witnesses an element X ∈ D if there
is Y ∈ N (C) such that X ≃ Y . The lemma thus requires that C witnesses every element of D
simultaneously. We will construct a family of chains, each witnessing one or two points of D, in such a
way that we may take the union of this family of chains, producing a single chain which witnesses all of
D.
42                                                                        The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


     Take a point X ∈ D, and assume without loss of generality that X ∈ N+ (P ) (the case for N− (P )
is of course similar). As X is isolated in D′ , we can find an element Y ∈ D′ with X ⋗ Y in D′ ; there
is no element of D′ —and thus also no element of D—between X and Y . Define the interval IX ⊆ P to
be (Y, X). We now consider two cases: either Y ∈ D and Y ∈ N− (P ), or one of these two conditions
is not the case.
     In the case where Y ∈ D and Y ∈ N− (P ), we have IX = IY = (Y, X), and so we find a chain
CX,Y witnessing both X and Y simultaneously; any chain containing CX,Y will then also witness both
X and Y . Indeed, let E be an increasing chain in P and F a decreasing chain so that E ≃+ X and
F ≃− Y . Then, as X > Y , there are points x ∈ E and y ∈ F such that Y < y < x < X; we may thus
take CX,Y := (E ∩ (≥ x)) ∪ (F ∩ (≤ y)), which is a chain, witnesses both X and Y , and is contained
in IX = IY .
     In the latter case, for all Z ∈ D \ {X}, IZ ∩ IX = ∅. We thus define a chain CX ⊆ IX which
witnesses X; any chain containing CX will then also witness X. Indeed, let E ⊆ P be an increasing
chain with E ≃+ X. As X > Y , there is thus some point x ∈ E with x > Y ; set CX := E ∩ (≥ x).
We then have CX ≃ X, and so CX witnesses X.
     We may now let C be the union of all chains CX and CX,Y constructed in the above manner. As the
intervals IX are either distinct or coincide in pairs, and D′ is a chain, we thus see that C is a chain, and
witnesses all elements of D, as required.

     We now give an example to show that the assumption of D being isolated in D′ in the statement of
Lemma 2.2.24 is necessary.

Example 2.2.25. Let P extend the poset ω ⊔ (ω ×lex ω) by setting n >P (m, r) whenever n > m as
integers. Then N (P ) = N+ (P ) is isomorphic to ω + 1 (and is, in particular, a chain), but there is no
chain in P witnessing all of N+ (P ) simultaneously. Note also that the topmost point of N (P ) is not
isolated in N (P ).

Lemma 2.2.26. Let P be an arbitrary poset, and C1 , C2 , . . . be an infinite collection of pairwise
domination-incomparable elements of N+0 (P ) such that, for all i, we have Ci ⊵ Ci+1 , and Ci ̸⊴ Ci+1 .
Then there is an infinite antichain A ⊆ P which intersects every Ci exactly once.

Proof. We firstly claim that, for each i, there is an xi ∈ Ci such that xi is not below any element of any
Cj for j > i. Assume for contradiction that there was no such x in some Cn ; let y1 , y2 , . . . be a cofinal
increasing sequence in Cn . By assumption, each yi is below some zi ∈ Cr for some r > n. But then, as
Cj ⊵Cj+1 for all j and ⊵ is transitive due to Lemma 2.2.18, we find that Cn+1 ⊵Cr , and so yi < zi < wi
for some wi ∈ Cn+1 . As the chains are atomic, this implies that Cn ⊴ Cn+1 , a contradiction. Thus there
are indeed elements xi ∈ Ci as claimed. Let Di ⊆ Ci be defined as Di = (≥ xi ) ∩ Ci for all i.
     We now find our infinite antichain inductively. Let a1 ∈ D1 be arbitrary. Assume that we have
picked ai ∈ Di for all i ≤ n. No element of Dn+1 is above any ai by definition of the sets Dj , and so
2.3. Strongly maximal chains                                                                                          43


it suffices to find a point of Dn+1 which is not below any of the ai . Dn+1 is not cofinally below any ai ,
as Di does not dominate Dn+1 , and so there is a final segment Si ⊆ Dn+1 which is incomparable to ai .
Then S := S1 ∩ . . . ∩ Sn is a nonempty final segment of Dn+1 , and so we may pick an arbitrary point
an+1 ∈ S to continue construction of our antichain. This concludes the induction, and hence we have a
set A = {a1 , a2 , . . .} such that, for all n, {a1 , a2 , . . . , an } is an antichain, i.e. A is an infinite antichain,
as required.

    Lemma 2.2.26 in particular implies that any infinite reduction-decreasing sequence of elements of
N+0 (P ) in an FAC poset P must have one dominating another.
    We note here that the conclusion of Lemma 2.2.26 does not necessarily hold when considering non-
atomic elements of N (P ); see Example 2.5.5 for a case when the conclusion fails. We moreover note
that, even in the atomic case, if we drop the reduction condition, then there can be an infinite antichain in
N+0 (P ) with no element dominating any other, as Example 2.5.3 shows. These two examples show that
Lemma 2.2.26 is in some sense optimal.


2.3     Strongly maximal chains

In this section we consider strongly maximal chains, or SMCs, as defined in Definition 1.1.8, and prove
Theorem 1.1.11. SMCs are interesting structures in their own right, but the results presented here also
serve a dual purpose as a warm-up for the more technical results to come in Section 2.4. Indeed, an
SMC can be thought of as a “weaker” spine: it is easy to see that a spine in a poset must certainly be an
SMC, and while being an SMC is not sufficient to be a spine in an infinite poset (see Example 2.5.1), it
is certainly a step in the right direction.
    To prove that all countable FAC posets have SMCs, we first prove the following partial result, requir-
ing that our poset P is also scattered.

Lemma 2.3.1. Every countable scattered FAC poset has a strongly maximal chain.

    This will be proved by first finding an intermediate structure, and then augmenting this to an SMC,
which is defined as follows.

Definition 2.3.2. We say that a chain C in a poset P is an ℵ0 -strongly maximal chain, or ℵ0 -SMC for
short, if it is maximal and there are no contiguous chains D ⊆ P \ C and E ⊆ C with |D| ≥ ℵ0 ,
|D| > |C|, and (C \ E) ∪ D a maximal chain.

    In other words, when P is countable, C is an ℵ0 -SMC if we cannot remove a finite contiguous
subchain of C and insert an infinite contiguous chain in its place.
    We will then extend this to the case of arbitrary countable FAC posets by using another intermedi-
ate step: finding a chain in P which is strongly maximal amongst only those chains which are order
isomorphic to Q.
44                                                                       The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


     We now find each of these structures in turn.


2.3.1    Scattered posets

First, we prove the following lemma.


Lemma 2.3.3. Every countable scattered FAC poset P contains an ℵ0 -SMC.


Proof. Let Q ⊆ N 0 (P ) consist only of those atomic elements which are equivalent to chains order-
isomorphic to ω or ω ∗ , and let Q inherit the structure of a poset from H(P ). Take a maximal chain
D ⊆ Q (which we know exists by a trivial application of Zorn’s lemma), and let D′ ⊇ D be given by
D′ = H(D) ⊆ H(P ).
     We claim that every point of D is isolated in D′ ; assume for contradiction that X ∈ D was an
accumulation point of D′ , i.e. for every Y < X in D′ there was some Z ∈ D′ with Y < Z < X (or
similarly for every Y > X). But then W := sup{Y ∈ D′ : Y < X} is an element of D′ , as D′ is
complete, and, due to the fact that X is an accumulation point, is not witnessed by a chain of order type
ω or ω ∗ . Thus W ∈ D′ \ D, and so W < X. Thus there is some Z with W < Z < X, contradicting the
definition of W .
     This means that we may apply Lemma 2.2.24, and find a chain C ′ ⊆ P witnessing all of D. Extend
C ′ arbitrarily to form a maximal chain C. We claim that C is an ℵ0 -SMC.
     Indeed, assume for contradiction that C is not ℵ0 -strongly maximal. Then there is a finite contiguous
subchain of C which can be removed and replaced by an infinite section to form a new chain E. This
operation must introduce a new element of Q, (i.e. (H(E)\H(C))∩Q is non-empty), but this contradicts
the maximality of D in Q. Thus C is a ℵ0 -SMC, as required.


     We now take an ℵ0 -SMC C ⊆ P and use this to construct a strongly maximal chain. Recall the
definition of ∼C for a chain C from Definition 2.2.3. We now define a similar relation on the whole of
P.

Definition 2.3.4. For elements x, y ∈ P with x < y, set x ∼ y (and y ∼ x, so ∼ is symmetric) if the
interval ⟨x, y⟩ as defined in (2.1.4) is finite.

     Note that C is an SMC if and only if it is a maximal chain, and ∼C coincides with ∼ on C. In
general, ∼C is an equivalence relation on C, but ∼ need not be an equivalence relation on P . Take for
example the poset formed by Z ∪ {⊤, ⊥, X, Y }, where ⊤ is the top element, ⊥ is the bottom element,
⊥ < X < Y < ⊤, and both X and Y are incomparable to all elements of Z. Then ⊥ ∼ X, X ∼ Y , and
X ∼ ⊤, but ⊥ ̸∼ ⊤.
     Similarly to as in Lemma 2.2.4, we may partition a chain C into equivalence classes under ∼C ; for
2.3. Strongly maximal chains                                                                              45


any x ∈ C, the equivalence class [x]C is defined as follows.


                                       [x]C := {y ∈ C : x ∼C y},


i.e. the set of elements of C a finite distance from x.
    We are now ready to prove Lemma 2.3.1. Note that the [x]C form a partition of C; let R be a set of
∼C -equivalence class representatives, so that for any x ∈ C, |R ∩ [x]C | = 1. We may then define

                                      [
                              Qx :=       { ⟨I⟩ : I ⊆ [x]C , I is finite } ⊆ P,


where ⟨·⟩ is as defined in (2.1.5). This poset must contain no proper infinite interval, for doing so would
contradict the ℵ0 -maximality of C. But then, by Theorem 1.1.6, there is an SMC Dx ⊆ Qx . We now
                S
claim that D := x∈R Dx is an SMC in P .
    Indeed, if we assume for contradiction that some finite contiguous sub-chain of D could be removed
and replaced with a larger chain, then this replacement must happen entirely within some Qx . But D∩Qx
is an SMC in Qx for any x, and so such a replacement cannot occur. Thus D is an SMC, as required.
This completes the proof of Lemma 2.3.1.


2.3.2   Non-scattered posets

We now drop the assumption that P must be scattered, and work with arbitrary countable FAC posets.
Much like the ℵ0 -SMCs discussed in the previous sections, we again go via an intermediate structure.
Recall that η is the order type of Q and that I(X) is the set of all intervals of X.

Definition 2.3.5. A chain C ⊆ P is said to be η-maximal if C has order type η, and the only chain
D ⊆ P with order type η and C ⊆ D is C itself. An η-strongly maximal chain, or η-SMC for short,
is then an η-maximal chain C which admits no η-replacement. An η-replacement from one η-maximal
chain C to another, C ′ , consists of a function f : C → I(C ′ ) satisfying all of the following.

    • For all distinct x, y ∈ C, we have f (x) ∩ f (y) = ∅, and moreover, if x < y then f (x) < f (y).
    • For all x ∈ C, the interval f (x) ⊆ C ′ is either the singleton {x}, or contains a set of order type η.
    • For all x, y ∈ C and z ∈ f (y), if x < y then x < z, and if y < x then z < x.

We will say in this case that f witnesses the replacement, and we will write C ⪯η C ′ .

    The key lemma here is that such chains exist.

Lemma 2.3.6. A countable FAC poset P has an η-SMC.

Proof. We need to prove that there is a chain which is maximal under ⪯η amongst η-maximal chains;
for this, we will use Zorn’s lemma. It thus suffices to prove that ⪯η is a partial order, and any ⪯η -chain
of chains of P has an upper bound.
46                                                                          The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


     We first prove that ⪯η is a partial order. It is clear that C ⪯η C for any η-maximal chain C; this is
witnessed by the function f sending x to {x}; call such a function trivial.
     Next, ⪯η is transitive: assume that C ⪯η D ⪯η E, and the witnessing functions are f : C → I(D)
and g : D → I(E). We claim that the function h : C → I(E) defined by h(x) := Conv(g[f (x)]) ∩ E
witnesses that C ⪯η E; indeed, h can easily be checked to satisfy all the requirements of Definition 2.3.5.
     Finally, we need to show that C ⪯η D ⪯η C implies that C = D. In fact, we prove a stronger
statement, captured by the following claim.

Claim 2.3.7. Let C, D, and E be η-maximal chains in P with C ⪯η D ⪯η E, and assume that there is
a point x with x ∈ C and x ∈
                           / D. Then x ∈
                                       / E.

Proof. Assume for contradiction that C ⪯η D is witnessed by a function f with x ∈
                                                                                / im(f ), and so in
particular f (x) ̸= {x}, and that D ⪯η E is witnessed by g, with x ∈ im(g).
     If we have x ∈ E \ g[f (x)], then g[f (x)] is comparable with x, and we know by definition of ⪯η
that g[f (x)] is comparable with C − x, and thus is comparable with C, but this contradicts η-maximality
of C. Thus we must have x ∈ g[f (x)].
     In this case, either X := g[f (x)] ∩ (> x) or Y := g[f (x)] ∩ (< x) must contain a set of order type η;
without loss of generality say it is X. But then X is comparable to all of C, and so we again contradict
η-maximality of C, and the claim is proved

     We can now see that antisymmetry of ⪯η follows immediately from Claim 2.3.7, and thus ⪯η is a
partial order.
     Our next task is to prove that any ⪯η -chain has an upper bound. We first show that any countable ⪯η -
chain has an upper bound, and then use this result to prove that there can be no uncountable ⪯η -chain.
Indeed, assume that
                                          C1 ⪯η C2 ⪯η C3 ⪯η · · ·

is such a chain, and assume that Cn ⪯η Cn+1 is witnessed by fn . We claim that any η-maximal chain C
containing limn→∞ Cn has Cn ⪯η C for all n; we prove this result for n = 1, as for larger values of n
we may simply forget the first few terms of the sequence without changing the limit set C. One of the
main difficulties here is simply proving that the limit set is non-empty.
     Our key tool here is the rooted tree Tx , defined as follows for arbitrary x ∈ C1 . Define the sets
Y0 , Y1 , Y2 , . . . by first setting Y0 := {x}, and then Ym+1 := fm [Ym ] for any m ≥ 0. Tx is then defined
                               S
on vertex set V (Tx ) := m<ω Ym by setting x to be the root, and for any y, z ∈ V (T ), setting z to be a
child of y if there is an i such that y ∈ Yi , y ∈
                                                 / Yi+1 , and z ∈ fi (y) (i.e. y is removed at step i and z is a
point added immediately in its place).

Claim 2.3.8. For all x ∈ C1 , Tx is a rooted tree, with every vertex either a leaf or having infinitely many
children. Furthermore, Tx has no infinite branch.
2.3. Strongly maximal chains                                                                                  47


Proof. It is immediate from Claim 2.3.7 that Tx is indeed a well-defined tree, and from the definition of
η-replacement that, if a vertex is not a leaf, then it has infinitely many children. Thus it remains to prove
that Tx has no infinite branch, so assume for contradiction that Tx does have an infinite branch.
    Translating back into the language of η-replacements, this says that there are sequences x1 , x2 , . . .
of points and n1 , n2 , . . . of indices such that, for each i, we have


                         xi ∈ Cni , xi ∈
                                       / Cni +1 , xi+1 ∈
                                                       / Cni , and xi+1 ∈ fni (xi ),


i.e. at step ni , xi is replaced by a set containing xi+1 . Moreover, we may assume by choice of xi+1 that
both (≥ xi+1 ) ∩ fn+1 (xi ) and (≤ xi+1 ) ∩ fn+1 (xi ) contain a dense linear order. We claim that, in this
situation, {x1 , x2 , . . .} would be an infinite antichain of P ; assume otherwise that for some r < s we
have xr ≤ xs (the case xr ≥ xs is entirely similar). Define


                                   D := {y ∈ Cnr : y > xr }, and

                                   Q := {z ∈ P : ∀y ∈ D, xr < z < y}.


    We claim that if y ∈ D then y > xs . Indeed, this follows by induction on j in the statement y > xr+j ;
this is true by definition for j = 0, and the inductive step follows immediately from the definition of ⪯η .
    But then E := (≥ xs ) ∩ Cns +1 ∩ Q is entirely above xr and entirely below D by definition of Q,
and E contains a dense linear order. This contradicts the η-maximality of Cnr , and so there is indeed no
infinite branch of Tx , and the claim is proved.

    We now prove that C1 ⪯η C. Define the function f : C1 → I(C) by setting f (x) := limn→∞ Yn ,
where the sets Yn are as defined before the statement of Claim 2.3.8. We claim that f witnesses C1 ⪯η C.
    We first prove that either f (x) = {x}, or f (x) contains a set of order type η. If f (x) = {x}, then we
have nothing to prove, so assume that this is not the case. We may thus apply Claim 2.3.8 and assume
that y is a non-leaf vertex of Tx such that all children of y are leaves.
    Indeed, if we say that leaves are of height 0, and then define height inductively on the rest of the
forest, then it suffices to find any vertex of a finite height greater than zero, as we may then find a vertex
of height 1, which has the required property. Thus assume for contradiction that all vertices are of height
either 0 or infinity. There must be a vertex of infinite height, so select it, and then successively choose
children of infinite height, which will produce an infinite branch in the forest, a contradiction.
    Thus there is an i such that y ∈ Yi , and all elements of fi (y) are in all of Yi+1 , Yi+2 , . . . , and thus
fi+1 (y) ⊆ f (x). Therefore, if f (x) is not the singleton {x}, then it contains a set of order type η, as
required.
    The other two requirements of f witnessing an η-replacement are immediate, and thus we do indeed
have that C1 ⪯η C.
48                                                                       The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


     It remains to prove that a sequence of η-replacements cannot be uncountable, and so assume for
contradiction that there was a family of distinct chains (Cα : α < λ) which formed a ⪯η -chain for some
uncountable ordinal λ. We know that, for all α < λ, there is an xα ∈ Cα with xα ∈
                                                                                / Cα+1 , and thus by
Claim 2.3.7, xα ∈
                / Cβ for any β > α. But then the family (xα : α < λ) would be an uncountable family
of distinct elements of P , contradicting the countability of P .
     From the above, we know that any ⪯η -chain of η-maximal chains has an upper bound, and so we
may apply Zorn’s lemma to find an η-SMC, as required, completing the proof of Lemma 2.3.6.


     We are now ready to prove Theorem 1.1.11, that P has an SMC. Take an η-SMC D ⊆ P , and
identify a maximal dense subset E of D with Q. We may then define, for each r ∈ R (considering R as
the completion of E ∼
                    = Q), the set


               Dr := {y ∈ P : (∀z ∈ D)((z > r =⇒ z > y) ∧ (z < r =⇒ z < y))}.


     Note that each Dr must be a scattered poset, as if it contained a copy of Q, then D would not be
an η-SMC. Applying Lemma 2.3.1 to each Dr , we can find an SMC Cr ⊆ Dr . We now claim that
C := ∪r∈R Cr is an SMC in P .
     Firstly, C is indeed a chain: take some u ∈ Cx and v ∈ Cy for reals x < y. Then, as D ⊇ E ∼
                                                                                               = Q,
there is a z ∈ E with x < z < y, and so u < z < v, and so C is a chain.
     Next, C is maximal: if w ∈ P was completely comparable to C, then it would have to lie in Dr for
some r ∈ R, but then we would have that Cr was not maximal, a contradiction.
     Similarly, if C were not strongly maximal, the replacement witnessing this fact must lie entirely
inside some Dr , as all other intervals of C are infinite. But this would contradict the fact that Cr is an
SMC. Thus C is an SMC of P , as required. This completes the proof of Theorem 1.1.11.
     There are many further questions that can be asked about SMCs, and this is discussed in Section 2.6.



2.4     Proof of Theorem 1.1.14

In this section, we prove the Aharoni–Korman conjecture for the class of countable vacillating FAC
posets. Recall that a poset is vacillating if there is no contiguous chain C in P such that either C or C ∗
has every proper interval wellfounded, but is not itself wellfounded.
     Much like in Section 2.3, our proof will proceed by first considering the case of scattered posets, and
then moving on to consider posets which admit an embedding of Q.
     The following definition will be useful in discussing vacillating posets.

Definition 2.4.1. We say that a poset P is in-wellfounded (hereinafter IWF) if there is no chain C ⊆ P
which is order isomorphic to ζ; there is no increasing chain in C above a decreasing chain. Similarly, P
2.4. Proof of Theorem 1.1.14                                                                                49


is said to be out-wellfounded (hereinafter OWF) if it contains no chain order-isomorphic to ω ⊕ ω ∗ , i.e.
there is no decreasing chain above an increasing one.

    The proof of Theorem 1.1.14 follows three main steps, each of which is given its own subsection.
We now provide an overview of these steps.

  (1) Alternating replacements and alternating maximal chains. We seek to break up the poset P
        into smaller chunks, and then work on each chunk separately. The “alternations” in question
        are between wellfoundedness and co-wellfoundedness, and we find a chain which is, in a certain
        sense, “maximally alternating.” (The name vacillating was chosen as such posets must alternate,
        or vacillate, between these wellfounded and co-wellfounded parts.)
  (2) Reduced chains. We now require that P is vacillating, and find a chain C which admits no
        reduction, in the sense of Definition 2.2.15. This will turn out to be precisely the condition we
        need to make the remainder of the proof work. Producing a reduced chain will follow from an
        application of Zorn’s lemma.
  (3) Thick chains. We will expand our reduced chain to a larger structure, finding a sub-poset of P in
        which every element is incomparable to only finitely many other elements (which still has a large-
        scale structure which resembles a chain, hence the name ‘thick chain’). We will take a reduced
        chain C, and then take the set F of all elements comparable to all but finitely many elements of C.
        This will turn out to be a thick chain, and moreover the points outside F will not only have infinite
        incomparability to F , but to arbitrary antichain partitions of F ; we will call such structures strong
        thick chains. We then prove that we can drop the assumption that P is scattered, and that, in fact,
        every strong thick chain of P contains a spine of P , proving Theorem 1.1.14.

    We remark here that, while the possibility of combining the replacements in Sections 2.4.1 and 2.4.2
into a single replacement operation might seem appealing, we have not pursued this avenue to better
distinguish which parts of the proof require P to be vacillating. In particular, it is only in Section 2.4.2
that the vacillation condition is required, and so we can find an alternating maximal chain even without
this assumption. An explanation of the ways in which the proof in Section 2.4.2 breaks down in the case
of non-vacillating posets is discussed in more detail in Section 2.5. Example 2.5.6 (for which Conjec-
ture 1.1.1 fails) in particular highlights why the vacillation condition is necessary to our arguments.


2.4.1     Alternating replacements

The goal of this section is to prove the existence of a particular type of chain in our poset P , which
we will call alternating-maximal. This will be used as a stepping-stone to get to the more complex
requirements of later sections, and we will again use the method of replacements. While the definition of
a reduced chain might at first seem technical, the idea behind it is simpler, and we explain this idea now.
50                                                                          The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


     It will be important in the sequel that in our chain C, if X is an interval of C and X is OWF, then the
interval Conv(X) (as defined in (2.1.3)) of P is also OWF. To find such a chain, we seek a chain which
admits no alternating replacement, which can be thought of as removing some OWF part of the chain
C, and replacing it with another chain which is not OWF. The substance of this section is primarily in
formalising the above idea into an actual definition, and then setting up a framework in which we can
find such a chain.
     Indeed, much like how, in Section 2.3, we proved first that we could find an ℵ0 -SMC, and then
proceeded to extend this to an SMC, we also have an intermediate step in this section. We first find a
chain which is strongly maximal in a particular subset of N (P ) (here, this subset will consist of points
called illfounded limits, to be defined shortly). Then, with this chain in hand, we show that between any
two consecutive illfounded limit points, we can find a locally alternating-maximal chain; then, taking a
union of these chains, we produce the alternating-maximal chain that we desire.
     We now give formal definitions of all of the above.

Definition 2.4.2. Given a chain C, the alternation number of C, written alt(C), is the value in N ∪ {∞}
equal to the supremum of those integers n such that there are disjoint continuous subchains I1 , I2 , . . . , In ⊆
C with each Ii containing elements X, Y ∈ N (P ) with X ∈ N+ (P ), Y ∈ N− (P ), and X > Y .
     An element X ∈ N (P ) is said to be an illfounded limit if there is no contiguous IWF chain I ⊆
H(P ) containing both X and elements x, y ∈ P with x < X < y. Such an X is either a decreasing
limit of increasing elements of N (P ) or an increasing limit of decreasing elements. Equivalently, every
convex subset of P containing X contains a chain passing through X of infinite alternation number.
     We say that an interval I ⊆ H(P ) is alternating-wellfounded if it has no internal illfounded limit
point.
                                                                                               ◦
     With the above definitions in hand, we can now proceed to define the equivalence relation ∼ C on a
                        ◦
chain C. Intuitively, x ∼ C y can be thought of as meaning that “x and y are in the same OWF part of

C”. However, this intuition runs into a slight snag: consider the poset (in fact, total order) comprising of
the following linear sum.


                                       Q := ω ∗ ⊕ ω ⊕ {a} ⊕ ω ∗ ⊕ ω.                                    (2.4.1)


     This poset certainly has alt(Q) = 2, as it consists of two OWF sections. The difficulty, however, is
to which of these two OWF parts the point a is assigned; it could go in either. For this reason, we need to
be more careful, and deal with these finite intervals specifically. This motivates the following definition.
                                                                                               ◦
Definition 2.4.3. Points x and y in a chain C are said to be alternating-equivalent, written x ∼ C y, if all

three of the following conditions on the interval (x, y) of C hold, assuming x < y (the case x > y is of
course similar).
2.4. Proof of Theorem 1.1.14                                                                              51


    • The interval (x, y) of C is OWF.
    • If there is an increasing limit of C between x and y, i.e. (x, y) ∩ N+ (C) ̸= ∅, then Y := inf{Z ∈
      N (C) : Z > y} must have Y ∈ N+ (C).
    • If there is a decreasing limit of C between x and y, i.e. (x, y) ∩ N− (C) ̸= ∅, then X := sup{Z ∈
      N (C) : Z < x} must have X ∈ N− (C).

    Note that taking the inf and sup above are valid, as they are taken over chains, on which H(C) is a
completion. Note further that if x ∼C y, i.e. the distance from x to y is finite, then all three of the above
                                        ◦
conditions hold automatically, and so x ∼ C y. Given that we have called our relation an ‘equivalence’,

the following lemma should come as no surprise.

                                                ◦
Lemma 2.4.4. For any chain C ⊆ P , the relation ∼ C is an equivalence relation on C.


                                                                                     ◦
Proof. First, we may restrict our poset P so that P = C. Reflexivity and symmetry of ∼ follow immedi-
                             ◦
ately from the definition of ∼ C , so it remains to check transitivity. Assume that points x, y, z ∈ C have
 ◦     ◦
x∼ C y ∼C z.

    We split into two cases, depending on whether y is between x and z or not.


    In the first case, in which y is between x and z, we may assume that x < y < z. Assume for
contradiction that (x, z) is not OWF, i.e. there are X, Y ∈ (x, z) with X ∈ N+ (C), Y ∈ N− (C), and
X < Y . We must have X < y < Y to avoid an immediate contradiction, but then X ∈ (x, y), so we
know that W := inf{Z ∈ N (C) : Z > y} ∈ N+ (C). Thus W < Y , contradicting the fact that (y, z) is
OWF. Therefore, (x, z) is OWF.
    Next, assume that there is some element X ∈ (x, z) ∩ N+ (C). If X ∈ (y, z), then we immediately
know that the infimal point of N (C) above z is in N+ (C), as required, so assume that we must take
X ∈ (x, y). Thus the infimal point Y of N (C) above y is in N+ (C). We cannot have Y < z, as this
would contradict our assumption that there is no point of N+ (C) in the interval (y, z) of C. Thus Y > z,
and so Y is also the infimal point of N (C) above z.


    In the second case, in which y is not between x and z, we may assume that x < z < y. In this case,
the fact that (x, z) is OWF follows immediately from the fact that (x, y) is OWF, so we are left to prove
the other two points.
    Firstly, if (x, z) contains a point of N+ (P ), then, as (x, y) is OWF, every element of N ((z, y)) is in
N+ ((z, y)), and so the infimal element of N (C) above z is in N+ (P ), as required.
    Secondly, if (x, z) contains a point of N− (P ), then the fact that the supremal element of N (C) below
                                                        ◦
x is in N+ (C) follows immediately from the fact that x ∼ C y.
                                  ◦
    This completes the proof that ∼ C is an equivalence relation on C.
52                                                                         The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


      Now that we have proved Lemma 2.4.4, we can continue with our definitions.
                                                                                   ◦
Definition 2.4.5. We define the set of alternation classes of C, R(C), to be those ∼ C -equivalence classes

that are infinite, that is

                                                  ◦
                                  R(C) := {X ∈ C/ ∼ C : X is infinite}.


                    ◦
We will write C ≃ D if R(C) = R(D); this is clearly an equivalence relation.

      Note, for example, that if C is finite, then R(C) is empty. For another example, in the case of Q as
                                    ◦
defined in (2.4.1), there are three ∼ Q -equivalence classes, one of which consists only of {a}. R(Q) thus

has two elements.
      Moreover, elements of R(C) are pairwise disjoint intervals of C, and so R(C) inherits a linear order
from C, and so is itself a linearly ordered set under ≤. It thus makes sense, for example, to talk about
the set I(R(C)) of intervals of R(C).
      We can now, finally, define the notion of replacement that we will use in this section. We will
primarily consider these replacements taking place on alternating-wellfounded posets, apart from when
we prove that it suffices to only consider alternating-wellfounded posets.

Definition 2.4.6 (Alternating replacements). If P is a scattered poset, and C, C ′ ⊆ P are two chains,
then C ′ is an alternating replacement of C if there is a function f : R(C) → I(R(C ′ )) satisfying the
following properties.

     (i) If R(C) is empty, then R(C ′ ) is non-empty.
  (ii) If X, Y ∈ R(C) are distinct, then f (X) ∩ f (Y ) = ∅.
 (iii) f is order-preserving. That is, if X, Y ∈ R(C) have X < Y , then for any U ∈ f (X) and
        V ∈ f (Y ), we have U < V .
 (iv) If X ∈ R(C), Y ∈ R(C ′ ) and z ∈ H(P ) have Y ∈ f (X) and z > X, then z > Y . Likewise, if
        z < X, then z < Y .
                                                                                          S
  (v) If, for all X ∈ R(C), we have that |f (X)| = 1 is a singleton and moreover              X∈R(C) f (X) =
        R(C ′ ), then f (X) = {X} for all X.
                                                  ◦
If all of these conditions hold, then we write C ⪯ C ′ , and say that this replacement is witnessed by f . If
item (v) applies and f (X) = {X} for all X, then we say that the replacement is trivial. We may then
                                                                ◦                     ◦
say that C is an alternating maximal chain if we have that C ⪯ D implies that C ≃ D, i.e. C admits no
nontrivial alternating replacement.

      As discussed previously, our goal is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 2.4.7. Let P be a countable scattered FAC poset. Then P has an alternating maximal chain C.

      Note in particular that we do not require that P is vacillating; that assumption will only be used in
2.4. Proof of Theorem 1.1.14                                                                                53


future sections. We first prove the following lemma, which shows that illfounded limit points cannot be
removed from a chain via alternating replacements.

Lemma 2.4.8. Let C ⊆ P be a chain, and let X ∈ N (C) be an illfounded limit point. Then, for any
                   ◦
chain D with C ⪯ D, there will be an illfounded limit point Y ∈ N (D) with X ≶ Y .

Proof. This follows from item (iv) of Definition 2.4.6: let X1 , X2 , . . . be an increasing sequence in
N− (C) with sup{Xn : n < ω} = X, the illfounded limit point in question. (The case of a decreasing
                                                                                                        ◦
sequence in N+ (C) is similar.) Let f : R(C) → I(R(D)) witness the alternating replacement C ⪯ D.
Then take Yn ∈ f (Xn ) for each n; by Definition 2.4.6 item (iii), these points form an increasing sequence
in D, and their supremum is thus an illfounded limit point Y . Moreover, item (iv) of Definition 2.4.6
tells us that Xn < Yn+1 < Xn+2 , and so these sequences are bicomparable, as required.

    We will now restrict our attention to alternating-wellfounded posets before generalising to Lemma 2.4.7,
and thus prove the following lemma.

Lemma 2.4.9. Let P be an scattered alternating-wellfounded FAC poset. Then P has an alternating
maximal chain C.

Proof. The proof will follow the same lines as that of Theorem 1.1.11, and proceed via Zorn’s lemma.
Note that, as P is alternating-wellfounded, if C ⊆ P is any chain, then R(C) is order-isomorphic to a
subset of ζ.
    We will consider only chains that occur as alternating replacements of some starting chain C0 ; we
require C0 to witness illfounded limit points at the top and bottom of P if they exist, i.e. if there exists a
chain C of P with R(C) unbounded in both directions, then start with C0 being such a chain. Otherwise,
start with R(C0 ) infinite if possible, and if this is not possible, then an arbitrary chain will do. Let U be
defined as follows.

                                                                 ◦
                                       U := {D ∈ N (P ) : C0 ⪯ D}.


    By Lemma 2.4.8, we see that all D ∈ U will also witness all illfounded limit points witnessed by C0 ,
i.e. those at the top and bottom of P , if they exist.
    It suffices to prove the following five statements, which we will consider in turn. The first three hold
for arbitrary maximal chains, whereas for the final two we consider only chains in U.
                                      ◦
  (i) For any chain C, we have C ⪯ C.
                                                    ◦    ◦                ◦
  (ii) For any chains C, D, and E such that C ⪯ D ⪯ E, we have C ⪯ E.
               ◦   ◦                            ◦
 (iii) If C ⪯ D ⪯ C, then we must have C ≃ D.
               ◦       ◦       ◦                                                                        ◦
 (iv) If C1 ⪯ C2 ⪯ C3 ⪯ · · · are chains in U, then there is a chain C ∈ U such that, for all n, Cn ⪯ C,
                           ◦
      i.e. countable ⪯-chains have upper bounds.
54                                                                          The Aharoni–Korman conjecture

                                  ◦
  (v) There is no uncountable ⪯-chain of distinct chains in U.

     Firstly, item (i) is immediate as it is witnessed by the function sending X to {X} for all X ∈ N (C).
                                                           ◦                                ◦
     Next, item (ii) is also simple: if the replacement C ⪯ D is witnessed by f , and D ⪯ E by g, then the
function h : R(C) → R(E) given by h(X) := g[f (X)] can be seen to satisfy all the required conditions
               ◦
to witness C ⪯ E.
                                                                    ◦                                   ◦
     For item (iii), the case of antisymmetry, assume that C ⪯ D is witnessed by f , and D ⪯ C is
witnessed by g. Define the function h : R(C) → I(R(C)) by h(X) := g[f (X)].
     We first claim that h must send X to {X} for every X ∈ R(C). Indeed, assume for contradiction
that for some X there was Y ∈ h(X) with Y > X (the case of Y < X is entirely similar). It then
immediately follows from item (iv) of Definition 2.4.6 that Y > Y , a contradiction.
                                                                                            S
