     We now seek to apply item (v) of Definition 2.4.6, and to this end we show that            X∈R(C) f (X) =
R(D). Assume for contradiction that there is some Y ∈ R(D) not in the image of f . Then we know
that g(Y ) contains some element Z ∈ R(C), but we also have that g[f (Z)] = {Z}, and this contradicts
item (ii) of Definition 2.4.6; the disjointedness of images of elements of R(D) under g. Thus we may
indeed apply item (v) of Definition 2.4.6 and deduce that f (X) = {X} for all X ∈ R(C), and that
                                                                                                    ◦
f hits every element of R(D). We may therefore conclude that R(C) = R(D), and thus ⪯ is indeed
                        ◦
antisymmetric (up to ≃).
                                             ◦                                                          ◦
     At this point, we have proved that ⪯ is a preorder (or a partial order after quotienting out by ≃), and
it remains to show that Zorn’s lemma can be applied.
                                         ◦       ◦   ◦
     Assume, as in item (iv), that C1 ⪯ C2 ⪯ C3 ⪯ · · · are chains in U witnessed by f1 , f2 , f3 , . . . . This
is the point at which we need to use that P is alternating-wellfounded, and we will prove a claim very
similar to Claim 2.3.8, which will allow us to take the limit of the sequence C1 , C2 , . . . .
     Indeed, define the forest F on vertex set


                                      V := {(n, X) : n < ω, X ∈ R(Cn )}


with edges given by connecting (n, X) to (n + 1, Y ) whenever Y ∈ fn (X). It is immediate from
definition that F is a forest of rooted trees. Note that F might not be a tree, as replacements could happen
              ◦
in the finite ∼-classes that are not elements of some R(Cn ), which would not occur as an element of
anything in the image of the witnessing function fn .
     We will consider children of elements of the forest (and their descendants) as below their parents.
The key claim about F is the following.

Claim 2.4.10. Any infinite branch (k, Xk ), (k + 1, Xk+1 ), (k + 2, Xk+2 ), . . . of the forest F consists
eventually of only vertices with exactly one child. That is, there is an integer N such that for all n > N ,
2.4. Proof of Theorem 1.1.14                                                                             55


the only child of (n, Xn ) in T is (n + 1, Xn+1 ). Furthermore, all degrees of F are finite.


Proof. We first prove that degrees in F are finite. Take some (n, X) ∈ R(Cn ), and assume for con-
tradiction that it had infinitely many children; let {(n + 1, Yi ) : i < ω} be these children. The set
{Yi : i < ω} either contains an infinite increasing or decreasing sequence; assume without loss of gen-
erality that Yi < Yi+1 for all i. We thus see that R(Cn+1 ) has an infinite increasing sequence, and so by
construction so too does R(C0 ), and, by applying Lemma 2.4.8, R(Cn ) also has no maximal element.
    Therefore there is some (n, Z) ∈ R(Cn ) with Z > X, and so by item (iv) of Definition 2.4.6,
Z > Yi for all i. But this contradicts the fact that P is alternating-wellfounded, and so we see that
degrees in F are indeed finite.
    Next, assume for contradiction that there is an infinite branch of F containing infinitely many vertices
                                                       ◦
with at least two children. Due to transitivity of ⪯, we may restrict to a subsequence of the branch and
in fact assume that we have


                                  (k, Xk ), (k + 1, Xk+1 ), (k + 2, Xk+2 ), . . .


all with at least two children. Indeed, assume that, for all n ≥ k, (n + 1, Zn+1 ) is a child of (n, Xn )
different from (n + 1, Xn+1 ). By again restricting to a subsequence, we may assume without loss
of generality that Zn+1 > Xn+1 for all n. But then, by item (iv) of Definition 2.4.6 we know that
Zn+1 > Zn for all n, and we may deduce a contradiction as we did for the Yi . Thus every branch of F
eventually has only vertices with exactly one child, completing the proof of Claim 2.4.10.


    With the above claim in hand, we can now complete the proof of Lemma 2.4.9. Firstly, if the
sequence C1 , C2 , C3 , . . . is eventually constant, then we may simply take this limiting chain. Thus we
may assume (by passing to a subsequence if necessary) that item (v) of Definition 2.4.6 never applies.
    We call an element (n, X) of F a stabilisation head if it and all its descendants have only one child
in F . If (n, X) is a stabilisation head and (m, Y ) is equal to or a descendant of (n, X) in F , then we
say that (m, Y ) is in the tail of (n, X). Let D be the set of all stabilisation heads in F , and D(n, X) be
the set of stabilisation heads below (n, X), and note that by Claim 2.4.10, this set is always finite, and
nonempty if there is no stabilisation head above D(n, X).
    Note further that if (n, X) and (m, Y ) are both stabilisation heads, then X and Y are comparable
due to item (iv) of Definition 2.4.6. We may thus define our chain C as follows.

                                                       (n,X)∈D

     It is then easy to check that all conditions of Definition 2.4.6 hold for this f , and so Cn ⪯ C for all
n, as required.

     It thus remains only to prove item (v), that there are no uncountable chains of alternating replace-
ments. However, this is immediate from the easy corollary of Claim 2.4.10 that every proper interval
of C admits only finite sequences of alternating replacements; a sequence of alternating replacements in
the whole of P is then a countable union of finite sets, which is necessarily countable.
     This completes the proof of Lemma 2.4.9.

     Our remaining task is thus to reduce the problem to that of alternating-wellfounded posets. The final
missing ingredient for a proof of Lemma 2.4.7 is that we can find a chain of illfounded limit points such
that the interval of P between any two consecutive illfounded limits is alternating-wellfounded. Indeed,
this is analogous to the ℵ0 -SMC found in Section 2.3, and we find it through similar means.

Lemma 2.4.11. If P is a scattered FAC poset, then P has a chain C such that, for X, Y ∈ N (P )
consecutive illfounded limits of C, the interval (X, Y ) ⊆ P is alternating-wellfounded.

Proof. Consider the sub-poset Q of N (P ) consisting only of illfounded limit points. Simply take a
maximal chain D in Q (under domination). It is certainly the case that the interval between any two
consecutive points of D, there is no illfounded limit (otherwise we would contradict the maximality of
D), and so it suffices to find a chain C ⊆ P extending D which witnesses every illfounded limit of D.
     We know from Lemma 2.2.9 that N (P ) is scattered, and so we let E be the set of elements of D
which are not themselves accumulation points of D (as in Definition 2.2.10). The elements of E are thus
all isolated in D, and so we can apply Lemma 2.2.24 to find a chain C ⊆ P such that every element of
E is a limit point of C ′ .
     Note that it is immediate from Lemma 2.2.4 that if X ∈ N+ (P ) is an illfounded limit and X ≃+ Y ,
then Y is also an illfounded limit. Thus we know that C does indeed witness the fact that every element
of E is an illfounded limit point. Therefore, it suffices to prove that C satisfies the condition in question.
     Indeed, let X, Y ∈ N (P ) be consecutive illfounded limits of C with X < Y . As the chain D was
maximal in Q, and only accumulation points of D were removed to form E, we see that in fact, X, Y ∈ E
and Y ⋗ X in D. But then it is immediate that the interval (X, Y ) of P is alternating-wellfounded, and
Lemma 2.4.11 is proved.

     We can finally put all the steps together to prove the main lemma of this section.

2.4.2   Reduced chains

For the entirety of this section, we will assume that our poset is vacillating.
    Our tool here will be reductions, as defined in Definition 2.2.15. We will call a reduction C ⊵ D
trivial if N (C) = N (D), and the reduction is witnessed by the identity function. The goal of this section
will be to show that, when P is vacillating, there are chains that admit no non-trivial reduction.

Definition 2.4.12. Call a maximal, alternating maximal, chain in P a candidate chain, and denote the set
of all candidate chains by C(P ). A candidate chain C is said to be reduced there there is no non-trivial
reduction of C.

    Note that if C is not a SMC, then we can remove a finite interval of C and insert an infinite interval,
which will produce a new limit point; this thus corresponds to a nontrivial reduction of C, and so reduced
chains must also be SMCs.
58                                                                      The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


     We now prove a series of lemmas about reduction, culminating in being able to apply Zorn’s lemma
to conclude that there is a reduced candidate chain.
     The first lemma shows that reduction meshes well with alternating-maximality.

Lemma 2.4.13. Let C ∈ C(P ) be a candidate chain, and D ⊆ P be a maximal chain with C ⊵ D. Then
D is also alternating maximal, and so D ∈ C(P ).

                                   ◦                                    ◦
Proof. We extend the definition of ∼ C to limit points by saying that X ∼C Y holds if and only if it holds

for a cofinal/coinitial sequence of points tending to X and Y . Note that, in the setting of a vacillating
poset, the only atomic chains are (order-isomorphic to) ω and ω ∗ . Thus for an atomic chain to be a
candidate chain, we would know that P itself is OWF, and the lemma becomes trivial. Thus we may
assume that we are not in the atomic case, and let f : N (C) → N (D) witness the reduction from C to
D.
     The key to the proof the the lemma is the claim that, for X, Y ∈ N (C) with X < Y , we have

                                ◦                          ◦
                               X∼ C Y if and only if f (X) ∼D f (Y ).                              (2.4.2)

                                               ◦                  ◦
     First assume for contradiction that f (X) ∼ D f (Y ), but X ̸∼C Y . Note that, if we assume that

(X, Y ) is OWF, then the other two conditions of Definition 2.4.3 follow immediately, as X and Y will
be the elements of N (C) that we seek. Thus assume for contradiction that (X, Y ) is not OWF. There
are thus U, V ∈ N (C) such that U ∈ N+ (C) and V ∈ N− (C). But then, as f is order-preserving and
injective, f (X) ≤ f (U ) < f (V ) ≤ f (Y ) with f (U ) ∈ N+ (D) and f (V ) ∈ N− (D), contradicting
      ◦
f (X) ∼ D f (Y ).

                                         ◦                ◦
     Now assume for contradiction that X ∼ C Y but f (X) ̸∼D f (Y ). It is for this point that we will

need to use our assumption that C is alternating maximal, and construct from our assumption a nontrivial
alternating replacement on C. Similarly to the previous case, the only nontrivial situation is when we
assume for contradiction that (f (X), f (Y )) is not OWF.
     Let S ∈ R(C) contain both X and Y , and let I ⊆ D be a minimal interval containing all of f [S].
We then claim that the set


                                           E := (C \ S) ∪ I


is a chain, and that the function g : R(C) → I(R(E)) sending S to R(I) and all other T ∈ R(C) to
                                                           ◦
{T } is an alternating replacement on C. Indeed, as f (X) ̸∼ D f (Y ), we know that |R(I)| > 1, and

so the only nontrivial step is showing that E is indeed a chain, as then all of Definition 2.4.6 follows
immediately.
     As f is order-preserving, it sends elements of N (C) above S to elements of N (D) above I. Consider
2.4. Proof of Theorem 1.1.14                                                                                59


Z := sup(N (S)), which exists as N (S) is complete. Either Z ∈ N+ (C) or Z ∈ N− (C). We deal with
these two cases separately.
    If Z ∈ N+ (C), then we know that f (Z) ∈ N+ (D) is cofinally below Z, as required for E to be a
chain in this case.
    If Z ∈ N− (C), then, as S ∈ R(C), there must be an illfounded limit point Q ∈ N− (C) with Q ⋗ S
in N (C). But then we know from Lemma 2.4.8 that f (Q) ≶ Q, and so f (Z) is entirely below Q, again
as required for E to be a chain.
    Thus E is indeed a chain and (2.4.2) is proved. Note that we can moreover deduce from the fact that
E is a chain that the following holds for any X, Y ∈ N (C) with X < Y .

                                    ◦
                              If X ̸∼ C Y then X < f (Y ) and f (X) < Y.                               (2.4.3)


    Note that (2.4.2) and (2.4.3) together tell us that a reduction witness function f satisfies all the crite-
ria of an alternating replacement apart from possibly item (v). These properties are all conserved under
composition, and so we can see that the composition of a reduction witness function with a nontriv-
ial alternating replacement witness function is itself a nontrivial alternating reduction witness function.
Thus a reduction of an alternating maximal chain is itself alternating maximal, completing the proof of
Lemma 2.4.13.


    We now collect some previous results in the following remark.

Remark 2.4.14. Recall that we know from Lemmas 2.2.18 and 2.2.22 that ⊵ is a partial order on H(P ),
and thus by Lemma 2.4.13, ⊵ is a partial order on the set C(P ) of candidate chains.

    Next, we prove a small technical lemma concerning the structure of OWF intervals in candidate
chains.

Lemma 2.4.15. Let C ∈ C(P ) be a candidate chain, and I ⊆ C be an OWF interval arising as an
         ◦
infinite ∼ C -equivalence class. Then Conv(I) ⊆ P is an OWF poset.


Proof. Assume for contradiction that there was such an interval I, points x, y ∈ I, and elements U, V ∈
N (P ) such that x < U < V < y, U ∈ N+ (P ), and V ∈ N− (P ). Let Q be the poset Conv(I), and let
D′ be a maximal chain of Q through x, U , V , and y which agrees with I outside of the interval (x, y).
Define


                                            D := (C \ I) ∪ D′ .


We claim that D can be attained from C by an alternating replacement, contradicting the alternating
maximality of C.
60                                                                          The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


                             ◦
     Indeed, we know that x ̸∼ D y due to the existence of U and V between them, and note that I ∈

R(C). We may thus define a function f : R(C) → R(D) by sending T to {T } for every T ∈ R(C) \
{I}, and sending I to R(D′ ), which is an interval of R(D). This immediately satisfies the definitions
required for an alternating replacement, giving rise to a contradiction, as required.

                                                                             ◦
     In particular, if P is vacillating, then for any maximal chain C, every ∼ C -equivalence class is OWF,
                              ◦
and so the convex hull of any ∼ C -equivalence class of a candidate chain in P is OWF.

     We now work towards our application of Zorn’s lemma, by showing that any infinite ⊵-decreasing
sequence of candidate chains has a lower bound.

Lemma 2.4.16. Let P be a countable scattered vacillating FAC poset, and let the countable sequence
C1 , C2 , C3 , . . . consist of candidate chains, with Ci ⊵ Ci+1 for all i. Then there is a candidate chain
C ∈ C(P ) such that Cn ⊵ C for all n. Moreover, there is no uncountable ⊵-chain of candidate chains.

Proof. We will let C be an arbitrary candidate chain extending the limit D := limn→∞ Cn (that is, all
elements which are present in Cn for all sufficiently large n). Note that it is not even clear a priori that
D is non-empty. The key claim in this proof is that, if X ∈ N 0 (C1 ) and the reduction Ci ⊵ Ci+1 is
witnessed by fi , then the sequence


                                X, f1 (X), f2 (f1 (X)), f3 (f2 (f1 (X))), . . .                      (2.4.4)


is eventually constant. Indeed, assume for contradiction that this sequence is not eventually constant, and
that, without loss of generality, X ∈ N+0 (P ).
     Let X0 := X. We now define Xn ∈ N+0 (P ) inductively. First define Yn := fn (Xn−1 ), and note that
Xn−1 ⊵ Yn . If Yn ∈ N+0 (P ), then set Xn = Yn and continue. Otherwise, we know by definition that
Xn−1 is cofinally above Yn , so we may pick a point x ∈ Cn with x < Yn and x < Xn−1 . Then we may
apply Lemma 2.2.14 to (x, Yn ) ∩ Cn to find Xn ∈ N+0 (Cn ) ∩ (x, Yn ), and note that Xn−1 > Xn , so in
particular, Xn−1 ⊵ Xn .
     We may then apply transitivity of ⊵ to (possibly passing to a subsequence) assume that Xn ̸= Xn−1
                                               ◦
for all n. Note that, as P is vacillating, any ∼-equivalence class is OWF, and so by Lemma 2.4.15, it
suffices to consider the case in which P is OWF. Thus, as P is vacillating, we may assume that N+ (P )
is wellfounded. Then the sequence (Xn : n ≤ ω) is an infinite non-increasing sequence of distinct
elements of a wellfounded poset.
     However, as will be shown in Example 2.5.3, N+0 (P ) need not be an FAC poset, so we cannot
immediately deduce a contradiction. Instead, we recall Lemma 2.2.26 to see that in fact, this ⊵-chain
of atomic chains must strictly decrease (in the domination order) infinitely often, and so we can restrict
to a strictly decreasing subsequence, which contradicts the fact that N+ (P ) is wellfounded. Thus the
sequence in (2.4.4) is eventually constant, and for some n, Xn = Xn+1 = Xn+2 = · · · .
2.4. Proof of Theorem 1.1.14                                                                             61


    Let C be an arbitrary candidate chain extending D, and define the function f : N (C1 ) → N (C) by
                    
                                                                   if X ∈ N 0 (C1 ),
                    
                    
                    
                    
                     limn→∞ {X, f1 (X), f2 (f1 (X)), . . .}
                    
            f (X) := sup{f (Y ) : Y ∈ N+0 (C1 ), Y < X}            if X ∈ N+ (C1 ) \ N+0 (C1 ),
                    
                    
                    
                     inf{f (Y ) : Y ∈ N−0 (C1 ), Y > X}           if X ∈ N− (C1 ) \ N−0 (C1 ).
                    
                    


We claim that f witnesses that C1 ⊵ C. Note that, as P is OWF by assumption, we do not need to worry
about complex limit points, such as increasing limits of decreasing chains, and so the above definition
suffices.
    Our previous work implies that f (X) exists for all X ∈ N 0 (C1 ), as the sequence in the definition
stabilises after finitely many steps. This in particular also implies that f is injective, order-preserving,
and X ⊵ f (X). Moreover, f is by definition continuous, and so meets all criteria in Definition 2.2.15,
and thus C1 ⊵ C. The case for general Cn ⊵ C is entirely similar, and so it remains to show that there is
no uncountable chain.
    Assume for contradiction that (Cα : α < λ) is a chain of distinct candidate chains for an uncountable
ordinal λ; in particular, α < β implies that Cα ⊵ Cβ . Let this reduction be witnessed by fα,β . We
construct an uncountable chain in N (P ). Indeed, we know by the above argument that the image of any
point of Cα stabilises under the witnessing functions after countable time.
    Take some element X0 ∈ C0 . There is then a countable α0 and element Y0 ∈ Cα0 such that, for all
β > α0 , Y0 ∈ Cβ , Y0 = f0,α0 (X0 ), and for all γ ≥ β ≥ α0 , fβ,γ (Y0 ) = Y0 . We will continue this
process inductively to produce sequences of distinct elements Xβ and Yβ of N (P ) and an increasing
sequence of ordinals αβ such that Yβ ∈ Cαβ and Yβ is a fixed point of fγ,δ for all δ ≥ γ ≥ αβ .
    Indeed, if we have chosen Xβ , Yβ , αβ for all β < γ for countable γ, then let ζ := sup{αβ : β < γ},
a countable ordinal. As the sequence Cα is not eventually constant, there must be Xγ ∈ Cζ which is
distinct from all the Yβ for β < γ. There are thus αγ and Yγ ∈ Cαγ such that Yγ = fζ,γ (Xγ ) and, for all
ε ≥ δ ≥ αγ , we have fδ,ε (Yγ ) = Yγ .
    Note that as each Yγ is present in Cβ for sufficiently large β, they are pairwise comparable, and
thus form a chain in N (P ). Moreover, the above inductive process will not stop before it has produced
an uncountable number of these Yβ , whence N (P ) has an uncountable chain, a contradiction. Thus
Lemma 2.4.16 is proved.


Lemma 2.4.17. There is a reduced chain C in any vacillating poset P .

Proof. We know from Remark 2.4.14 that ⊵ is a partial order on the set C(P ) of candidate chains, and
from Lemma 2.4.16 that any chain of reductions has a lower bound. Thus we may apply Zorn’s lemma
to ⊵, and produce a candidate chain minimal with respect to ⊵, i.e. a reduced chain, as required.
62                                                                         The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


2.4.3    Thick chains

We now deduce Theorem 1.1.14 by means of a new structure, which we call a thick chain.

Definition 2.4.18. A thick chain in a poset P is a subset F ⊆ P such that every element x ∈ F is
incomparable to only finitely many elements of F .

     Note that a chain is in particular a thick chain. We will consider thick chains which also satisfy the
following property.

Definition 2.4.19. A thick chain F is called a strong thick chain if it satisfies the following property.


                ∀y ∈ P \ F, {x ∈ F : (x ⊥ y) ∧ (∀z ∈ F )(z ∥ y =⇒ z ∥ x)} ≥ ℵ0                        (2.4.5)


     Intuitively, condition (2.4.5) in the context of FAC posets means that elements outside of F not only
have infinite incomparability to F , but also to arbitrary antichain partitions of F .
     The vital result about strong thick chains is that a strongly maximal chain in a strong thick chain
satisfies the conditions of Conjecture 1.1.1. We first show that we can in fact find these structures.
     Working in a vacillating poset, we will take a reduced chain C, as guaranteed by Section 2.4.2, and in
particular Lemma 2.4.17, and define F to be the set of all elements of P incomparable with only finitely
many elements of C. It will turn out that the fact that C is a reduced chain is sufficient for F to be a
strong thick chain. This is proved by the following two lemmas.


Lemma 2.4.20. Let C be a reduced chain in a vacillating scattered FAC poset P , and let F be defined
as
                                 F := C ∪ {x ∈ P : (⊥ x) ∩ C is finite}.

Then every element of F is incomparable with only finitely many other elements of F , i.e. F is a thick
chain.


Proof. Take an arbitrary x ∈ F , and assume for contradiction that x is incomparable with an infinite
set X of elements of F . As P is an FAC poset, there is thus an infinite chain in X. Assume that X is
increasing; the decreasing case is similar. We thus have elements y1 ⋖y2 ⋖y3 ⋖· · · of F all incomparable
to x. Each yi is incomparable to only finitely many elements of C by definition; we thus define


                                Ji := (⊥ yi ) ∩ C     and    I := (⊥ x) ∩ C.


     Note that the maximal elements of the intervals Ji are non-decreasing, and note further that there
cannot be an element x separating Ji from I.
2.4. Proof of Theorem 1.1.14                                                                                 63

               S
    Let J :=       i<ω Ji , and let K be a contiguous chain satisfying


                                  {yi : i < ω} ⊆ K ⊆ Conv({yi : i < ω}).


    If J is finite, then we can perform a nontrivial reduction by removing J from C and inserting K, as
J is finite and K is infinite, contradicting our assumption that C is reduced. Thus we may assume that J
is infinite, and so contains a contiguous copy of either ω or ω ∗ .
    In the first case, when J contains ω, let J ′ be this copy of ω. Define for each i the point zi ∈ J ′ to be
the minimal point above Ji , so yi < zi ; note that such a point must exist as Ji is finite. As I is finite, for
sufficiently large k, we will have zi > max(I) for all i > k, and thus zi > x. Then for all i, j > k, we
have yi ̸> zj as otherwise we would have yi > zj > x, contradicting yi ⊥ x. But now we see that we
can perform a nontrivial reduction to C, removing J and inserting K, contradicting that C is reduced.
    In the second case, we may assume that J does not contain ω, and does contain ω ∗ . We may then
define the point zi ∈ C to be the maximal point below Ji , so yi > zi for all i. In particular, y1 > z1 , and
so K > z1 . Furthermore, as J does not contain ω, there is a point w ∈ C with w = max(J) and for
which the interval (z1 , w) is finite. But then we can perform a nontrivial reduction by removing (z1 , w)
and inserting K, again contradicting that C is reduced.
    Thus in all cases we have reached a contradiction, and so the lemma is proved.

Lemma 2.4.21. The thick chain F , as defined in Lemma 2.4.20, satisfies property (2.4.5), i.e. F is a
strong thick chain.

Proof. Assume for contradiction that there is a point y ∈ P \ F such that the set


                           S := {x ∈ F : (x ⊥ y) ∧ (∀z ∈ F )(z ∥ y =⇒ z ∥ x)}


is finite.
    By definition of F , y is incomparable with an infinite segment of C; assume that there is a contiguous
copy of ω in this segment; the case of ω ∗ is similar. Let I be this copy of ω. As the set S is finite, we can
remove some finite initial segment of I, and label the points above this segment as x1 < x2 < x3 < · · · ,
and note that none of these points are in S. Thus, by definition of S, we find that for each i < ω there is
a point zi ∈ F such that zi ∥ y and zi ⊥ xi .
    Now, zi is incomparable with only finitely many elements of I, and so there must be an integer ni > i
such that zi < xni . Thus we see that zi ∥ y implies zi < y (as y ⊥ xni ), and so there are no i and j so
that zi > xj . But then we could perform a reduction by removing I from C and inserting a contiguous
chain containing {zi : i < ω}. This contradicts that C is reduced, and so the lemma is proved.

    Given the above two lemmas, we may now apply Theorem 1.1.7 of Zaguia [121]; we first restate this
64                                                                         The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


theorem in our terminology.

Theorem 2.4.22. If P is a thick chain, then P has a spine

     We now show that, given any spine of F and corresponding partition of F into antichains, we can
extend it into a partition of P into antichains with the same spine. This is where the condition (2.4.5)
of being a strong thick chain comes in. Indeed, condition (2.4.5) can be read as saying that every point
y of P \ F has infinitely many elements x ∈ F such that any antichain A ⊆ F containing x is entirely
incomparable to y, and so y can be added to this antichain.

Lemma 2.4.23. Let F be a strong thick chain in a countable FAC poset P , let C be a maximal chain
               S
in F , and F = x∈C Ax a partition of F into antichains, with x ∈ Ax for all x ∈ C. Then there is a
              S
partition P = x∈C Bx of P into antichains, with Ax ⊆ Bx for all x ∈ C.

Proof. Enumerate the countable set P \ F as y1 , y2 , y3 , . . . ; we will assign each yi to some x ∈ C in
turn, and set Bx = Ax ∪ {yi }. We will furthermore guarantee that each x ∈ C has at most one yi
assigned to it; in other words, |Bx \ Ax | ≤ 1. Assume that y1 , y2 , . . . , yn−1 have been assigned, and we
seek to assign yn .
     Condition (2.4.5) gives us an infinite set S ⊆ F such that, for all x ∈ S, x ⊥ yn , and for all z ∈ F , if
z ∥ yn , then z ∥ x. But now note that if x ∈ S and A is an antichain with x ∈ A, then A ⊆ F is entirely
incomparable to y: if z ∈ A has z ∥ yn , then z ∥ x, contradicting A being an antichain.
     As the antichains Ax partition F , there is thus an infinite set X ⊆ C such that y is entirely incompa-
rable to Ax for every x ∈ X. As only finitely many yi have been assigned to antichains so far, there are
still infinitely many choices of x ∈ C such that no yi has been assigned to Ax . We may thus pick some
such x arbitrarily and set Bx = Ax ∪ {yn }.
     Continuing this process inductively, and setting Bx = Ax if x is never used, we see that we have thus
produced a partition of P into antichains Bx with Ax ⊆ Bx , as required.

     Finally, we deduce Theorem 1.1.14.

Proof of Theorem 1.1.14. Our first task is to reduce from the case of a vacillating FAC poset P to a
scattered such poset. Indeed, take P to be a vacillating FAC poset which admits an embedding of the
rationals, and let D ⊆ P be an η-SMC, as guaranteed by Lemma 2.3.6, and identify D with Q. Following
the argument from Section 2.3, we can thus produce a family Dr of (not necessarily nonempty) scattered
posets, indexed by r ∈ R, with q ∈ Dq for each q ∈ D.
     Note that, for any maximal chain C which intersects a dense subset of {Dr : r ∈ R}, and any r, the
inf of all elements of H(C) above Dr and the sup of all elements of H(C) below Dr are both illfounded
                                                                                              S
limits by Lemma 2.4.8. We therefore see that, if we start with an arbitrary maximal chain C in r∈R Dr
and apply reductions in each Dr to produce a chain C ′ such that C ′ ∩ Dr is a reduced chain for all r ∈ R,
2.5. Examples and counterexamples                                                                      65


then C ′ is a reduced chain in P . Thus, starting with such a C and applying Lemma 2.4.17, we find a
reduced chain C ′ ⊆ P .
    We may then apply Lemmas 2.4.20 and 2.4.21 to every C ′ ∩ Dr individually to produce a strong
                                                      S
thick chain Fr in Dr . We now claim that the union F = r∈R Fr is a strong thick chain in P .
   Indeed, as Dr and Ds are totally comparable for any r < s (as there is t ∈ Q with r < t < s, and
then all elements x ∈ Dr and y ∈ Ds satisfy x < t < y, where t ∈ Q is identified with the corresponding
element of D), it is immediate that F is a thick chain.
    To show that F is a strong thick chain, consider a point y ∈ P with y ∈  / Dr for all r. There is thus
                                                                                              S
an infinite interval I ⊆ R such that y is entirely incomparable to Dr for all r ∈ I. But then r∈I Dr is
infinite, and for any r ∈ I, x ∈ Dr , and z ∈ F with z ∥ y, we have z ∈ Ds for some s ∈  / I, whence
                S
x ∥ z, and so r∈I Dr is contained in the set in condition (2.4.5), so this condition holds. Thus F is
indeed a strong thick chain.
   We can now apply Theorem 1.1.7 to find a chain C ⊆ F and a partition A of F into antichains,
each of which meets C. Finally, we can apply Lemma 2.4.23 to extend this antichain partition of F into
an antichain partition of P , while preserving the property that every antichain meets the chain C. The
existence of this partition thus proves Theorem 1.1.14, as required.


2.5     Examples and counterexamples

In this section, we first present in Section 2.5.1 several examples which exhibit some behaviours which
appear to be instructive to understanding the proofs given in Sections 2.3 and 2.4. We then build on these
examples to produce in Section 2.5.2 a poset which contains no spine, thus providing a counterexample
to Conjecture 1.1.1.


2.5.1   Examples

The four examples presented in this subsection show that some of the assumptions and conditions of
the arguments in previous sections are necessary. Indeed, Example 2.5.1 demonstrates that strongly
maximal chains need not be spines, and Example 2.5.2 is an example of a poset with no finite cutset.
Then, Example 2.5.3 shows that, even when P is an FAC poset, N+0 (P ) may contain infinite antichains.
Finally, Example 2.5.5 shows that, for the conclusion of Lemma 2.2.26 to hold, we do require that the
chains in question are atomic.

Example 2.5.1. We define P1 to be a poset on


                                    X1 := {⊥, ⊤} ∪ (N × 2) ∪ {a}


The order ≤ on this poset is given by precisely those relations induced by the following, for all n ∈ N,
66                                                                           The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


i ∈ 2, and x ∈ X1

     • ⊥ ≤ x and x ≤ ⊤.
     • (n, i) ≤ (n + 1, i).
     • (n, 1) ≤ (n + 1, 0).
     • (n, 1) ≤ a.

     A Hasse diagram of this poset is shown in Figure 2.1.

                                                    ⊤

                                                         a

                                          •
                                          •                   •••
                                          •
                                          •                     ••
                                          •
                                          •                      •••
                                          •
                                          •
                                          •
                                          •                        ••
                                          •                         •
                                          •                         •
                                          •                         •
                                   C1     •                         •   C2
                                          •                         •

                                          •                   •

                                                    ⊥

       Figure 2.1: A Hasse diagram of P1 . The regions in grey triangles are order-isomorphic to ω.

     Now, we define maximal chains C1 and C2 as follows.


                                C1 := {⊥, ⊤} ∪ {(n, 0) : n ∈ N},

                                C2 := {⊥, ⊤} ∪ {(n, 1) : n ∈ N} ∪ {a}.


     We claim that both C1 and C2 are strongly maximal, but, while C2 is a spine in P , C1 is not a spine.
Indeed, every point of the form (n, 0) is incomparable to all points of the form (m, 1) for m ≥ n, and
so we can partition P into antichains of the form {(n, 0), (n, 1)} and {y} for all y ∈ {a} ∪ {⊥, ⊤}, and
thus show that C2 is a spine.
     It is clear to see that C1 is also strongly maximal; any finite contiguous chain in C1 is of elements
of the form (n, 0), and in this part of P , C1 is a level-chain. However, now assume for contradiction
that P could be partitioned into antichains, all meeting C1 . We see that a must be in some antichain
Z meeting C1 ; it must be the case that Z ∩ C1 = {(m, 0)} for some integer m. But now consider the
chain {(n, 1) : 0 ≤ n ≤ m}. These elements of P are all comparable to a and to all of C1 except for
{(n, 0) : 0 ≤ n ≤ m}. But, as (m, 0) is already covered, this leaves m + 1 comparable elements to be
paired up with m elements of C1 , which is impossible. Thus C1 is not a spine.
2.5. Examples and counterexamples                                                                       67


   We now produce an example of a poset P2 such that ⊵ is not a partial order. In fact, P2 also has no
finite cutset—that is, a finite set meeting every maximal chain—as we will be able to express P2 as an
infinite union of disjoint maximal chains.

Example 2.5.2. P2 is a poset on X2 := Z × 2 × N, with ordering ≤ defined to be the minimal partial
order which contains all of the following for all z ∈ Z, i ∈ 2, and n ∈ N.

    • (z, i, n) ≤ (z, i, n + 1).
    • (z, i, n) ≤ (z + 1, i, 0).
    • (z, 0, n) ≤ (z, 1, n) ≤ (z + 1, 0, n).

   A Hasse diagram of this poset is shown in Figure 2.2.




                                             ••
                                              ••
                                               ••
                                                ••
                                                 •
                                                 •            •••
                                                 •              ••
                                                                 •••
                                             •                     •
                                                                   •
                                             ••                    •
                                              ••                   •
                                               ••
                                                ••            •
                                                 •
                                                 •            ••
                                                 •             ••
                                                                ••
                                                  •              ••
                                                                  •
                                             ••                   •
                                              ••••                •
                                                 ••
                                                  •           •
                                                  •
                                                  •
                                                  •
                                             •




Figure 2.2: A Hasse diagram of P2 . The regions in grey triangles are order-isomorphic to ω, and this
structure continues infinitely in both directions.

   Define the chains C0 and C1 by Ci := {(z, i, n) : z ∈ Z, n ∈ N}. Furthermore, define for each
n ∈ N the chain Dn := {(z, i, n) : z ∈ Z, i ∈ 2}. Note that the chains Ci and Dn are all maximal, and
both the family {C0 , C1 } and the family {Dn : n ∈ N} partition P2 . Thus, even though P2 has width 2,
it has no finite cutset, as such a set would have to intersect every member of a family of disjoint maximal
chains such as {Dn : n ∈ N}.
68                                                                       The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


     Note further that P2 is not vacillating; we now show that it does not satisfy the conclusion of
Lemma 2.2.22. In particular, C0 ⊴ C1 and C1 ⊴ C0 , but we do not have C0 ≃ C1 . It is easy to see
that every point in C0 has infinite incomparability in C1 , and vice versa; (z, 0, n) is incomparable to
(z, 1, m) for all m > n. We also have the relations


                 {(z, 0, n) : n ∈ N} ⊴ {(z, 1, n) : n ∈ N} ⊴ {(z + 1, 0, n) : n ∈ N},


which show that C0 ⊴ C1 and C1 ⊴ C0 , as required. Thus the condition of vacillation is indeed essential
to Lemma 2.2.22.
     Finally, we note that C0 ⊵ C0 is witnessed by a nontrivial function f ; if we let Xz ∈ N (C0 ) be
given by the chain {(z, 0, n) : n ∈ N}, then we can set f (Xz ) = Xz−1 for all z, which can be seen to
satisfy all conditions of Definition 2.2.15. Moreover, the argument in the proof of Lemma 2.4.16 would
not apply to this function f , again showing that the assumption of vacillation is required for our proofs
as presented to go through.

     We now provide an example to show that N+0 (P ) need not be an FAC poset, even when P is well-
founded.

Example 2.5.3. P3 is a poset on N × N, with ordering ≤ defined to be the minimal partial order which
contains both of the following relations for all x, y ∈ N.

     • (x, y) ≤ (x, y + 1).
     • (x, y) ≤ (x + y + 1, y).

     It is not immediately obvious that P3 is an FAC poset, and so we provide a proof of this fact.

Claim 2.5.4. P3 has no infinite antichain.

Proof. Assume for contradiction that A ⊆ P3 is an infinite antichain. Consider the set Sy := {(x, y) :
x ∈ N}. We claim that the size of A ∩ Sy is at most y + 1.
     Indeed, if we take any y + 2 elements {(xi , y) : 0 ≤ i ≤ y + 1} of Sy , then by the pigeonhole
principle, there must be some distinct i and j such that xi − xj is a multiple of y + 1, and thus the points
(xi , y) and (xj , y) are comparable.
     Thus A would need to contain elements (x, y) with arbitrarily large values of y. Take some point
(b, c) ∈ A. It is straightforward to prove that there are b′ > b and c′ > c such that (b, c) < (x, y) for
all x ≥ b′ and y ≥ c′ . Thus there is some x0 < b′ such that there are infinitely many points of the form
(x0 , y) in A. But such points are comparable, contradicting the fact that A is an antichain. Thus P3 is
indeed an FAC poset.

     If we let Cn ⊆ P3 be defined as {(n, y) : y ∈ N}, then it is clear that for any m ̸= n, we have
Cn ̸≃ Cm , from which it is immediate that N+0 (P3 ) has an infinite antichain.
2.5. Examples and counterexamples                                                                      69


    Finally, we provide an example to show that the conclusion of Lemma 2.2.26 need not hold when the
chains in question are not atomic.

Example 2.5.5. P4 is a poset on N × N × N, with the ordering ≤ defined to be the minimal partial order
which contains all of the following relations for all x, y, z ∈ N.

    • (x, y, z) ≤ (x, y, z + 1).
    • (x, y, z) ≤ (x, y + 1, z).
    • For all x′ , z ′ ∈ N, (x, y, z) ≤ (x′ , y + 2, z ′ ).
    • (x, y, z) ≥ (x + 1, y, z).
    • For all z ′ ∈ N, (x, y, z) ≥ (x + y + 1, y, z ′ ).

Let En ⊆ P4 be the chain {(n, y, z) : y, z ∈ N}.

    The poset P4 has no infinite antichain for similar reasons to P3 . Moreover, note that each En is a
non-atomic chain, that En ⊵ En+1 for all n, but there are no n, m ∈ N for which En > Em , immediately
implying that the conclusion of Lemma 2.2.26 does not hold.


2.5.2   A counterexample to the Aharoni–Korman conjecture

We now present a counterexample to the Aharoni–Korman conjecture, Conjecture 1.1.1. Due to the
results of Section 2.4, we know that if a poset P is to be a counterexample to Conjecture 1.1.1, then P
cannot be vacillating. Indeed, our counterexample will consist of an infinite non-increasing sequence of
copies of ω × ω, along with some extra structure. We now define this counterexample.

Example 2.5.6. Let P5 be a poset defined on the set N × N × N, with the order ≤ on elements (x, y, n)
of P5 given by setting (x, y, n) ≤ (u, v, m) if and only if

    • n ≥ m + 2, or
    • n = m and x ≤ u and y ≤ v, or
    • n = m + 1 and min{x, y} + 1 ≤ min{u, v}, or
    • n = m + 1 and x + y ≤ 2(u + v).

We note without proof that the above relation is in fact a partial order. Define the set Ln ⊆ P5 to be the
nth level of P5 , defined as Ln := {(x, y, n) : x, y ∈ ω}. A Hasse diagram of the high-level structure of
P5 is shown in Figure 2.3, and two examples showing the relations between Ln and Ln+1 are shown in
Figure 2.4.

    We remark before continuing that the final condition in the above definition would work just as well
with the number 2 replaced by any real number α > 1, we merely use 2 for convenience. Moreover, this
condition is only used at the very end of the proof.
    We now prove the following proposition, which states that P5 is a counterexample to Conjecture 1.1.1.
70                                                                                        The Aharoni–Korman conjecture

Proposition 2.5.7. P5 is a countable scattered FAC poset, but there is no chain C ⊆ P5 which is a spine
of P5 .

      Before proceeding to the proof, we remark that our proof of Proposition 2.5.7 has been formally
verified using the Lean theorem prover by Bhavik Mehta, and the code is available online as part of the
mathlib4 library [100].
   We know that, due to Theorem 1.1.3, a counterexample to Conjecture 1.1.1 cannot be vacillating.
                                                                   L
Indeed, the set {(x, 0, 2n) : x, n ∈ N} ⊆ P5 is order-isomorphic to i∈ω∗ ω, and thus P5 is not
vacillating.
      We will prove Proposition 2.5.7 via a series of observations and lemmas. Fix for the rest of this
section a chain C ⊆ P5 , which we will prove is not a spine of P5 . For ease of reference, we first
enumerate a series of observations.

Observation 2.5.8. The following facts are all immediate.

     (i) For all n, Ln is an interval of P5 .
  (ii) For any n and s, the set Kn,s := {(x, y, n) : x + y = s} ⊆ Ln is an antichain of P5 .
 (iii) For any fixed n, the induced order on Ln ⊆ P5 is isomorphic to ω × ω.
 (iv) For any three points (x, y, n) ≤ (u, v, n) ≤ (w, z, n) in Ln , there is a chain D ⊆ [(x, y, n), (w, z, n)]
          containing (u, v, n) of length w + z + 1 − x − y.

Lemma 2.5.9. P5 contains no infinite antichain.

Proof. Assume for contradiction that there is some infinite antichain A ⊆ P5 . As Ln ∥ Lm whenever
|n − m| ≥ 2, we know that A can intersect at most two levels of P5 , and thus there must be some level
Ln such that A ∩ Ln is infinite. We may thus assume that in fact A ⊆ Ln for some integer n.
    However, by Observation 2.5.8 item (iii), Ln is order-isomorphic to ω × ω, and it is easy to check
that ω × ω is an FAC poset. Thus P5 has no infinite antichains, as required.

    Define a sequence m0 , m1 , . . . of integers by


                                                       mn := min{min{x, y} : (x, y, n) ∈ C}.


Lemma 2.5.10. There is some integer n ≥ 1 such that the size of the intersection |Ln ∩ C| of C with the
nth level Ln of P5 is finite.

Proof. Assume for contradiction that |Ln ∩ C| is infinite for every integer n. We claim that m1 >
m2 > · · · , which will immediately yield a contradiction. Indeed, fix some n, and let (u, v, n) ∈ C have
min{u, v} = mn . Consider Ln+1 ∩ C; this set is infinite, and so due to Observation 2.5.8 item (ii),
there must be a infinitely many points (x, y, n + 1) ∈ C such that x + y > 2(u + v). Thus to have
(x, y, n + 1) < (u, v, n), we must have mn+1 ≤ min{x, y} + 1 ≤ min{u, v} = mn . Therefore
mn+1 < mn for all n, leading to a contradiction, as required.

    Fix n as in Lemma 2.5.10 so that |Ln ∩C| is finite; we will prove that there is no function f : C ∪Ln ∪
Ln+1 → C such that f acts as the identity on C, and the preimage of any point x ∈ C is an antichain.
Note that the existence of such a function is equivalent to the existence of a partition into antichains (the
72                                                                           The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


sets in the partition are simply the preimages of f ), and that if there does not exist such a function for
C ∪ Ln ∪ Ln+1 , then there certainly cannot exist such a function for the whole of P5 .

     Assume for contradiction that some such function does exist, and thus fix for the rest of this section
a function f : C ∪ Ln ∪ Ln+1 → C which acts as the identity on C and for which every preimage is an
antichain of P5 .

     We first show that there are infinitely many points in Ln which f does not send to C ∩Ln or C ∩Ln+1 .
Indeed, define R ⊆ Ln to be those elements which are greater than all elements of C ∩ Ln :


                                    R := {x ∈ Ln : x > max(C ∩ Ln )}.


As C ∩ Ln is finite due to Lemma 2.5.10, we know that there is some integer a such that {(x, y, n) : x ≥
a ∧ y ≥ a} ⊆ R. As R is entirely comparable with C ∩ Ln by definition, f cannot send any element of
R to C ∩ Ln , and so must send these elements to C ∩ Ln−1 or C ∩ Ln+1 .

     If C ∩ Ln+1 is non-empty, then, noting that Ln+1 is well-founded, and so any chain it contains is
well-ordered, set (x0 , y0 , n + 1) = min(C ∩ Ln+1 ), note that mn+1 = min{x0 , y0 }, and define a set
S ⊆ R as follows.
                    
                     {(x, y, n) ∈ R : (x, y, n) ∥ C ∩ Ln+1 }
                    
                                                                              if C ∩ Ln+1 is finite,
            S :=
                     {(x, y, n) ∈ R : min{x, y} ≥ max{x0 , y0 } + 1}
                    
                                                                              otherwise.


     Note that S is always infinite, and that if (x0 , y0 , n + 1) does not exist, then we are in the first of the
above cases, so do not attempt to make use of its value. Moreover, as was also the case for R, note that
there is some integer b such that {(x, y, n) : x ≥ b ∧ y ≥ b} ⊆ S. We now prove that f cannot send any
element of S to C ∩ Ln+1 .


Lemma 2.5.11. If p = (x, y, n) ∈ S, then f (p) ∈
                                               / C ∩ Ln+1 .


Proof. If C ∩ Ln+1 is finite, then the result is immediate as S is entirely comparable to C ∩ Ln+1 by
definition. We may thus assume that C ∩ Ln+1 is infinite. Define the chains


                D0 := {(x, y0 , n + 1) : x ≥ x0 }      and   D1 := {(x0 , y, n + 1) : y ≥ y0 }.


Note that either C ∩ Ln+1 ⊵ D0 or C ∩ Ln+1 ⊵ D1 (or both); we may assume without loss of generality
(by exchanging the roles of x and y) that the former holds.

     Recall the definition of Kn,s from Observation 2.5.8 item (ii). We now claim that |C ∩ Kn+1,s | = 1
for every s ≥ x0 + y0 . Firstly, we know from Observation 2.5.8 that Kn+1,s is an antichain, so certainly
2.5. Examples and counterexamples                                                                            73


|C ∩ Kn+1,s | ≤ 1. If there is some s1 > x0 + y0 such that C ∩ Kn+1,s1 = ∅, then let


                        (x1 , y1 , n + 1) := min{(x, y, n + 1) ∈ C : x + y > s1 }.


We thus know that the set [(x0 , y0 , n+1), (x1 , y1 , n+1)]∩C has size at most x1 +y1 −x0 −y0 . However,
as x1 ≥ x0 and y1 ≥ y0 (as (x1 , y1 , n + 1) ≥ (x0 , y0 , n + 1) in P5 ), Observation 2.5.8 item (iv) tells us
that there is a chain of length x1 +y1 +1−x0 −y0 contained in the interval [(x0 , y0 , n+1), (x1 , y1 , n+1)]
of Ln+1 . Thus we can replace a finite contiguous sub-chain of C with a longer chain, and so C is not
a strongly maximal chain. However, we know from Observation 2.1.1 that a spine must in particular
be a strongly maximal chain, and so this contradicts C being a spine. Thus |C ∩ Kn+1,s | = 1 for all
s ≥ x0 + y0 .
    Let s0 := x0 + y0 , and for s ≥ s0 , let ps be the unique element of C ∩ Kn+1,s . We now claim that


                                  ∀i ≥ 0, f ((x0 + i, y0 , n + 1)) = ps0 +i .                           (2.5.1)


    In fact, we will prove the following stronger statement, from which (2.5.1) follows immediately.


                      ∀s ≥ s0 ∀ℓ ≥ s − s0 , f is constant on Kn+1,s ∩ [ps0 , ps0 +ℓ ].                  (2.5.2)


Intuitively, (2.5.2) says that f is “constant on the rows” of a suitably defined region of Ln+1 .
    Indeed, if we let Bℓ := [ps0 , ps0 +ℓ ], then it suffices to prove that if i, j ≥ 0 are such that the points
q := (x0 + i + 1, y0 + j, n + 1) and q ′ := (x0 + i, y0 + j + 1, n + 1) are both in Bℓ , then we have that
f (q) = f (q ′ ).
    Note that q− := (x0 + i, y0 + j, n + 1) and q+ := (x0 + i + 1, y0 + j + 1, n + 1) are both in Bℓ . Thus,
applying Observation 2.5.8 item (iv), we see that there are chains E and E ′ in Bℓ , both containing ps0 ,
q− , q+ , and ps0 +ℓ , with |E| = |E ′ | = ℓ + 1, and differing only in that E \ E ′ = {q} and E ′ \ E = {q ′ }.
    As f must send all of Bℓ to C∩Bℓ (as Bℓ is entirely comparable to the rest of C), and |C∩Bℓ | = ℓ+1,
we see that f must act as a bijection when restricted to either E or E ′ ; we may thus conclude that
f (q) = f (q ′ ), as required. Hence (2.5.2)—and thus also (2.5.1)—holds.
    Consider a point p = (x, y, n) ∈ S, as in the statement of Lemma 2.5.11. As p ∈ S, we know that
min{x, y} ≥ max{x0 , y0 } + 1, and so, for any integer i ≥ 0, p ≥ (x0 + i, y0 , n + 1). But then (2.5.1)
implies that p is comparable with some element of f −1 (ps0 +i ) for every i ≥ 0, and so f (p) ∈
                                                                                               / C ∩ Ln+1 ,
as required.



    The only remaining possibility is that f sends S to C ∩ Ln−1 . However, (x, y, n) ∈ S is comparable
to all points (u, v, n − 1) ∈ Ln−1 with 2(u + v) ≥ x + y. Fix some integer a ≥ 1 such that the point
74                                                                           The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


(a, a, n) ∈ S, and consider the chain


                       F := {(a, a, n), (a + 1, a, n), . . . , (3a − 1, a, n), (3a, a, n)}.


The chain F has length 2a + 1, and is entirely comparable to all points of the form (u, v, n − 1) with
u + v ≥ 2a. Thus f must send the chain F to the intersection of C with the set


                 T := {q ∈ Ln−1 : q ̸≥ (3a, a, b)} = {(u, v, n − 1) : u + v ≤ 2a − 1}.


However, the longest chain in T has length 2a, and so f cannot injectively map F into C ∩ T , a con-
tradiction. Therefore the function f cannot exist, and so P5 has no spine, completing the proof of
Proposition 2.5.7.


2.6     Concluding remarks and open problems

In this chapter, we have both provided a counterexample to the Aharoni–Korman conjecture while also
proving that the conjecture holds in a wide variety of cases. However, the work we have done does
not totally answer every question concerning this conjecture, as there are still non-vacillating posets for
which the conjecture holds (non-vacillating posets which are also chains are perhaps the simplest such
example). For example, the counterexample presented in Example 2.5.6 has width ℵ0 , and it does not
seem easy to adjust the example to have finite width. For this reason, we are hopeful that the answer to
the following question might be positive.

Question 2.6.1. Let P be a poset of some finite width w. Must P have a spine?

     We note that it is known (and was proved by Aharoni and Korman themselves [9]) that every poset of
width 2 has a spine. However, to the best of the author’s knowledge, Question 2.6.1 remains open even
for countable posets of width 3.
     In a different direction, the proofs presented here were in places highly sensitive to the cardinality of
P , and so we ask whether larger cardinalities present a genuine issue or not.

Question 2.6.2. Let P be a vacillating FAC poset of cardinality κ. For which κ must P have a spine?

     The case wherein κ < ℵω would seem to be particularly approachable.
     Noting that a spine must also be a SMC, we recall Conjecture 1.1.10, which states that every FAC
poset P contains a SMC. Following the line of investigation of Aharoni and Berger [7], who considered
strongly maximal antichains, we ask the following question.

Question 2.6.3. Is there a set of conditions which can be placed on a (countable) poset P , weaker than
being an FAC poset, which imply that P has a strongly maximal chain?
2.7. Summary of terminology and notation                                                                   75


    One may also ask about extending the work in Section 2.3 to posets which are not countable. While
there appear to be further technical difficulties in extending the work to non-scattered posets of cardinality
at least ℵω , the author does not believe these to be insurmountable, and that the following conjecture
should be amenable to an induction on the cardinality.

Conjecture 2.6.4. Let P be an FAC poset of cardinality ℵn for some n < ω. Then P has a strongly
maximal chain.

    It is then of course natural to ask about larger cardinalities as well, but the situation appears to be
more difficult for limit cardinals, where an inductive proof necessarily becomes more involved. In the
scattered case however, there seems to be more hope, and so we also make the following conjecture.

Conjecture 2.6.5. Let P be a scattered FAC poset. Then P has a strongly maximal chain.

    The intersection of 2.6.4 and Conjecture 2.6.5, namely finding an SMC in a scattered FAC poset of
cardinality less than ℵω would appear to be a particularly appealing avenue for further research.


2.7      Summary of terminology and notation

In order to aid the reader of this chapter, we include here a list of the terminology and notation used in
this chapter, given in the order in which they are introduced.


Basic poset terminology

These terms are mostly standard (with some exceptions).

      • A partially ordered set or poset P is a pair (X, ≤) consisting of a set X of elements, and a
        reflexive, antisymmetric, transitive relation ≤ on X.
      • (∥) Elements x and y of P are comparable, written x ∥ y, if x ≤ y or y ≤ x.
      • (⊥) Elements x and y of P are incomparable, written x ⊥ y, if they are not comparable.
      • A chain C in a poset P is a subset of the elements of P such that every pair of elements of C are
        comparable.
      • An antichain A in a poset P is a subset of the elements of P such that every pair of elements of
        A are incomparable.
      • A chain C of P is contiguous if there is no x ∈ P \ C and y, z ∈ C with y < x < z and C ∪ {x}
        a chain.
      • The width of a poset P is the supremum over all sizes of antichains in P .
      • A poset P satisfies the finite antichain condition, or is an FAC poset if all antichains of P are
        finite.
76                                                                         The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


     • The linear sum or lexicographic sum of a collection (Pi : i ∈ I) of posets indexed by a total
                       L                           F
       order I, written i∈I Pi , is the ordering on i∈I Pi wherein all elements of Pj are set to be greater
       than all elements of Pi whenever j > i in I.
     • (P ∗ ) Given a poset P with order ≤, the reverse ordering P ∗ is the ordering ≤∗ on the same set
       with x ≤∗ y if and only if y ≤ x.
     • Given two chains C, D ⊆ P , say that C is cofinal in D if for all y ∈ D there is x ∈ C with y ≤ x.
       If instead we can always find x ∈ C with x ≤ y, then C is coinitial in D.
     • Given two chains C, D ⊆ P , say that C is a final segment of D if C is contiguous in D and
       whenever we take x, y ∈ D with x ∈ C and x ≤ y, then y ∈ C. If conversely x, y ∈ D with
       x ∈ C and y ≤ x implies y ∈ C, then C is an initial segment of D.
     • The up-set of an element x ∈ P is the set (≥ x) := {y ∈ P : y ≥ x}. The down-set (≤ x),
       along with (> x) and (< x) are defined similarly.
     • The set of comparable elements to an x ∈ P is (∥ x) := {y ∈ P : y ∥ x}. The set of
       incomparable elements to x, (⊥ x), is defined similarly.
     • A poset P is scattered if it does not contain the poset Q as a sub-order.
     • Given elements x, y ∈ P , say that x covers y, and write x ⋗ y, if x > y and there is no z ∈ P
       with x > z > y.
     • (ω) The order type of the naturals N is written ω.
     • (ζ) The order type of the integers Z is written ζ.
     • (η) The order type of the rationals Q is written η.
     • A poset is wellfounded if it has no infinite decreasing sequence, and is co-wellfounded if it has
       no infinite increasing sequence.
     • (×) Given two posets P and Q with orders ≤P and ≤Q respectively, the Cartesian product poset
       P × Q is is the poset on the Cartesian product set P × Q, with order ≤ given by (p, q) ≤ (p′ , q ′ )
       if and only if p ≤P p′ and q ≤Q q ′ .
     • (×lex ) The lexicographic product P ×lex Q is also a poset on the Cartesian product set P × Q,
       except now the ordering ≤lex is given by (p, q) ≤lex (p′ , q ′ ) if and only if p <P p′ , or p = p′ and
       q ≤Q q ′ .
     • The incomparability graph of a poset P is the graph G with vertex set equal to the set of elements
       of P , and having an edge between x and y if and only if x ⊥ y in P .
     • A poset P is vacillating if there is no contiguous chain C of P which can be written as a linear
           L
       sum n∈ω Cn where each Cn is infinite and co-wellfounded.
2.7. Summary of terminology and notation                                                                77


Intervals

    • A subset X ⊆ P is said to be convex, or an interval of P if for all x, y ∈ X and z ∈ P , if
      x ≤ z ≤ y, then z ∈ X.
    • The set of intervals of a poset P is denoted by I(P ).
    • An interval I ∈ I(P ) is said to be proper if there are x, y ∈ P \ I with x < I < y.
    • The open interval (x, y) is the set {z ∈ P : x < z < y}.
    • The closed interval [x, y] is the set {z ∈ P : x ≤ z ≤ y}.
    • The convex hull of a set X ⊆ P is the interval Conv(X) := {x ∈ P : (∃y, z ∈ X) y ≤ x ≤ z}.
    • The wide interval ⟨x, y⟩ is the set {z ∈ P : ∀w ∈ P (w > y =⇒ w > z) ∧ (w < x =⇒ w <
      z)}. The wide interval of a set X ⊆ P is defined to be {z ∈ P : ∀w ∈ P (w > X =⇒ w >
      z) ∧ (w < X =⇒ w < z)}.


Chains and chain completions

This covers the first half of the material introduced in Section 2.2, and is largely non-standard. Through-
out, C and D are chains in a poset P .

    • C is a spine of P if there is a partition (Ai : i ∈ I) of P into antichains such that C meets every
      Ai .
    • C and D have mutually finite incomparability if they are both maximal chains in the poset
      Conv(C ∪ D) ⊆ P , and for all x ∈ C, the set {y ∈ D : x ⊥ y} is finite, and for all y ∈ D, the
      set {x ∈ C : x ⊥ y} is finite.
    • C is a maximal chain if it is a maximal element of the set of chains of P ordered by inclusion.
    • (≃− , ≃+ ) Maximal chains C and D are equivalent as increasing chains, written C ≃+ D if
      C and D have equal maximal elements, or neither has a maximal element and there are final
      segments C ′ ⊆ C and D′ ⊆ D such that C ′ and D′ have mutually finite incomparability. They are
      equivalent as decreasing chains, written C ≃− D if they have equal minimal elements or initial
      segments with mutually finite incomparability.
    • (∼C ) For elements x, y ∈ C, write x ∼C y if there are only finitely many elements of C between
      x and y, i.e. (x, y) ∩ C is finite.
    • ([x]C ) Denote the ∼C -equivalence class of an element x ∈ C by [x]C .
    • (F (P )) The set of all contiguous chains of P is denoted F (P ).
    • (G− (P ), G+ (P )) The set of decreasing chains of P , denoted by G− (P ), is defined as F (P )/ ≃− .
      The set of increasing chains, G+ (P ), is similarly F (P )/ ≃+ .
    • (≃prin ) For X ∈ G+ (P ) and Y ∈ G− (P ), write X ≃prin Y if there is some x ∈ P such that
      {x} ∈ X and {x} ∈ Y .
    • (G(P ), H(P )) Define G(P ) := G− (P ) ∪ G+ (P ) and H(P ) := G(P )/ ≃prin .
78                                                                        The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


     • (N (P )) The set of nonprincipal elements of H(P ), i.e. those equivalence classes of chains in
       G+ (P ) with no maximal element, or in G− (P ) with no minimal element, is denoted N (P ).
     • (N− (P ), N+ (P )) Write N+ (P ) and N− (P ) for the nonprincipal increasing and decreasing chains
       in N (P ) respectively.


Domination and cofinal comparability

This covers the second half of the material introduced in Section 2.2, and is non-standard.

     • (≥ on N (P )) For C, D ∈ N+ (P ), say that C dominates D and write C ≥ D if there is x ∈ C
       such that for all y ∈ D, we have x ≥ y, i.e. there are final segments C ′ ⊆ C and D′ ⊆ D such
       that C ′ ≥ D′ . Generalise to arbitrary C, D ∈ N (P ) similarly.
     • An element X ∈ N+ (P ) is an accumulation point of N (P ) if for any Y ∈ N (P ) there is
       Z ∈ N (P ) with Y < Z < X. Accumulation points in N− (P ) are defined similarly.
     • An element of N (P ) which is not an accumulation point is called an isolated point.
     • (N 0 (P )) Denote by N+0 (P ) the set of ≃-equivalence classes of increasing chains which do not
       admit an order-preserving injection from the poset ω + 1, and define N−0 (P ) similarly. Then
       N 0 (P ) := N−0 (P ) ∪ N+0 (P ).
     • (⊵ on N 0 (P )) For C, D ∈ N+0 (P ), say that C reduces to D and write C ⊵ D if ∀y ∈ D ∃x ∈
       C (x > y). For C, D ∈ N−0 (P ), write C ⊵ D if ∀y ∈ D ∃x ∈ C (x < y).
     • (⊵ on N (P )) For non-atomic C, D ∈ N+ (P ), say that C reduces to D and write C ⊵ D if
       there is function f : N (C) → N (D) (say f witnesses the reduction) satisfying the following five
       properties.

            – f is order-preserving,
            – f is injective,
            – f is continuous (i.e. commutes with sup and inf),
            – if C ∈ N+ (P ) then f has cofinal image in N (D), otherwise f has coinitial image in N (D),
              and
            – for all ε ∈ {−, +} and X ∈ Nε0 (C), we have f (X) ∈ Nε (D) and X ⊵ f (X).

     • (≶) Chains C, D ∈ G+ (P ) are said to be bicomparable and write C ≶ D if ∀x ∈ C ∃y ∈
       D (x < y) and ∀y ∈ D ∃x ∈ C (y < x).
     • C and D are strongly bicomparable if C ⊵ D and D ⊵ C.


Strongly maximal chains

This section covers the terminology discussed in Section 2.3. Throughout, C and D are chains in the
poset P .
2.7. Summary of terminology and notation                                                               79


    • C is a strongly maximal chain or SMC if it is a chain of P , and for every chain D, |C \ D| ≥
      |D \ C|.
    • C is an ℵ0 -strongly maximal chain or ℵ0 -SMC if it is maximal and there are no contiguous
      chains D ⊆ P \ C and E ⊆ C with |D| ≥ ℵ0 , |D| ≥ |C|, and (C \ E) ∪ D a maximal chain in P .
    • (∼) If, for elements x, y ∈ P with x < y, the interval ⟨x, y⟩ is finite, then write x ∼ y.
    • C is an η-maximal chain if C has order-type η and the only chain with order type η and C ⊆ D
      is C itself.
    • (⪯η ) An η-replacement from one η-maximal chain C to another, C ′ , consists of a witness function
      f : C → I(C ′ ) satisfying the following three properties.

         – For all distinct x, y ∈ C, we have f (x) ∩ f (y) = ∅, and moreover, if x < y then f (x) <
            f (y).
         – For all x ∈ C, the interval f (x) ⊆ C ′ is either the singleton {x}, or contains a set of order
            type η.
         – For all x, y ∈ C and z ∈ f (y), if x < y then x < z, and if y < x then z < x.

      If such a replacement exists, write C ⪯η C ′ .
    • C is called an η-strongly maximal chain, or η-SMC if it admits no η-replacement.


Vacillating posets

This section covers the terminology used in Section 2.4.


    • A poset P is in-wellfounded or IWF if it has no chain C order-isomorphic to ζ.
    • A poset P is out-wellfounded or OWF if it contains no chain order-isomorphic to ω ⊕ ω ∗ .
    • (alt(C)) Given a chain C, the alternation number of C, written alt(C) the value in N ∪ {∞}
      equal to the supremum of those integers n such that there are disjoint continuous subchains
      I1 , I2 , . . . , In ⊆ C with each Ii containing elements X, Y ∈ G(P ) with X an increasing point, Y
      a decreasing point, and X > Y .
    • An element X ∈ G(P ) is said to be an illfounded limit if there is no contiguous IWF chain
      I ⊆ H(P ) containing both X and elements x, y ∈ P with x < X < y.
    • An interval I ⊆ H(P ) is said to be alternating-wellfounded if it contains no illfounded limit
      point.
       ◦                                                                                 ◦
    • (∼ C ) Points x, y ∈ C with x < y are said to be alternating-equivalent, written x ∼C y, if the

      following three conditions hold.

         – The interval (x, y) of C is OWF.
         – If there is an increasing limit of C between x and y, i.e. (x, y) ∩ N+ (C) ̸= ∅, then Y :=
            inf{Z ∈ N (C) : Z > y} must have Y ∈ N+ (C).
80                                                                       The Aharoni–Korman conjecture


            – If there is a decreasing limit of C between x and y, i.e. (x, y) ∩ N− (C) ̸= ∅, then X :=
              sup{Z ∈ N (C) : Z < x} must have X ∈ N− (C).
                                             ◦
     • (R(C)) An alternation class of C is a ∼ C -equivalence class of C that is infinite. The set of all

       alternation classes is denoted by R(C).
        ◦             ◦
     • (≃) Write C ≃ D if R(C) = R(D).
        ◦                                                                                  ◦
     • (⪯) A chain C ′ is an alternating replacement from a chain C, and we write C ⪯ C ′ , if there is a
       witness function f : R(C) → I(R(C ′ )) satisfying the following five properties.

            – If R(C) is empty, then R(C ′ ) is non-empty.
            – If X, Y ∈ R(C) are distinct, then f (X) ∩ f (Y ) = ∅.
            – f is order-preserving. That is, if X, Y ∈ R(C) have X < Y , then for any U ∈ f (X) and
              V ∈ f (Y ), we have U < V .
            – If X ∈ R(C), Y ∈ R(C ′ ) and z ∈ H(P ) have Y ∈ f (X) and z > X, then z > Y .
              Likewise, if z < X, then z < Y .
                                                                                           S
            – If, for all X ∈ R(C), we have that |f (X)| = 1 is a singleton and moreover       X∈R(C) f (X) =
              R(C ′ ), then f (X) = {X} for all X.

       If |f (X)| = 1 for all X ∈ R(C), then the replacement is called trivial.
                                                       ◦                   ◦
     • A chain C is called alternating-maximal if C ⪯ D implies that C ≃ D, i.e. it admits no nontrivial
       alternating replacement.
     • (C(P )) A chain C is called a candidate chain if it is maximal and alternating maximal in P . The
       set of candidate chains in P is denoted by C(P ).
     • A reduction C ⊵ D is said to be trivial if N (C) = N (D).
     • A candidate chain is said to be reduced if it admits no nontrivial reduction.
     • A thick chain in a poset P is a subset F ⊆ P such that every element x ∈ F is incomparable to
       only finitely many elements of F .
     • A thick chain F ⊆ P is called a strong thick chain if it satisfies the property ∀y ∈ P \ F, {x ∈
       F : (x ⊥ y) ∧ (∀z ∈ F )(z ∥ y =⇒ z ∥ x)} ≥ ℵ0 .
