Discrepancies of spanning trees

The work presented in this chapter is joint with Lyuben Lichev, Adva Mond, and Julien Portier [79].



3.1    Introduction

In this chapter, we prove a number of results about discrepancy and balance of trees—and in some
cases, more general sparse graphs—embedded in dense graphs. The first few results we prove concern
discrepancy, and the first result we prove is the following generalisation of a result of Erdős, Füredi,
Loebl and Sós from two to many colours, which we now restate.

Theorem 1.2.1. There exists a universal constant c > 0 such that the following holds. Fix an integer
r ≥ 2 and an r-colouring of the edges of Kn . Then, for every tree T on n vertices with maximum degree
∆, there exists a colour i ∈ [r] and a copy of T in which the number of edges of colour i is at least

                                        n−1   c
                                            + 2 (n − 1 − ∆).
                                         r   r

    We deduce the above result from the following, more general form.

Theorem 1.2.2. There exists a universal constant C > 0 such that the following holds. Fix an integer
r ≥ 2 and an r-colouring of the edges of Kn . For every colour i ∈ [r], denote by mi the number of
edges of colour i. Then, for every i ∈ [r] and every tree T on n vertices with maximum degree ∆, there
is a copy of T with at least
                                                                      2
                                                                m∗i
                                                            
                                   2mi
                                        + C(n − 1 − ∆)
                                    n                           n2
                                               
edges of colour i, where m∗i := min mi , n2 − mi .
                                           


    Next, we consider a different angle on the results proved in Theorem 1.2.2. Indeed, while Theo-
rem 1.2.2 guarantees that a given colour can occur in T more than the expected amount, we now consider
82                                                                          Discrepancies of spanning trees


when we can guarantee that a given colour can be made to occur significantly more frequently than any
other colour. The following two constructions demonstrate that this is not always possible when either
the number of colours or the maximum degree of the tree is very large.

Proposition 1.2.3. There is a constant ε0 ∈ (0, 1) such that, for all 0 < ε < ε0 and integers r ≥ 1/ε5 ,
the following holds. There is n0 = n0 (ε, r) such that, for all n ≥ n0 , there is a balanced r-colouring of
Kn such that, in every copy of a balanced ⌈1/ε⌉-caterpillar on n vertices in Kn , the number of edges of
colour 1 is smaller than the number of edges in one of the colours 2, . . . , r.

Proposition 1.2.4. There is a constant C > 0 such that the following holds. For every integer r ≥ 3
there is an integer n0 = n0 (r) such that, for all n ≥ n0 , there is a balanced r-colouring of Kn such that
for every tree T on n vertices with maximum degree ∆ ≥ 23n/24 + C and every copy of T in Kn , the
number of edges of colour 1 is smaller than the number of edges in some colour among 2, . . . , r.

     However, we show that under certain conditions, we can find embeddings of T in which a given
colour (which we will assume is colour 1) appears more frequently than all other colours. The following
definition captures those colourings for which this is possible for trees of sufficiently small maximum
degree.

Definition 1.2.6. For every η > 0, an r-colouring of G is η-standard if, for every partition V1 , V2 of its
vertex set with |V1 | − |V2 | ∈ {0, 1} and every integer i ∈ [2, r], G[V1 ] contains at least ηn2 edges of
colour different from 1 or G[V2 ] contains at least ηn2 edges of colour different from i.

     The following two theorems consider two different regimes of this problem, depending on the maxi-
mum degree of T .

Theorem 1.2.7. For every integer r ≥ 3 there is η0 = η0 (r) > 0 such that, for all η ∈ (0, η0 ), the
following holds. There is ε = ε(r, η) > 0 and an integer n0 = n0 (r, η) such that, for every n ≥ n0 and
every tree T on n vertices and maximum degree ∆ ≤ εn, every ε-balanced η-standard colouring of Kn
in r colours contains a copy of T where colour 1 is given to at least (η 2 /106 r3 )n more edges than any
other colour.

Theorem 1.2.9. Fix integers r ≥ 3 and ∆ ≥ 2. There exists η0 = η0 (r) > 0 such that the following
holds. There is ε = ε(r, ∆, η) > 0 and an integer n0 = n0 (r, ∆, η) such that, for every n ≥ n0 ,
every graph G on n vertices and minimum degree at least (1 − ε)n, and every tree T on n vertices and
maximum degree at most ∆, every ε-balanced η-standard colouring of G in r colours contains a copy of
T where colour 1 is given to at least εn more edges than any other colour.

     Finally, we consider what could be thought of as the opposite problem, seeking a copy of a given
n-vertex graph H in G with a given distribution of colours. In this context, we only work in the setting
of two colours, which we consider as −1 and +1, and thus seek a copy of H for which the embedding
3.2. Preliminary results and tools                                                                        83


has a given sum. We now recall the key definition which determines whether or not we can control the
sum of the embedding modulo 4.

Definition 1.2.13. For an integer n ≥ 1 and a spanning subgraph G of Kn , a colouring c : E(Kn ) →
{−1, 1} is called bipartite if there is a partition (V1 , V2 ) of the vertices of Kn such that either c−1 (1)
or c−1 (−1) is equal to the edge set of G[V1 ] ∪ G[V2 ]. Then, the sets V1 , V2 are called the parts of c.
Moreover, for an n-vertex graph H and G, c as above, the triplet (H, G, c) is called inert if all degrees
in H have the same parity and the colouring c is bipartite.

Proposition 1.2.14. For every n ≥ 1, every inert triplet (H, G, c) and every copy H ′ of H in G, c(H ′ )
has the same residue modulo 4.

    We now recall our final result, which states that we can find embeddings of any given sum, provided
that the sum is not too large, and satisfies the parity conditions discussed above.

Theorem 1.2.15. For every integer ∆ ≥ 1, there are constants ε = ε(∆), ν = ν(∆), α = α(∆) > 0 and
integers n0 = n0 (∆) and m0 = m0 (∆) such that the following holds for all n ≥ n0 and m ≥ m0 . Let
H be an n-vertex graph with m edges and maximum degree ∆, let G be an n-vertex graph with minimum
degree at least (1 − ε)n, and let c be a ν-balanced 2-edge-colouring of G. If the triplet (H, G, c) is inert,
set a = 4; otherwise, set a = 2. Then, there is an integer b ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3} such that, for every integer
k ∈ [−αm, αm], there is a copy H ′ ⊆ G of H with c(H ′ ) = k if and only if k ≡ b (mod a)).


3.1.1     Chapter outline

The remainder of this chapter is organised as follows. In Section 3.2, we describe the switching method
and prove several useful lemmas. In Section 3.3, we prove Theorem 1.2.2 and deduce Theorem 1.2.1.
Section 3.4 is dedicated to the proofs of Proposition 1.2.3 and 1.2.4, and Section 3.5 deals with the
proofs of Theorem 1.2.7 and Theorem 1.2.9. Then, in Section 3.6, we prove Proposition 1.2.14 and
Theorem 1.2.15. We conclude the chapter with additional remarks and open questions in Section 3.7.


3.2      Preliminary results and tools

In the proofs in this and the following sections, upper and lower integer parts will be omitted when
irrelevant for the argument.


3.2.1     Switchable edges and the switching technique

Fix a tree T on n vertices. A pair of vertex-disjoint edges uw and vz is called switchable if one of the
following conditions holds.

      • Both edges contain a leaf of T , say w in uw and z in vz (switchable pair of type I).
84                                                                             Discrepancies of spanning trees


      • Both edges contain a vertex of degree two, say u in uw and v in vz. Moreover, u and v share a
        common neighbour different from w and z (switchable pair of type II).
      • Both edges contain a vertex of degree two, say u in uw and v in vz. Moreover, uv is an edge in T
        (switchable pair of type III).




            u                  v               u                  v               u                  v




            w                  z               w                  z               w                  z

                      I                                  II                                III

Figure 3.1: An illustration of a switchable pair of edges uw, vz of type I, II and III, respectively. The
vertices marked with small circles have all their neighbours in the figure.


      Note that, for each of the three types of switchable pairs, the switching uw, vz → uz, vw leaves us
with a tree isomorphic to T . We call this switching allowed, see Figure 3.1. Observe that a switching
is an operation of replacing a pair of edges in some embedding of T with another pair of edges, thus
forming another embedding of the tree, while the term switchable is a characteristic of a pair of edges
indicating that the switching operation applied to them results into a tree isomorphic to T .
      We say that a family S of switchable edge pairs is free if all pairs in it are edge-disjoint. In particular,
this means that every allowed switching of a pair in S can be done in parallel of all other allowed
switchings of pairs in S.

Lemma 3.2.1. Let T be a tree on n vertices with maximum degree ∆. Then T contains a free family of
pairs of switchable edges of size at least (n − 1 − ∆)/40.

Proof. Set ζ = ζ(n, ∆) = ⌈(n − 1 − ∆)/40⌉. First, we consider three special cases.

     (i) If T is a path of length n − 1 ≥ 3, one can find ⌊(n − 1)/3⌋ ≥ ζ edge-disjoint paths of length
        three, each containing a switchable pair of type III. Moreover, these switchable pairs are pairwise
        disjoint, so we get a free family of the required size.
  (ii) If ζ = 0, the statement is trivially satisfied.
 (iii) If ζ = 1, then T contains at least one pair of leaves with different parents that form an allowed
        switching of type I.

      In the remainder of the proof, we assume that ∆ ∈ [3, n − 42] (so n ≥ 45). For every i ∈ [∆],
denote by di the number of vertices of degree i in T . By double counting the vertices and the edges of
3.2. Preliminary results and tools                                                                              85


T , respectively, we get that

                                    i=1                     i=1

every edge e in L, there are at most ∆ − 1 other edges in L that do not form a switchable pair of type I
with e. Hence, by forming disjoint switchable pairs greedily, we end up with at least ⌈(d1 − ∆)/2⌉ ≥ ζ
such pairs, as desired.

3.2.2      Marked 4-cycles

We define a marked 4-cycle to be a cycle γ ⊆ Kn of length 4 where one of the two pairs of opposite
edges in the cycle is said to be marked. These marked edges will later serve us to indicate which of the
two possible switchings along the cycle is advantageous for us. However, we keep the setting general to
allow for more flexibility and potentially further applications of this approach.
     Consider a switchable pair of edges uw, vz in T , an embedding of T in Kn and a family C of marked
4-cycles in Kn . The switching uw, vz → uz, vw is called desirable (with respect to C) if these edges are
embedded onto a pair of marked edges in a cycle γ ∈ C so that (the images of) u and v are at distance
two in γ. The next lemma shows that, given families C1 , C2 , . . . , Ck of marked 4-cycles, each of size at
least cn4 , a uniformly random embedding of a tree T with sufficiently small maximum degree in Kn
typically contains linearly many desirable switchings with respect to each family.

Lemma 3.2.2. Fix constants ε ∈ (0, 1/2), c ∈ (0, 1/4) and an integer k ≥ 1. Then there exists an
integer n0 = n0 (ε, c) such that, for every n ≥ n0 , every tree T on n vertices with maximum degree
∆ ≤ εn, and families C1 , C2 , . . . , Ck , each containing at least cn4 marked 4-cycles in Kn the following
holds with probability at least 1 − 400εk/c. In a uniformly random embedding of T in Kn , there exists
a free family containing at least cn/40 switchable pairs in desirable switchings with respect to Ci for
every i ∈ [k].

Proof. Fix a tree T with maximum degree ∆ ≤ εn and identify it with its random embedding in Kn .
Then, Lemma 3.2.1 ensures the existence of a free family S containing s := |S| ≥ (n − 1 − ∆)/40 ≥
3.2. Preliminary results and tools                                                                                 87


n/80 switchable pairs. We show via a second moment computation that, for every i ∈ [k], up to choos-
ing ε small, typically a constant proportion of the switchable pairs in S form a desirable switching with
respect to Ci .

is in Ci . As there are n(n − 1)(n − 2)(n − 3)/4 possible marked 4-cycles in Kn , we have

3.2.3    i-marked 4-cycles in edge-coloured graphs

In this section, we restrict the general setting introduced in Section 3.2.2. Fix an r-edge-colouring
E(Kn ) 7→ [r]. For our purposes, a 4-cycle will be marked if the corresponding desirable switching
88                                                                          Discrepancies of spanning trees


increases the number of edges of colour 1, which we shall refer to as blue for convenience. More
precisely, a 4-cycle γ = xyuv is marked if one of the following holds (see Figure 3.2):

     (i) xy, yu, uv are blue and vx is not blue, or
  (ii) xy, uv are blue and yu, vx are not blue, or
 (iii) xy is blue and the other edges are not blue.

      Note that, in each of the three cases, the marked edges in γ are yu and vx. Moreover, in the last case,
we call the cycle i-increasing if γ contains a single edge of colour i ∈ [2, r] and this edge is opposite to
the blue one.
            y                 u               y                u               y                u




            x                 v              x                 v               x                v
                    (i)                               (ii)                            (iii)

Figure 3.2: The three cases of a marked 4-cycle. Solid edges are blue, and dashed edges are some colour
other than blue.

      With an eye on the proof of Theorems 1.2.7 and 1.2.9, for every i ∈ [2, r], we consider the family Ci
consisting of the marked 4-cycles of types (i) and (ii) as well as all non-i-increasing 4-cycles. The goal of
this section is to show that, for a tree T as in Theorem 1.2.7, which is embedded uniformly at random in
a nearly balanced edge-colouring of Kn , we can perform a linear number of desirable switchings in such
a way that there is no i for which almost all of these desirable switchings are i-increasing. In general this
is not always possible, as Example 1.2.5 shows; however, the next lemma proves that such problematic
scenarios do not happen under the assumption that the colouring is η-standard for some small η > 0.
                                                                                                 √
Lemma 3.2.3. Fix an integer r ≥ 2, c ≤ 2−39 r−5 and ε ∈ (0, c). Consider an ε-balanced, 32r rc-
standard r-colouring of the complete graph Kn , and let G be a spanning subgraph of Kn with minimum
degree δ(G) ≥ (1 − ε)n. Then there is an integer n0 = n0 (r, c, ε) such that, for all n ≥ n0 and for
every colour i ∈ [2, r], there are at least cn4 marked 4-cycles in G that are not i-increasing.
                 √
Proof. Let α := 8rc and fix a colour i ∈ [2, r], denoting the edges in this colour as red. Recalling that
we refer to colour 1 as blue, a 4-clique in G is called blue if all 6 of its edges are blue, and good if it
contains two vertex-disjoint blue edges but is not a blue clique. Note that every good 4-clique contains a
marked 4-cycle which is not i-increasing. Our proof proceeds in several steps.


Step 1: an approximate blue clique. Let EB be the set of blue edges e for which there are at most αn2
blue edges f disjoint from e such that e ∪ f does not induce a blue clique in G. Note that for every pair
of edges e, f as above, if the clique they span is included in G, then it is good. We consider two cases.

3.2.4   Nearly balanced r-edge-colourings of Kn

We turn to the analysis of ε-balanced r-colourings of Kn . Our next step is to show that, in any nearly
balanced r-colouring of Kn , a uniformly random embedding of any spanning tree with suitably bounded
maximum degree contains approximately the same number of edges in each colour. In fact, the next
more general lemma follows from a simple application of the second moment method.


Lemma 3.2.5. Fix an integer r ≥ 1, δ ∈ (0, 1/2), and ε ∈ (0, δ 2 /400r). Then there is an integer
n0 = n0 (r, ε) such that the following holds for all n ≥ n0 . Consider an ε-balanced r-colouring of the
edges of Kn . Let H be a graph on n vertices, m ≥ 1/ε edges and with maximum degree ∆ ≤ εm. Then,
with probability at least 1 − 80εr/δ 2 , a uniformly random embedding of H into Kn contains (1 ± δ)m/r
edges in each colour.


Proof. Identify H with its uniformly random embedding and, for every i ∈ [r], let Xi be the random
variable counting the number of edges in H with colour i; in particular, E[Xi ] = (1/r ± ε)m for every
i ∈ [r]. We turn to a computation of Var(Xi ). Note that, for every edge e in H, there are at most
2∆ edges in H which share a vertex with e. For any two vertex-disjoint edges e, f in H, denote by
p1 = p1 (i) the probability that e receives colour i and by p2 = p2 (i) the probability that both e and f
receive colour i. Then, p1 = 1/r ± ε and p2 ≤ p1 · p1 n2 / n−2     ≤ p21 + 5/n. Thus, for every i ∈ [r],

3.3        Proof of Theorems 1.2.1 and 1.2.2

In this section, we prove Theorem 1.2.2 and then deduce Theorem 1.2.1 from it. The proof combines
a brief combinatorial case analysis, an application of the probabilistic method and a counting argument
and goes roughly as follows: first, we consider a random embedding of the tree T , and then, for some
fixed i ∈ [r], we switch some pairs of edges in order to increase the number of edges of colour i.


Proof of Theorem 1.2.2. Fix i ∈ [r] and an r-edge-colouring of Kn . We start by analysing several
particular cases. If mi ∈ {0, n2 }, the statement is trivial and, if ∆ = n − 1, the statement holds by a
                                

direct application of the probabilistic method. Suppose that m∗i ∈ [1, 16n/3) and ∆ ≤ n − 2. Then, by
choosing C < 9/256 so that
                                                                                     ∗ 2

C(n−1−∆)(m∗i )2 ≤ n3 ,       and consequently             +1 ≥         ≥     +C(n−1−∆)       ,
                                                       n         n        n            n2

it is sufficient to show that some copy of T contains a number of edges of colour i different from the
expected one: indeed, this guarantees that a copy overshooting this expected number exists. On the one
hand, since T is not a star, Lemma 3.2.1 ensures that T contains a switchable pair of edges. On the
other hand, the existence of a non-monochromatic 4-cycle with two edges of colour i opposite to each
other guarantees that the above pair of edges can be switched in a suitable copy of T (see Figure 3.2(i)
and (ii)). If the latter does not hold, then either all edges of colour i form a clique or contain a common
vertex. In the first case, let the clique have size k ∈ [n − 1]. Then, we must have (k − 1)k = 2mi , which
ensures a copy of T with at least k − 1 > 2mi /n edges of colour i. In the second case, 2mi /n < 2 but
a copy of T with at least two edges of colour i exists, as desired.

3.4     Proof of Propositions 1.2.3 and 1.2.4

Fix ε > 0. Before we proceed with the proof of Proposition 1.2.3, we recall that, given a prime number
p, there is a finite projective plane PG(2, p) with p2 + p + 1 points and p2 + p + 1 lines where every line
contains p + 1 points, every point lies on p + 1 lines and every two lines intersect at a single point. We
use this simple geometric object in combination with the following classical result about the density of
primes.

Lemma 3.4.1 (Theorem 1 in [12]). Let (pn )n≥1 be the sequence of prime numbers listed in increasing
order. Then, for all sufficiently large n, pn+1 − pn ≤ p0.525
                                                        n     .

    We are now ready to prove Proposition 1.2.3.


Proof of Proposition 1.2.3. Let α = 0.525 and ε be sufficiently small so that, by Lemma 3.4.1, we have
pm+1 − pm ≤ pαm for every prime pm satisfying pm ≥ (80/ε)1/(1−α) . Let pm0 be the smallest prime
greater than or equal to (80/ε)1/(1−α) . Set r0 = p2m0 + pm0 + 2 and fix any r ≥ r0 . Then, there is a
prime number p ≥ pm0 such that


                p2 + p + 2 ≤ r ≤ (p + pα )2 + (p + pα ) + 2 ≤ (1 + 3pα−1 )(p2 + p + 2).


Setting k = p2 + p + 2, the above rewrites as


                                                  0 ≤ r − k ≤ 3pα−1 k.                                    (3.4.1)


    We first define a suitable k-colouring of Kn and then modify it to form an r-colouring. To begin with,
we identify the set of colours {2, . . . , k} with the set of points in PG(2, p) via an arbitrary bijection. Also,
we assume for convenience that n is divisible by (r −1)(k −1); the general case follows by putting lower
and upper integer parts wherever is necessary.
    Now, we describe the k-colouring ck of Kn we are going to work with. We begin by partitioning the
vertices of Kn into k − 1 sets V2 , . . . , Vk of equal size (called parts). First, we give colour 1 to all edges
whose both endpoints are in the same part. Second, we identify the parts with the lines in PG(2, p) in a
bijective manner and, for every pair of distinct integers i, j ∈ [2, k], we colour all edges between Vi and
96                                                                          Discrepancies of spanning trees


Vj in the colour associated to the unique point in PG(2, p) where the lines corresponding to Vi and Vj
intersect. In this way, every vertex is adjacent to edges in exactly p + 2 colours.
     Next, we modify the k-colouring ck to create a suitable r-colouring cr of Kn . For every pair of
distinct integers i, j ∈ [2, k], we partition the edges between Vi and Vj into n/(k − 1) perfect matchings.
Then, we further partition this set of matchings into r−1 groups of equal size and, for every i′ ∈ [k+1, r],
recolour all (edges in) matchings in the i′ -th group with colour i′ . By construction, every colour in [2, r]
is given to

                                   !                              
      1        n             n/(k − 1)      1      n/(k − 1) − 1   n    1 n
                  − (k − 1)              =      1−                    ≤                               (3.4.2)
     r−1       2                 2         r−1         n−1         2    r 2

edges in cr . In particular, in cr , all colours in [2, r] are given to the same number of edges while colour
1 is given to Θ(n2 ) more edges than the rest.
     Fix L := ⌈1/ε⌉ ≤ 1 + 1/ε, a balanced L-caterpillar T and set s = ⌈(L + 1)/n⌉. By definition,
every vertex in the central path of T has degree s or s + 1. Fix an arbitrary copy of T in Kn . On the one
hand, it can contain at most (L + 1) · (n/(k − 1) − 1) edges of colour 1 in cr : indeed, every such edge
is incident to a vertex v in the central path of T and its other endvertex must therefore be in the same
part as v. On the other hand, every vertex u in Kn is incident to n/(k − 1) − 1 edges of colour 1, and
(k − 2)n/(k − 1)(r − 1) edges in each colour among k + 1, . . . , r (by rearranging the formula given by
(3.4.2)) while all remaining edges incident to u have one of p + 1 colours among 2, . . . , k. Moreover, it
follows from p ≥ (80/ε)1/(1−α) and (3.4.1) that max(2(p+1)(L+1)(L+2), 2(L+2)(r−k+1)) ≤ k−1.
Combining this with s · (L + 1) ≥ n, we get

                                                  !                                
         1                  n       (r − k)(k − 2)n       1          n       (r − k)n
                  s−           −1 −                   >        s−        −
        p+1                k−1       (k − 1)(r − 1)     p+1        k−1        (k − 1)
                                                                                       
                                                            s                    n
                                                      ≥          ≥ (L + 1) ·          −1 .
                                                        2(p + 1)                k−1

We deduce that, in cr , some colour among 2, . . . , k is given to more edges in the current copy of T than
colour 1. Finally, to account for the fact that cr is not a balanced colouring (colour 1 is given to more
edges than the rest), it suffices to reassign the colour of some edges with cr -colour 1. Recall that ε has
been chosen sufficiently small so that pm+1 − pm ≤ pαm ≤ pm is satisfied for every pm ≥ (80/ε)1/(1−α)
and, therefore,


                  r0 = p2m0 + pm0 + 2 ≤ (2(80/ε)1/(1−α) )2 + 2(80/ε)1/(1−α) + 2 ≤ 1/ε5 ,


as desired.


     Now, we proceed to the proof of Proposition 1.2.4.
3.5. Proofs of Theorems 1.2.7 and 1.2.9                                                                     97


Proof of Proposition 1.2.4. We assume for convenience that n is divisible by 2(r − 1) and show the
statement for C = 1; the general case requires only minor technical modifications resulting in a larger
constant C. Let V2 , . . . , Vr be a partition of the vertex set of Kn into r − 1 parts of size t = n/(r − 1).
    We define a balanced r-colouring cr of Kn as follows. To begin with, for every pair of i, j ∈ [2, r]
with i < j, decompose the edges between Vi and Vj into t matchings M1 , . . . , Mt of size t. Then,
give colour i to the edges in M1 ∪ . . . ∪ Mt/2 and colour j to the edges in Mt/2+1 ∪ . . . ∪ Mt . In
this way, every colour in [2, r] is given to (r − 2)t2 /2 edges. Finally, for every i ∈ [2, r], define d =
⌊(n − (r − 1)2 )/r(r − 1)⌋, pick an arbitrary d-regular subgraph of Kn [Vi ] and give colour i to each of its
edges while all remaining edges in Kn are given colour 1. It is easy to check that our choice of d satisfies

                                          (r − 2)t2 dt
                                                           
                                                         1 n
                                                   +   ≤      ,
                                              2      2   r 2

so colours 2, . . . , r are given to the same number of edges (equal to the left-most term in the above
display) while colour 1 is given to the same or a slightly larger number of edges.
    Now, fix a tree T of maximum degree ∆ ≥ 23n/24 + 1 and a copy of T in Kn . On the one hand,
the number of edges in T with colour 1 in cr is at most


                                          t − 1 − d + (n − 1 − ∆).                                     (3.4.3)


On the other hand, every vertex v of degree ∆ in our copy of T is incident to at least


                                       ∆ − (t − 1 − d) − (r − 2)t/2                                    (3.4.4)


edges in the same colour (given by the index of the part containing v). A direct computation shows
that (3.4.3) < (3.4.4) when ∆ ≥ (3r2 − 4)n/4r(r − 1) + 1. Moreover, the function x ∈ [3, ∞) 7→
(3x2 − 4)/4x(x − 1) is decreasing and its value at x = 3 is 23/24, so the condition on ∆ from the
statement of the lemma is sufficient to guarantee that, for any copy of T , some colour among 2, . . . , r
is given to more edges than colour 1. Finally, to account for the fact that cr is not a balanced colouring
(colour 1 is given to more edges than the rest), it suffices to reassign the colour of some edges with
cr -colour 1. This concludes the proof.




3.5    Proofs of Theorems 1.2.7 and 1.2.9

As in the proof of Theorem 1.2.2, the existence of many pairs of edges in T that can be switched indepen-
dently plays a key role in the proof of Theorems 1.2.7 and 1.2.9. However, this time, we need to control
the number of edges in each colour, not only a single one. We start with the proof of Theorem 1.2.7.
98                                                                          Discrepancies of spanning trees


Proof of Theorem 1.2.7. Fix r ≥ 3, η0 = 2−15 r−1 , and η ∈ (0, η0 ). For every i ∈ [2, r], consider
the family Ci of non-i-increasing switchings introduced in Section 3.2.3 (see Figure 3.2), and define
c = η 2 2−10 r−3 , δ = c/200 ≥ η 2 10−6 r−3 and ε = δ 2 10−3 r−1 , so that the assumptions of Lem-
mas 3.2.2, 3.2.3 and 3.2.5 are simultaneously satisfied, as well as 400εr/c + 80εr/δ 2 < 1. Then, there
is a copy of T in Kn where each colour is given to (1 ± δ)(n − 1)/r edges and moreover, there is a
free family containing at least cn/40 non-i-increasing switchings for every i ∈ [2, r]. After performing
each of these switchings, the difference between number of edges of colour 1 and the number of edges
of colour i becomes at least cn/40 − 2δn/r ≥ δn for every i ∈ [2, r], which finishes the proof.


3.5.1    Dealing with the missing edges: deducing Theorem 1.2.9

To prove Theorem 1.2.9, we extend our techniques to sparser host graphs. Given a graph G, we say that
a vertex pair uv is a ghost edge (for G) if uv is not an edge of G. We will perform an additional round
of vertex exchanges to ensure that there are no ghost edges remaining in our final copy of T .
     When we say that two copies T ′ , T ′′ of T differ in at most ℓ edges, we mean that the implicit iso-
morphisms f : T → T ′ ⊆ Kn and g : T → T ′′ ⊆ Kn have different images for at most ℓ edges of T .
We also say that T ′′ is obtained from T ′ by exchanging the vertices u, v ∈ V (Kn ) if f (g −1 (u)) = v,
f (g −1 (v)) = u and, moreover, f and g coincide over V (T ) \ f −1 ({u, v}) = V (T ) \ g −1 ({u, v}).

Lemma 3.5.1. Fix ε ∈ (0, 1/2(∆ + 1)3 ), δ > 0, a graph G ⊆ Kn with n vertices and minimum degree
δ(G) ≥ (1 − ε)n, and a copy T ′ ⊆ Kn of a graph T containing at most δn ghost edges. Then, there is
a copy T ′′ ⊆ G of T such that T ′ and T ′′ differ in at most 2∆δn edges.

Proof. If n < 2(∆ + 1)3 , there is nothing to prove (since G = Kn ), so suppose that n ≥ 2(∆ + 1)3 . We
construct a sequence of copies T ′ = T0 , T1 , . . . , Tk of T in Kn such that, for every i ∈ [k], Ti contains
fewer ghost edges than Ti−1 and Ti differs from Ti−1 in at most 2∆ edges. Moreover, we will ensure that
Tk contains no ghost edges, which will allow us to choose T ′′ = Tk : note that, by construction, k ≤ δn,
so T ′ and T ′′ will indeed differ in at most 2∆δn edges.
     We only describe the construction of T1 from T0 , as the subsequent constructions are done similarly.
Let NH [x] denote the closed H-neighbourhood of x, which consists of x and all its neighbours in H.
Suppose that x is incident to a ghost edge in T0 . We will show that there is a vertex z at distance at least
3 from x in T0 such that none of the edges between x and NT0 [z] is a ghost edge, and none of the edges
between z and NT0 [x] is a ghost edge. We then obtain the tree T1 by exchanging the vertices x and z in
T0 ; clearly, in this process, at least one ghost edge is eliminated.
     To find a vertex z as above, we first construct a set S of vertices containing x and of size at least
⌈n/(1 + ∆ + ∆2 )⌉, such that any two vertices in S are at distance at least three in T0 . We do this by
performing the following greedy deletion procedure. At the first stage, we add x to S and delete all
3.5. Proofs of Theorems 1.2.7 and 1.2.9                                                                     99


vertices within distance 2 from x. Then, as long as there is at least one not yet deleted vertex, we choose
an arbitrary such vertex y, add it to S and delete all remaining vertices within distance 2 from y in T0 .
    Observe that for every distinct z, z ′ ∈ S, we have that NT0 [z] and NT0 [z ′ ] are disjoint. Additionally,
there are at most (∆ + 1)εn < n/(1 + ∆ + ∆2 ) − 1 ghost edges going out of NT0 [x]. As a result, there
is a vertex z in S such that the bipartite graph with parts NT0 [x] and NT0 [z] does not contain any ghost
edge, which is therefore a complete bipartite graph. This choice of z satisfies the desired properties.

Remark 3.5.2. Note that the assertion of Lemma 3.5.1 is valid for any graph T of maximum degree ∆ (in
particular, ones containing cycles). Moreover, we note that, at the last step of the proof of Lemma 3.5.1,
there are at least

                   n                    n          n            n
                      2
                        − (∆ + 1)εn ≥      2
                                             −          2
                                                          ≥
                 1+∆+∆                1+∆+∆    2(∆ + 1)     2(∆ + 1)2

choices for z. This observation will be useful in Section 3.6.

    We now apply Lemma 3.5.1 to prove Theorem 1.2.9.


Proof of Theorem 1.2.9. Fix r ≥ 3, ∆ ≥ 2 and η ∈ (0, 2−15 r−1 ), and define c = η 2 2−10 r−3 , ε =
                      √
c2 10−6 r−1 and δ = 30 rε. In particular, we have that

                             80εr 400εr                                      c
                                 +      <1          and    4∆ε + 20rε ≤         .
                              δ2    c                                       160

Moreover, fix an ε-balanced η-standard r-colouring of G and extend it arbitrarily to an ε-balanced η-
standard r-colouring of Kn . For every i ∈ [2, r], denote by Ci′ the family of marked 4-cycles in G which
are not i-increasing. Recall that, for every i ∈ [2, r], Lemma 3.2.3 implies that |Ci′ | ≥ cn4 .
    Consider a uniformly random copy T ′ of T in Kn and let X be the number of ghost edges in T ′ . Since
E[X] ≤ εn, a computation similar to (3.2.4) shows that Var(X) ≤ 2∆n + O(n), which implies that
X ≤ 2εn with high probability. Together with Lemma 3.2.2 applied for C2′ , . . . , Cr′ and Lemma 3.2.5,
we deduce that, with positive probability, T ′ contains at most 2εn ghost edges, between (1 − δ)(n − 1)/r
and (1 + δ)(n − 1)/r edges in each colour, and a free family with at least cn/40 pairs in non-i-increasing
switchings for all i ∈ [2, r]. We condition on these properties of T ′ .
    Using Lemma 3.5.1, one can produce a copy T ′′ of T in Kn that contains no ghost edges and differs
from T ′ in at most 2∆εn edges (and thus, the symmetric difference of E(T ′ ) and E(T ′′ ) contains no
more than 4∆εn edges). As a result, since 4∆ε < c/80, one can find a free family S containing at least
cn/80 pairs in non-i-increasing switchings in T ′′ ⊆ G for every i ∈ [2, r]. Moreover, for every i ∈ [2, r],
T ′ contains at most 2δn more edges of colour i than of colour 1, which means that after performing all
switchings in S, the resulting copy of T contains at least (c/40 − 4∆ε − 2δ)n ≥ cn/160 ≥ εn more
edges of colour 1 than of colour i, as desired.

3.6      k-sum subgraphs and inert triplets: proofs of Proposition 1.2.14 and
         Theorem 1.2.15

We start with a short proof of Proposition 1.2.14.

Proof of Proposition 1.2.14. Fix an inert triplet (H, G, c) and extend the bipartite colouring c to a com-
plete bipartite colouring of the edges of Kn with parts V1 and V2 . Note that, for every copy H ′ of H
in Kn , the number of vertices of odd degree in H ′ [V1 ] is even. As a result, the parity of the number of
edges of H ′ in the cut (V1 , V2 ) is fixed. Since c(H ′ ) = |E(H)| − 2|E(H ′ ) ∩ (V1 × V2 )|, this finishes the
proof.

      Proposition 1.2.14 and the simple fact that c(H ′ ) has the same parity for all copies H ′ of H in G
show the “only if” part of Theorem 1.2.15. We turn to the more interesting “if” part of the theorem.
If the triplet (H, G, c) is not inert, our first task is to find a small subgraph of the graph G where one
vertex exchange can change the remainder modulo 4 of c(H ′ ) for a suitable copy H ′ of H. Given a
2-edge-colouring c of a graph G in red and blue, call a 4-cycle in G a (1, 3)-cycle if it has three edges in
one colour and one edge in the other. We start with the following auxiliary structural lemma.

Lemma 3.6.1. For any ε ∈ [0, 1/10] there is an universal constant n0 such that, for any n ≥ n0 and
n-vertex graph G with minimum degree at least (1 − ε)n, along with a non-bipartite 2-edge-colouring c
of G, G contains a (1, 3)-cycle.

Proof. Suppose for contradiction that no (1, 3)-cycle exists. To begin with, the classical Turán’s theorem
implies that G contains a copy of the complete graph K6 . Moreover, since every 2-edge-colouring of
K6 contains a monochromatic triangle, there is a monochromatic triangle T in G. Then, to avoid a
(1, 3)-cycle, every vertex v in G \ T must have all of its edges towards T in the same colour.
      We may suppose without loss of generality that all edges in G[T ] are red. Denote by R (resp. B) the
set of vertices in G\T with three red (resp. blue) edges towards T , and set X = V (G)\(R∪B∪T ). Then,
since no (1, 3)-cycles exist, all edges in G[R] ∪ G[B] must be red. Furthermore, since the minimum-
degree condition on G implies that |X| ≤ 3εn, there exists an inclusion-maximal set Y of size at least
(n − 3 − 3εn)/2 ≥ n/3 such that all edges in G[Y ] are red.
      We claim that, for every vertex x in G \ Y , all edges between x and Y are blue. Suppose otherwise
for contradiction. Then, by maximality of Y , there must be vertices x in G \ Y and y, z ∈ Y such that
xy, xz are respectively red and blue. Since |Y | ≥ 2εn + 3, the vertices y and z must have a common
neighbour w in Y . However, the cycle xywz would then form a (1, 3)-cycle, leading to a contradiction.
Thus, all edges between G \ Y and Y are blue.
      Finally, we claim that all edges within G \ Y are red. Suppose for contradiction that there is a blue
edge xy in G \ Y . Let z be a neighbour of x in Y . Since y and z have a common neighbour w in Y ,
3.6. k-sum subgraphs and inert triplets: proofs of Proposition 1.2.14 and Theorem 1.2.15                  101


the cycle xywz would then form a (1, 3)-cycle, contradicting the assumption. Hence, the colouring of G
is bipartite with parts Y and V (G) \ Y , contradicting the assumptions of the lemma and confirming the
existence of a (1, 3)-cycle.


    Consider a graph G with a 2-edge-colouring c, an integer ∆ ≥ 1 and a copy K of the complete
bipartite graph K2,2∆ in G. Suppose that K has parts A = {x, z} and B = {y} ∪ B ′ with |B| = |A|∆ =
2∆. We call K a type-1 copy (of K2,2∆ ) if:

   (i) each of the paths {xwz : w ∈ B ′ } is coloured identically, and
  (ii) c(xy) ̸= c(yz) and the path xyz is coloured differently from the other 2∆ − 1 paths of length 2
       with endpoints x, z, that is, c(xy) ̸= c(xw) or c(yz) ̸= c(wz) for all w ∈ B ′ .

Moreover, we call K a type-2 copy (of K2,2∆ ) if (i) holds and, additionally, for all w ∈ B ′ , |c({xy, yz})| ̸=
|c({xw, wz})|. In particular, in a type-2 copy, the path xyz is monochromatic if and only if the paths
xwz with w ∈ B ′ are not.
    Our gadgets (resp. 2-gadgets) mentioned in the proof outline will consist of parts of the graph H
embedded into a type-1 (resp. type-2) copy of K2,2∆ in G. The next lemma shows that a type-2 copy of
K2,2∆ can be found in dense host graphs equipped with non-bipartite colourings.

Lemma 3.6.2. Fix ε, n, G and c as in Lemma 3.6.1. Then, G contains a type-2 copy of K2,2∆ .

Proof. Using Lemma 3.6.1, let xyzw be a (1, 3)-cycle where the edge xy has a different colour than the
other edges of the cycle. Then, since at least (1 − 2ε)n − 5 ≥ 8∆ − 5 vertices different from x, y, z, w
are adjacent to both x and z, there must be a set B ′ of size 2∆ − 1 such that the paths {xwz : w ∈ B ′ }
are coloured identically. If these paths are monochromatic, the edges between {x, z} and {y} ∪ B ′ form
a type-2 copy of K2,2∆ . Otherwise, the edges between {x, z} and {w} ∪ B ′ form a type-2 copy of
K2,2∆ .


    We now formally define our gadgets. In the setting of Theorem 1.2.15, given a copy H ′ of H in
G, we define a gadget (resp. 2-gadget) to be a type-1 (resp. type-2) copy K of K2,2∆ in G with parts
A = {x, z} and B = {y} ∪ B1 ∪ B2 ∪ B ′ for disjoint sets B1 , B2 , B ′ such that


                                NH ′ (x) = {y} ∪ B1     and    NH ′ (z) = B2


have the same size. We highlight the following observation.

Remark 3.6.3. In a gadget (resp. a 2-gadget), exchanging the positions of the vertices x, z in the embed-
ding H ′ of H produces an embedding H ′′ of H in G with c(H ′′ ) = c(H ′ )±4 (resp. c(H ′′ ) = c(H ′ )±2)
but c(H ′′ ) ̸= c(H ′ ).
102                                                                         Discrepancies of spanning trees


A gadget is called positive if c(H ′′ ) > c(H ′ ) and negative otherwise.
      Our next goal is to show that dense host graphs contain many type-1 copies of K2,2∆ . The next
lemma is a preliminary step towards proving this.

Lemma 3.6.4. Fix ε, ν > 0, and consider an n-vertex graph G with minimum degree at least (1 − ε)n
and a ν-balanced 2-edge-colouring c of G. Set ξ := (1 − 2ν − 4ε)/8. Then, in G, there are at least ξn
vertices, each having at least ξn blue neighbours and at least ξn red neighbours in G.

Proof. Assume for contradiction that the statement does not hold. Consider a partition R ∪ B ∪ Y of
V (G) where the vertices in Y are incident to at least ξn edges in each colour, the vertices in R are
incident to fewer than ξn blue edges and the vertices in B are incident to fewer than ξn red edges.
Suppose without loss of generality that |R| ≤ |B|. We consider two cases. If |R| ≥ (2ξ + ε)n, since
every vertex in R (resp. B) is incident to fewer than ξn blue (resp. red) edges, there are fewer than
(|R| + |B|)ξn ≤ 2|B|ξn edges between R and B. However, this implies that some vertex in B has more
than εn non-neighbours in R, contradicting the minimum-degree condition for G. If |R| < (2ξ + ε)n,
then |B| ≥ n − |R ∪ Y | ≥ (1 − 3ξ − ε)n and each vertex in B is incident to at least (1 − ε)n edges, of
which less than ξn are red, and so at least (1 − ε − ξ)n are blue. Hence, the number of blue edges in G
is at least                                                           
                 (1 − 3ξ − ε)n · (1 − ξ − ε)n                 n    1      n
                                              > (1 − 4ξ − 2ε)   ≥    +ν     ,
                              2                               2    2      2
which contradicts the assumption that c is a ν-balanced colouring. This completes the proof.

Lemma 3.6.5. Fix ε, ν ∈ [0, 1/800], ∆ ≥ 1 and consider an n-vertex graph G with minimum degree
at least (1 − ε)n and a ν-balanced 2-edge-colouring c of G. Then, G contains at least n2∆+2 /(40∆)!
type-1 copies of K2,2∆ .

Proof. For a vertex u, let NB (u) (resp. NR (u)) denote the set of neighbours of u that are connected
to u via a blue (resp. red) edge in G. By Lemma 3.6.4, there are at least n/9 vertices x such that
min(|NB (x)|, |NR (x)|) ≥ n/9. Fix one such vertex x and assume without loss of generality that at least
half of the edges between NB (x) and NR (x) are blue. Consequently, there must be at least (n/9)(n/9 −
n/800)/2 ≥ n2 /170 blue edges between NB (x) and NR (x). Therefore there must be at least n/340
vertices y ∈ NR (x) such that |NB (x) ∩ NB (y)| ≥ n/340.
      Fix one such vertex y ∈ NR (x) and let Y = NB (x) ∩ NB (y). Also, fix a vertex z ∈ Y and
suppose that |NB (z) ∩ Y | ≥ |NR (z) ∩ Y | (the opposite case is treated similarly). Then, we have
|NB (z) ∩ Y | ≥ (n/340 − εn − 1)/2 ≥ n/1600 and, for every subset W ⊆ NB (z) ∩ Y of size 2∆ − 1,
the complete bipartite graph between {x, z} and {y} ∪ W is a type-1 copy of K2,2∆ . As a result, the
number of such copies is at least

    For the remainder of this section, given a host graph G ⊆ Kn with a ν-balanced 2-edge-colouring c :

  (a) H ′ has (1 ± ν/5)m/2 edges in each colour,

  (c) H ′ contains at least m/(45∆)! positive and m/(45∆)! negative vertex-disjoint gadgets.

Note that property (a) holds with probability at least 0.8 by Lemma 3.2.5 applied with δ = ν/5 ∈

      We are ready to prove Theorem 1.2.15.



Proof of Theorem 1.2.15. Fix m ≥ (50∆)!, ν ∈ (0, 1/(50∆)!], ε ≤ ν 2 /105 and α = 1/5(45∆)!.
We begin by considering the case where the triplet (H, G, c) is not inert, with the other case handled
similarly. Let b be the residue class of m modulo 2, and let k ∈ [−αm, αm] such that k ≡ b ( mod 2)).
We then consider an embedding H ′ of H taken uniformly at random in Kn . As previously noted, property
(a) holds with probability at least 0.8 by Lemma 3.2.5 applied with δ = ν/5 ∈ (0, 2−20 ], and property
(b) holds with probability at least 2/3 by Markov’s inequality. Additionally, property (c) is satisfied with
probability at least 0.8 by Lemma 3.6.6. Therefore, properties (a), (b) and (c) hold simultaneously with
positive probability, and we fix a copy H1 of H satisfying all of them. We proceed with four consecutive
rounds of vertex exchanges to complete the proof:


  (i) First, we select a type-2 copy K of K2,2∆ (as provided by Lemma 3.6.2) and perform up to
        2∆ + 2 exchanges of vertex pairs in H1 to obtain a copy H2 of H in Kn containing a 2-gadget
        embedded into K. We assume that the vertex exchange described in Remark 3.6.3 increases the
        value of c(H2 ) by 2, the other case being analogous. Moreover, combining the fact that H1 and
        H2 differ in at most (2∆ + 2)2∆ ≤ 8∆2 edges with property (b), it follows that H2 contains at
        most 3εm + 8∆2 non-edges of G.
  (ii) Next, by applying Lemma 3.5.1 and Remark 3.5.2 (with n ≥ 5(∆ + 1)3 , ensuring n/2(∆ + 1)2 ≥
        2∆ + 3), we can find a copy H3 of H in G that differs from H1 in at most


                                  8∆2 + 2∆(3εm + 8∆2 ) ≤ 24∆3 + 6∆εm


        edges. Furthermore, since the 2-gadget contains 2∆ + 2 vertices, Remark 3.5.2 guarantees that H3
        can be obtained from H2 while leaving this 2-gadget untouched.
3.7. Concluding remarks and open problems                                                                               105


 (iii) Using property (c) and the inequality
                                                                          
                                           m
                                                ≥ 5 (2∆ + 2)∆ + 24∆3 + 6∆εm ,
                                         (45∆)!

          it follows that there are at least 4m/5(45∆)! positive and at least 4m/5(45∆)! negative vertex-
          disjoint gadgets that remained untouched in the process of obtaining H3 from H1 . Using Re-
          mark 3.6.3, recall that the described vertex exchange in a positive (resp. negative) gadget changes
          the value of c(H3 ) by 2 or 4 (resp. −2 or −4). Furthermore, we have that
                                                                  
                                        νm                  3        1   4m
                          |c(H3 )| ≤ 2     + (2∆ + 2)∆ + 24∆ + 6∆εm ≤ ·        .
                                        10                           2 5(45∆)!

          Thus, |k − c(H3 )| ≤ 2m/5(45∆)!. As a result, by activating a suitable number of positive or
          negative gadgets (depending on the sign of k − c(H3 )), we obtain a copy H4 of H in G such that
          k − c(H4 ) = 0 or k − c(H4 ) = 2.
 (iv) If k − c(H4 ) = 0, we are done. If k − c(H4 ) = 2, then we perform a final vertex exchange as
          described in Remark 3.6.3 within the 2-gadget embedded into K during the first round of vertex
          exchanges. This way, we obtain a copy H5 of H in G satisfying c(H5 ) = k, as desired.



3.7        Concluding remarks and open problems

We begin with a few comments concerning analogues of our results for digraphs. This topic was also
raised by Freschi, Hyde, Lada, and Treglown [56] who asked whether any n-vertex r-edge-coloured
digraph with minimum in- and out-degree at least (1/2 + 1/2r + o(1))n contains a Hamilton cycle with
significant colour-bias.1 The next simple example shows that, in fact, no in- and out-degree conditions
are sufficient to guarantee a significantly colour-biased copy of any rooted spanning tree oriented away
from its root. Since Hamilton cycles are one edge away from a consistently oriented spanning path, the
example also gives a negative answer to the question in [56].

Example 3.7.1. Partition a set V of n vertices into r parts V1 , . . . , Vr whose sizes are as equal as possible.
In the complete digraph on V , colour every edge towards Vi with colour i. For every rooted spanning
tree T with edges oriented away from its root, every vertex except the root has a single incoming edge.
As a result, every copy of T has between ⌈n/r⌉ and ⌊n/r⌋ − 1 edges in each colour.

       We note that the above example relies on the fact that almost all vertices in the tree T have the
same in-degree. Going further by analysing spanning trees and, in general, bounded degree spanning
subgraphs with in- and out-degree sequences which are far from regular remains an interesting open
question.
   1
       Recall that, in a digraph containing vertices u and v, the directed edges uv and vu can appear simultaneously.
106                                                                      Discrepancies of spanning trees


      We believe that some aspects of our work can be extended. First of all, we observe that the poly-
nomial dependency on r in Theorem 1.2.1 is necessary. To see this, fix k satisfying k 2 + k ≤ 2r and
consider a copy of Kn with vertex partition V1 , . . . , Vk of sizes as equal as possible. Colouring the
edges between Vi and Vj (with i, j possibly coinciding) in a distinct colour for all pairs i, j provides a
construction where no spanning tree has discrepancy more than 2⌈n/k⌉. The exact dependency on r
remains a curious open problem. Regarding Theorem 1.2.9, we believe that ideas of Komlós, Sárkózy
and Szemerédi [88] can be used to remove the dependency of ε on ∆ but no such attempt has been made
in our work.
      Another intriguing direction of research is to understand whether Theorem 1.2.15 could be gen-
eralised for r ≥ 2 colours. While we believe that extending our method to the 3-coloured setting is
possible, the main approach breaks down for r ≥ 4 due to pathological constructions similar to Exam-
ple 1.2.5. In this direction, Pardey and Rautenbach [104] conjectured that in a balanced colouring of
the complete graph with many colours, one can always find a perfect matching which is almost colour
balanced. Indeed, this conjecture is the focus of the next chapter. However, understanding the general
setting for several colours when H is a graph with maximum degree bounded above by an absolute
constant, or even the simpler case of an R-factor for some fixed graph R, remains widely open.
Chapter 4


Colour-balanced perfect matchings

The work presented in this chapter is drawn from a paper of which I am the sole author [74].



4.1    Introduction

This work presented in this chapter consists primarily of a proof of Theorem 1.3.3. We first recall that we
are considering edge-colourings of the clique K2kn , and that a colouring c : E(K2kn ) → [k] is colour-
balanced if there are equally many edges of each colour. Given such a colour-balanced colouring, recall
further that the function f of a matching M ⊆ E(K2nk ) is defined as follows.

                                                k
                                                X
                                    f (M ) :=         |c−1 (i) ∩ M | − n .
                                                i=1

    We now restate Theorem 1.3.3 for convenience.

Theorem 1.3.3. For positive integers n and k, and colour-balanced c : E(K2nk ) → [k], there is some
perfect matching M of K2nk satisfying

                                                             2
                                                f (M ) ≤ 4k .                                        (1.3.3)


    Before providing a proof of Theorem 1.3.3, we first state and prove Theorem 4.2.1, which is very
similar to Theorem 1.3.2 of Pardey and Rautenbach, to serve as a warm-up and introduce some of the
ideas we will use in the main proof.



4.2    A new proof of Theorem 1.3.2

In this section we prove the following theorem, which is slightly stronger than Theorem 1.3.2, but still has
                            √
the crucial dependence on n. The purpose of Theorem 4.2.1 is not the result itself (as it is superseded
108                                                                        Colour-balanced perfect matchings


by Theorem 1.3.3), but rather to introduce the ideas that will be used in the proof of our main result in
Section 4.3.

Theorem 4.2.1. If n and k are positive integers, and c : E(K2kn ) → [k] is colour-balanced, then there
is a perfect matching M of K2kn with

                                                         √
                                               f (M ) ≤ k 2n.


      Before giving the details of the proof, we present some discussion and preliminary definitions, which
will be referred back to in later sections.
      The idea in this proof is that, instead of seeking to reduce f (M ), the sum of the absolute deviations
from balancedness of a perfect matching M , we seek to minimise the sum of the deviations squared. To
this end, we make the following definitions.


                                   wM (e) := |{e′ ∈ M : c(e′ ) = c(e)}|.                              (4.2.1)


      In words, wM (e) is the number of edges in the matching M with the same colour as the edge e. We
will refer to wM (e) as the weight of the edge e. We can then also define the function wM applied to a set
S ⊆ E(K), and for convenience we also give a notation for the average value of wM .

                                                       X
                                           wM (S) :=         wM (e).                                  (4.2.2)
                                                       e∈S

                                           wM (S) := wM (S)/|S|.                                      (4.2.3)


      We make the following simple, but crucial, definition and observation.

                                                                    k
                                                   X                X            2
                            g(M ) := wM (M ) =           wM (e) =         mi (M ) ,                   (4.2.4)
                                                   e∈M              i=1


      where mi (S) is the number of edges in the set S of colour i, or, formally:


                                      mi (S) := {e ∈ S : c(e) = i} .


We use equality (4.2.4) to prove the following claim, which is the main tool in our proof of Theo-
rem 4.2.1.

Claim 4.2.2. For edges uv, xy ∈ M , define M ′ := (M − {uv, xy}) ∪ {ux, vy}. Then if wM (uv) +
wM (xy) − wM (ux) − wM (vy) > 4, then g(M ′ ) < g(M ).

Proof. Removing edges uv and xy from M decreases the value of g(M ) by at least 2(wM (uv) +
wM (xy) − 2), as we remove the edges with weights wM (uv) and wM (xy), and either reduce the weights
4.2. A new proof of Theorem 1.3.2                                                                      109


of wM (uv) + wM (xy) − 2 edges by 1 (when c(uv) ̸= c(xy)), or reduce the weights of wM (uv) − 2
edges by 2 (when c(uv) = c(xy)). A similar calculation gives that adding edges ux and vy increases the
value of g(M ) by at least 2(wM (ux) + wM (vy) − 2), again with equality when the colours are equal,
and distinct from the colours of uv and xy. Thus we find that overall,


                   g(M ) − g(M ′ ) ≥ 2(wM (uv) + wM (xy) − wM (ux) − wM (vy) − 4),


from which the claim follows.


   We will assume throughout the rest of the chapter that the situation of Claim 4.2.2 does not occur,
and for this reason, we shall refer to uv, xy ∈ M as in the statement of Claim 4.2.2 as a contradicting
swap. It is worth noting that the condition in Claim 4.2.2 depends only on wM , not wM ′ , and so we
may determine if a swap M is contradicting from wM alone. We are now ready to prove our warm-up
theorem.


Proof of Theorem 4.2.1. Throughout this proof, we let K be our clique of order 2nk. Assume that M is
some perfect matching minimising the value of g(M ). By symmetry, the average value of


                   2(wM (uv) + wM (xy)) − wM (ux) − wM (uy) − wM (vx) − wM (vy)


over all uv, xy ∈ M is equal to 4(wM (M ) − wM (E \ M )). Thus if wM (M ) − wM (E \ M ) > 2 there
must be some u, v, x, y ∈ V (K) forming a contradicting swap, whereupon Claim 4.2.2 applies and we
find some M ′ with g(M ′ ) < g(M ), a contradiction.
   Therefore we may assume that wM (M ) − wM (E \ M ) ≤ 2. Expanding definitions, this implies the

4.3      Proof of Theorem 1.3.3

We now extend the proof given in Section 4.2 to remove the n-dependence. The intuition here is that the
equality case in the previous proof, namely that every swap decreases wM by 2, cannot actually occur. In
fact, as will be shown, it is very far removed from reality. All notation and terminology from Section 4.2
will be used throughout this section as well. As the cases k = 2, 3 have been dealt with (as discussed in
Section 4.1), we may assume that k ≥ 4.
      Our proof has several steps. Firstly, in Section 4.3.1 we provide an algorithm for grouping colours
into sets A1 , . . . , At , so that colours which occur a similar number of times in M are in the same set.
Then in Section 4.3.2, we define a partial order ≻ on [t] × [t] to record in which cases swaps between
colours in our sets would necessarily be contradicting (i.e. satisfying the conditions of Claim 4.2.2).
      In Section 4.3.3, we prove that ≻ is contained within a larger, simpler partial order. In particular, we
will be able to associate a number ai with each set Ai in such a way that (i, j) ≃ (i′ , j ′ ) if and only if
ai + aj = ai′ + aj ′ . Finally in Section 4.3.4 we combine the results of the previous subsections to derive
a contradiction, completing the proof of Theorem 1.3.3.
      Assume for contradiction that we have a perfect matching M which minimises g(M ), but still has
              2
f (M ) > 4k .


4.3.1     Collecting similar colours

We iteratively construct sets of colours A1 , . . . , At as follows. Initially set t = k and each Ai = {i},
assuming, after possibly reordering, that


                                    m1 (M ) ≥ m2 (M ) ≥ · · · ≥ mk (M ).                               (4.3.1)
4.3. Proof of Theorem 1.3.3                                                                              111


We now iteratively decrease t. Assume that t ≤ ℓ and let ℓ := k − t. If, for some i,


                       min{|mx (M ) − my (M )| : x ∈ Ai and y ∈ Ai+1 } ≤ 4(ℓ+1)k ,                    (4.3.2)


then we combine sets Ai and Ai+1 into one set. We also decrease the index of sets Aj with j > i + 1 so
that the indices remain consecutive and the ordering from (4.3.1) is preserved. The process terminates
when either t = 1 or no pair of sets satisfy (4.3.2).
    Note that if the process terminates with t = 1, i.e. all colours are collected into a single set, then we
can bound the values of mi (M ) as follows.

                                                                      k−1
                                                                      X
                         |mi (M ) − n| ≤ m1 (M ) − mk (M ) ≤                4jk < 4k(k−1)+1 .
                                                                      j=0


From which Theorem 1.3.3 quickly follows.
    Thus we may assume that the process terminates with at least two distinct sets of colours remaining.
We now make some definitions and observations about the sets Ai , which we will refer back to later.
    For any set A ⊆ [k], we define

where we recall that ℓ = k − t. Finally, define α(e) ∈ [t] to be such that c(e) ∈ Aα(e) for any edge
e ∈ E. With these notions in hand, we proceed to the rest of the proof.


4.3.2    Partially ordering pairs of colour sets

We now define a partial order ≻ on [t] × [t] as follows.
112                                                                                Colour-balanced perfect matchings


Definition 4.3.1. For i, j, i′ , j ′ ∈ [t], let (i, j) ≻ (i′ , j ′ ) if every swap from colours (x, y) ∈ Ai × Aj to
(x′ , y ′ ) ∈ Ai′ × Aj ′ is contradicting, as in Claim 4.2.2. In other words, for all x ∈ Ai , y ∈ Aj , x′ ∈
Ai′ , y ′ ∈ Aj ′ , we have wM (x) + wM (y) > wM (x′ ) + wM (y ′ ) + 4.

      We now prove some results about the structure of the partial order ≻. Firstly, define relations ∼ and
≃ on [t] × [t] as follows. Set (i, j) ∼ (i′ , j ′ ) if (i, j) and (i′ , j ′ ) are ≻-incomparable, and then let ≃
be the transitive closure of ∼. That is, ≃ is the minimal transitive relation extending ∼, which is then
reflexive, symmetric, and transitive, i.e. an equivalence relation. Finally, define B1 , B2 , . . . , Bs to be the
equivalence classes of the equivalence relation ≃, ordered so that if q < r, (i, j) ∈ Bq and (i′ , j ′ ) ∈ Br ,
then (i, j) ≻ (i′ , j ′ ); it is natural to write


                                                    B1 ≻ B2 ≻ · · · ≻ Bs .


In particular, (1, 1) ∈ B1 and (t, t) ∈ Bs .

Lemma 4.3.2. For any x, y, z ∈ [t] with x ̸= y, we have that (x, z) ̸≃ (y, z) and (z, x) ̸≃ (z, y). In
particular, s ≥ 2t − 1.

Proof. The arguments in these two cases are entirely similar, so we consider the first case. Assume for
contradiction that (x, z) ≃ (y, z). Then there is some sequence


                               (x, z) = (a0 , b0 ) ∼ (a1 , b1 ) ∼ . . . ∼ (ar , br ) = (y, z),


and we may furthermore assume that we have chosen the triple x, y, z and intermediate steps (ai , bi ) to
minimise the value of r amongst all choices with x ̸= y and (x, z) ≃ (y, z).
      We see that a0 , a1 , . . . , ar , b0 , b1 , . . . , br are all distinct, as otherwise there would be a different choice
of x, y, z resulting in a smaller value of r. Thus by definition of ∼ and (4.3.3), we see that for any colours
b ∈ Ax and c ∈ Ay , we have the following.

                                                        r

into a single set, a contradiction, and the claim is proved.

Remark 4.3.3. We may assume that there are no edges uv, xy ∈ M , indices i, j, i′ , j ′ ∈ [t], and q, r ∈ [s]

4.3.3    Approximating ≻ with (R, +)

In this subsection we prove the following lemma.


Lemma 4.3.4. There are numbers a1 , a2 , . . . , at ∈ R such that ai + aj = ai′ + aj ′ if and only if
(i, j) ≃ (i′ , j ′ ). Furthermore, we may assume that a1 > a2 > · · · > at .


Proof. We will think of a = (a1 , a2 , . . . , at ) as a vector, and find it as an element of the null space of a
certain matrix N . For each relation (i, j) ≃ (i′ , j ′ ), we seek to enforce that ai + aj = ai′ + aj ′ . To this
end, add a row to N with +1 in columns i, j, and −1 in columns i′ , j ′ , and 0 in all other columns (or a
+2 if i = j or −2 if i′ = j ′ ). We seek a vector a = (a1 , a2 , . . . , at ) such that N a = 0.
    If we define


                                 bi := min{mc (M ) : c ∈ Ai } for each i ∈ [t],


then letting b = (b1 , b2 , . . . , bt ), we have N b = ε, where ε = (ε1 , ε2 , . . . , εt ) is a vector of error terms.
This vector b is our starting point for finding a, but we still need to manipulate it to be exactly in the null
space of N . For each r ∈ [t], we have εr = bi + bj − bi′ − bj ′ for some (i, j) ≃ (i′ , j ′ ). Using an entirely
similar argument to the proof of Lemma 4.3.2, we discover that


                                            εr < 2 4ℓk for all r ∈ [t].
                                                      
                                                                                                                (4.3.5)


    We now construct a t × t square matrix T based on N . If N has fewer than t rows, then append
additional all-zero rows to the end of N to form the square matrix T . If N has more than t rows then,
possibly reordering rows, we may assume that all rows of N after the first t are in the linear span (over
R) of the first t rows. Then we may let T consist of the first t rows of N .
    In either case, note that T b = ε′ , where each component of ε′ equal to either zero or some component
of ε; in particular, ∥ε′ ∥∞ ≤ ∥ε∥∞ .
    If T is the zero matrix, then we can let a = b. Otherwise, if λ is the minimal non-zero eigenvalue of
114                                                                       Colour-balanced perfect matchings


T , then we know by projecting b onto the null space of T that we can find some a with T a = 0 and


                                          ∥a − b∥∞ ≤ |λ|−1 ∥ε′ ∥∞ .


Next, note that the product of the non-zero eigenvalues of an integer matrix must itself be a non-zero
integer (as it appears as a coefficient of the integer polynomial p(x) = det(T − xI)), and hence is at
least 1 in absolute value. Then, noting that the sum of the absolute value of the coefficients in any row of
N is 4, we see that 4 is an upper bound on the absolute value of any eigenvalue of T . Thus 4t−1 |λ| ≥ 1,
and so |λ| ≥ 41−t . Putting this all together, we discover the following.


                             ∥a − b∥∞ ≤ |λ|−1 (2(4ℓk ))         by (4.3.5)

                                        ≤ 4t 4ℓk /2

                                        < 4(ℓ+1)k /8,        as k ≥ 4 and k > t.


Now we may further note that inequality (4.3.4) gives


                               4(ℓ+1)k /2 < min{d(Ai , Ai+1 ) : 1 ≤ i < t}/2

                                          ≤ min{bi − bi+1 : 1 ≤ i < t}/2.


and so, as the sequence bi is strictly decreasing, we find that ai is strictly decreasing too, as required.

                              |{(uv, xy) ∈ S : {c(ux), c(vy)} = {i, j}}| <                          pi,j .   (4.3.8)
               q=1 (i,j)∈Bq                                                          q=1 (i,j)∈Bq


Both sides of (4.3.8) count elements of S. The right-hand side counts the number of ordered pairs of
ordered edges (uv, xy) ∈ S with colours (c(uv), c(xy)) ∈ B1 ∪ · · · ∪ Bh , i.e. the number of swaps from
a pair of colours in B1 ∪ · · · ∪ Bh . The left-hand side of (4.3.8) counts the number of such pairs for
116                                                                                           Colour-balanced perfect matchings


which (c(ux), c(vy)) ∈ B1 ∪ · · · ∪ Bh , i.e. the number of swaps to a pair of colours in B1 ∪ · · · ∪ Bh .
Any swap from a pair of colours in B1 ∪ · · · ∪ Bh to a pair of colours not in B1 ∪ · · · ∪ Bh is necessarily
contradicting, but (4.3.8) tells us precisely that there are more of the former than the latter.

      Thus the inequality in (4.3.8) implies that there must be a contradicting swap, a contradiction, and so
the claim holds.




      We now manipulate the definition of ξi ; our goal here is to show that ξi /|Ai | is an increasing function
of i, from which we will derive our final contradiction. For the sake of intuition, not much is lost by
considering the case when all |Ai | = 1, and this may ease understanding.

   Indeed, in the case when all Ai have size 1, tj=1 yi,j counts the number of edges of colour i in
                                                   P

E \ M , and tj=1 pi,j counts (a constant multiple of) the number of edges of colour i in M , from which
            P

it is clear that ξi should be increasing. We now prove this generally. We first deal with sums of yi,j and
pi,j .
