      Define νi to be the sequence formed by |A1 | copies of ξ1 /|A1 |, then |A2 | copies of ξ2 /|A2 |, and so
4.4. Conclusion and future work                                                                         117


on. Thus we may re-write ϕ as follows.

                                                       k
                                                       X
                                                  ϕ=          ai νi .
                                                        i=1


Then we know that ai is a strictly decreasing sequence, νi is an weakly-increasing not-entirely-constant
sequence with zero sum (due to (4.3.7) and that ξi /|Ai | is strictly increasing), and so we see that ϕ < 0.
To reach our contradiction, we now prove the following claim.

Claim 4.3.6. ϕ ≥ 0.

Proof. Consider the weaker situation where instead the numbers zi,j merely form some zero-sum sym-
metric matrix satisfying the conclusion of Claim 4.3.5; we will show that this is already enough to deduce
that ϕ ≥ 0. In the case when all zi,j = 0, we have ϕ = 0.
    Now, any other valid value of zi,j can be formed by starting in the case zi,j = 0, and then performing
the following sequence of operations a number of times.

      • Select i ≤ j, i′ ≤ j ′ with (i, j) ∈ Bq and (i′ , j ′ ) ∈ Br for some q ≤ r.
      • Select some weight ζ > 0, and replace zi,j with zi,j − ζ, and zi′ ,j ′ with zi′ ,j ′ + ζ.
      • Similarly, move ζ weight from zj,i to zj ′ ,i′ to preserve symmetry.

This sequence of operations decreases ξi and ξj by ζ and increases ξi′ and ξj ′ by ζ. But as q ≤ r, we
know that ai + aj ≤ ai′ + aj ′ , so the above increases (or keeps constant) the value of ϕ. Thus ϕ ≥ 0, as
required.

    This contradiction completes our proof of Theorem 1.3.3.



4.4      Conclusion and future work

We have removed the n dependence from Theorem 1.3.2 to prove Theorem 1.3.3, and thus shown that for
a fixed number of colours, an arbitrarily large colour-balanced clique contains a perfect matching only a
constant number of edges away from being colour balanced itself. However, there is still a significant gap
in the k-dependence between our result and the bound of Conjecture 1.3.1. The techniques presented here
seem far from reaching a quadratic dependence on k, and so little effort has been expended in optimising
the bound produced. That said, the author believes that it would be interesting to attempt to improve the
upper bound, and investigate whether quadratic dependence on k is truly the correct answer.
    One particular limitation of the techniques explored here is that they will only find a matching which
locally minimises the function g(M ), and cannot directly say anything about the globally optimal match-
ing. Based on this observation, we ask the following question.
118                                                                  Colour-balanced perfect matchings


Question 4.4.1. What is the largest value of f (M ) in terms of k for which there exists a colouring
of K2kn and a perfect matching M for which any small perturbation of M (i.e. performing a swap)
increases the value of f (M )? In particular, can this be significantly larger than the global minimum of
f over all perfect matchings M ?

      In terms of lower bounds, we have not discovered any instance of a colour-balanced clique K with


                             min{f (M ) : M a perfect matching of K} > 2.


However, it is not too hard to see that there are balanced colourings and perfect matchings M forming
local minima for g for which f (M ) = Ω(k 2 ). It is for this reason that we believe it is plausible that
the cases of local and global minima are significantly different, in which case a new approach would be
necessary in order to get close to the true bound.
      A different direction to extend this work would be to consider almost colour-balanced H-factors;
here we have dealt with the case H = K2 . The natural extension in this direction is, in the author’s
opinion, the following question.

Question 4.4.2. For which graphs H on r vertices and m edges is it there a function hH : Z → Z with
the following property?
      For any n and any number k of colours, any colour-balanced c : Krnk → [k] contains an H-factor
F satisfying


                                             f (F ) ≤ hH (k),

                  Pk       −1
where f (F ) :=     i=1 ||c (i) ∩ F | − nm| measures how far F is from being colour-balanced.


      It would appear that approaching the above question requires ideas beyond those presented in this
chapter.
Chapter 5


Rademacher sums

The results presented in this chapter are drawn from a combination of three papers. In particular, the
results presented in Section 1.4.1 are drawn from a paper written in collaboration with Julien Portier
[77]. The results presented in Section 1.4.2, with the exception of Theorem 1.4.7, are drawn from a
paper written in collaboration with Julien Portier and Victor Souza [81]. Finally, Theorem 1.4.7 and the
results presented in Section 1.4.3 are drawn from a paper written in collaboration with Gregory Sorkin
[78].
    Throughout the chapter, ξ1 , ξ2 , . . . , ξn are independent Rademacher random variables, that is, dis-
tributed uniformly on the two-element set {−1, +1}. As discussed in Section 1.4, a Rademacher sum is
of the form ni=1 ξi vi , where vi ∈ Rd for all i. In Section 5.1, we will take d = 1, and normalise so that
            P
Pn      2
   i=1 vi = 1. In Section 5.2 and Section 5.3, we will take d ≥ 2, and will be primarily interested with
the case wherein all vi are unit vectors. We begin by considering anti-concentration of sums weighted
by reals, and then proceed to the case of sums of vectors.



5.1     Anti-concentration of Rademacher sums over R

The main result discussed here is Theorem 1.4.1, concerning anti-concentration of Rademacher sums
over R. Full proofs of the results discussed in this section can be found in [77], and only proof outlines
are presented here. We recall that X is the set of random variables of the form X = ni=1 ai ξi , where
                                                                                        P

ai > 0 are positive reals with ni=1 a2i = 1.
                              P


Theorem 1.4.1. For any x ≥ 0, the infimum


                                        f (x) := inf P(|X| ≥ x)
                                                 X∈X

                                              Pn
is attained by a Rademacher sum of the form            √1 ξi for some n ∈ {1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 7}.
                                                 i=1     n
120                                                                                         Rademacher sums


      We in fact prove Theorem 1.4.1, and its consequence Theorem 1.4.2, via the following, more techni-
cal result.

Theorem 5.1.1. The function f (x) := inf X∈X P(|X| ≥ x) is piecewise constant. More precisely,


      • f (0) = 1,
                                  √
      • f (y) = 1/2 for y ∈ (0, 1/ 7],
                                  √     √
      • f (y) = 29/64 for y ∈ (1/ 7, 1/ 5],
                                √     √
      • f (y) = 3/8 for y ∈ (1/ 5, 1/ 3],
                                √     √
      • f (y) = 1/4 for y ∈ (1/ 3, 2/ 6],
                                 √
      • f (y) = 7/32 for y ∈ (2/ 6, 1],
      • f (y) = 0 for y ∈ (1, ∞).

                                Pn
Equality is witnessed by X =              √1 ξi for n = 1, 2, 7, 5, 3, 6, 1 respectively.
                                    i=1     n


      We now outline the means by which we prove Theorem 5.1.1. In this outline we consider only the
case of the inequality P(|X| ≥ 1) ≥ 7/32, the other cases being treated in a similar way. The first
obstacle towards proving this inequality is that there exist Rademacher sums X satisfying P(|X| > 1) <
7/32, for instance X = ξ1 , or X = 9i=1 ξi /3. We refer to a Rademacher sum with this property as
                                      P

a ‘hard case’. For this reason, the inequality P(|X| ≥ 1) ≥ 7/32 cannot be proven using exclusively
continuous probabilistic tools—such as deducing it from moment bounds on X—as these tools are not
able to distinguish between P(|X| ≥ 1) and P(|X| > 1).
      Our proof expands on the methods of Dvořák and Klein [47] developed for the proof of the weaker
bound P(|X| ≥ 1) ≥ 3/16, which itself used some of the ideas from Keller and Klein [85]. Our
first improvement concerns the efficiency of the key algorithm designed by Dvořák and Klein to obtain
computer-assisted lower bounds on P(|X| > y). This technical improvement enables us to conclude
the desired inequality everywhere except in a small neighbourhood of some hard cases, whereas Dvořák
and Klein’s approach could not obtain good bounds on ai for i ≥ 4. Our second improvement is the
development of new elementary methods to deal with the close neighbourhood of the hard cases, enabling
us to obtain the desired inequality for all cases.


5.1.1     Notation
                                                                                            Pn
Throughout this section, X will be a sum of Rademacher random variables, i.e. X =             i=1 ai ξi , where
ai satisfy a1 ≥ a2 ≥ · · · ≥ an > 0, and each ξi is a random variable picked independently and uniformly
from {−1, +1}. It will be convenient to assume that Var(X) = 1, or equivalently that a21 + · · · + a2n = 1,
so we let X be the set of all such Rademacher sums.
5.1. Anti-concentration of Rademacher sums over R                                                          121


    We will often end up in a situation where we know that, for some k < n, each of the ai (1 ≤ i ≤ k)
lie within some small interval. In such a case, we will write ai = ci + δi , where each ci will be a known
constant, and we will have bounds of the form |δi | ≤ δ for some constant δ and each 1 ≤ i ≤ k.
    Furthermore, in cases like the above, we will in practice always have that c21 + · · · + c2k = 1, and so
we will know that ak+1 , . . . , an will be small. This partition into large and small ai will be represented
by writing X = Yk + Zk , where Yk = a1 ξ1 + · · · + ak ξk and Zk = ak+1 ξk+1 + · · · + an ξn .


5.1.2   Algorithm overview

A key tool in our proof is the use of a computerised bound for G̃(a, x) = inf X∈Xa P(X ≥ x) where Xa
is the set of Rademacher sums of variance 1 satisfying a1 ≤ a.
    We lower bound this function G̃(a, x) by a function D(a, x), which can itself be lower bounded in a
manner which can be efficiently computed. We note here that our bounding function D(a, x) is identical
to that of Dvořák and Klein [47]. A thorough introduction to this function and the tools used to prove
that it is a lower bound for G̃(a, x) can be found in their paper.
    What is new in our method is how we use this lower bound D(a, x) to automatically sift through
cases. The key (yet simple) tool in this automated process is the elimination of variables, by which we
may remove the k largest weights from a Rademacher sum, to be left with only the tail to consider.
Indeed, we may condition on the value of ξ1 to write

                        1                                                                        
         P(X ≥ x) =       P(a2 ξ2 + · · · + an ξn ≥ x − a1 ) + P(a2 ξ2 + · · · + an ξn ≥ x + a1 ) .
                        2

This process may be repeated for ξ2 , . . . , ξk , to produce a sum of 2k terms, each of which may then be
bounded by D(a, x). See Observation 5.1.2 for a precise statement of this elimination of variables.
    With these tools in hand, we may now outline our algorithm. Whereas Dvořák and Klein proceeded
via taking a mesh over possible values for a1 , a2 , a3 , we proceed in an iterative manner. This allows us
to, for instance, skip considering all possible values for a2 , a3 , . . . , ak when we can already conclude our
desired result from the value of a1 alone.
    More precisely, our algorithm first considers which values a1 might take, splitting the interval [0, 1]
into some number d of smaller intervals of width 1/d. For each of these ranges of values of a1 , we
consider eliminating a1 from the sum. In some cases, this allows us to apply our bounding function
D(a, x) and immediately conclude the desired result, which will be of the form P(X ≥ s) ≥ p for some
s and p. We discard all such intervals.
    For each interval which is not discarded, we repeat the process for a2 , considering which values
it may take, eliminating a1 and a2 , and applying our bounding function. We continue this process for
a3 , . . . , ak , for some fixed k. When the algorithm terminates, we will be left with a list of k-tuples of
intervals of values for a1 , . . . , ak which have not been dealt with. By choosing suitably large d and k,
122                                                                                           Rademacher sums


and applying the above algorithm iteratively, we can reduce our problem to a small enough number of
regions that may be checked by hand; these are the neighbourhoods of the ‘hard cases’.


5.1.3     Neighbourhoods of hard cases

The term ‘hard case’ refers to a sequence of ai ’s for which we have P(X ≥ s) ≥ p, whereas P(X >
s) < p. These cases cannot be dealt with by our algorithm, as our bounding function D(a, x) is in fact
also a lower bound for P(X > x). Therefore they must be dealt with by another method.
      Our second improvement on previous work is a general and simple method to deal with these situ-
ations, provided that the neighbourhood is sufficiently small. We do this by means of two key results,
Lemma 5.1.5 and Lemma 5.1.7. More precisely, we let c1 , . . . , ck be the hard case itself, so ai is close
to ci for 1 ≤ i ≤ k. We decompose X as X = Yk + Zk , where Yk = ki=1 ai ξi captures the first k
                                                                           P

terms of X, and Zk = ni=k+1 ai ξi is the tail. Assuming for contradiction that the desired inequality
                      P

P(X ≥ s) ≥ p does not hold, our approach proceeds in two main steps

      • We first apply Lemma 5.1.7 to prove a bound on a linear combination of the ai with i ≤ k, for
        example a1 + a2 + a3 < 1.
      • Leveraging this bound, we show that the variance of the tail Zk is sufficiently large to satisfy the
        conditions of Lemma 5.1.5, which then gives the required bound.

      To give more insights about this method, we illustrate it in the case of the neighbourhood of the
hard case a1 = · · · = a9 = 1/3 for the bound P(X ≥ 1) ≥ 7/64. More precisely, suppose that
ai ∈ [1/3 − δ, 1/3 + δ] for some very small constant δ for i ∈ [9], and define ∆ = maxi∈[9] {|ai − 1/3|}
and ∆3 = maxi∈[3] {|ai − 1/3|}, noting that those two variables are also very small. Assuming for
contradiction that the desired inequality P(X ≥ 1) ≥ 7/64 does not hold, we first use Lemma 5.1.7 to
show that a1 + a2 + a3 < 1.
      In particular, this last inequality shows that a3 (and consequently a4 , . . . , a9 as this sequence is non-
increasing) are all bounded from above by 1/3−∆3 /2. Exploiting this property, elementary computation
shows that the Variance of the tail Z9 = ni=10 ai ξi is of the order of ∆. Setting γ = 9∆, the assumptions
                                        P

to apply Lemma 5.1.5 are met, and we can therefore bound P(X ≥ 1) from below by q1/3 /4 + 3q1 /4 + t,
where qa is the probability that Y9 falls in the interval [a − 9∆, a + 9∆], and t is the probability that Y9
is at least 4/3 − 9∆. It is now easy to get exact values for those quantities, and plugging them in the
expression obtained gives the required lower bound of 7/64.

    The proof of Lemma 5.1.7 runs by assuming for contradiction that i∈I λi ai ≥ s, and then pairing

then pairing elements of R with any leftover elements of T , and finally pairing any remaining elements

of R with other elements of R, some elementary computations allow us to deduce a contradiction, and

    In practice, the key condition which we will need to check when applying the above lemma is (5.1.2).
Note that in every case we will actually apply the lemma, we will take the ci all to be integer multiples

bounded below by 2ck , so it suffices to check that δ ≤ 2ck /(2k).

      In regard to the induction, note that by elimination of a1 we have the following.

                               1           n       a      x−a             a      x + a o

      It follows by induction and (5.1.7) that G̃(a∗ , x) ≥ Di (a∗ , x) for every i. In practice, we compute
Di (a∗ , x) for a∗ ∈ [0, 1] and x ∈ [−3, 3] with granularity of β = 1/2000 (with a∗ starting from 0 and x
starting from −3). We replace (5.1.8) with a discrete variant where arguments of Di+1 are rounded up to
                                                                                          a
a multiple of β, hence underestimating Di+1 , and we also apply this rounding-up to the √1−a2
                                                                                              and the
√x±a arguments. Hence, we only consider a finite set of a ∈ [0, a∗ ] in the infimum at (5.1.8) to compute
 1−a2
Di+1 (a∗ , x), and this results in a dynamic-programming method for computing the Di (a∗ , x)’s. Using
this method, we compute DI (a, x) for I = 1000, we set D(a, x) = DI (a, x), and D(a, x) is the lower
bound we will use for G̃(a, x) in the rest of this chapter.

      Iterative bounding algorithm. As explained in the proof outline, for fixed s, p and k our algorithm
finds sub-boxes of [0, 1]k in which a candidate counterexample to P(X ≥ s) < p must lie. For instance,
in proving Theorem 1.4.2 we would be mostly interested in the case s = 1 and p = 7/64. We now
explain this algorithm in greater detail.
      We first fix some positive integer d and divide up the interval [0, 1] into d intervals of width 1/d. We
then test, using the function D, which of these intervals contain a value for a1 which could be the start of
                                                                                               q
a sequence for which P(X ≥ s) < p. More precisely, for each interval [a1,− , a1,+ ], let σ = 1 − a21,+
be the minimum possible standard deviation of the rest of the Rademacher sum. We then eliminate a1 by
Observation 5.1.2 and check whether

                               1  a1,+ s − a1,−  1  a1,+ s + a1,+ 
                                 D     ,          + D      ,           ≥ p.                            (5.1.9)
                               2    σ      σ       2    σ      σ
5.1. Anti-concentration of Rademacher sums over R                                                             127


    If it is, then we discard this interval [a1,− , a1,+ ]. If it is not, then we reiterate the same process for
a2 : split [0, 1] into intervals and test by elimination of a1 and a2 which of the products [a1,− , a1,+ ] ×
[a2,− , a2,+ ] we can discard. This process is continued until some fixed ak , keeping track of the interval
of values of ai which cannot be ruled out for each i, at which point the process halts.
    To generalise equation (5.1.9) to more variables, assume we are considering a1 , . . . , ar , and in par-
ticular whether they may take values in [a1,− , a1,+ ] × · · · × [ar,− , ar,+ ]. Set


                           q                                               q
                    σ− =       1 − a21,+ − · · · − a2r,+ and σ+ =           1 − a21,− − · · · − a2r,− ,


and assume for now that these are both real. For ζ ∈ {−1, +1}k , we define

Then, noting that the above maximum will take zi = ai,− if ζi = −1 and zi = ai,+ if ζi = +1, we need

We may note that the maximum in equation (5.1.11) will take σ = σ− if s + h(ζ) ≥ 0 and σ = σ+

   However, we run into an issue if ri=1 a2i,− ≤ 1 < ri=1 a2i,+ , as then σ− is not real. (Note that
                                   P                 P

if ri=1 a2i,− > 1 then we may immediately discard the sequence as it contradicts Var(X) = 1.) In

this case, all terms with s + h(ζ) ≥ 0 contribute nothing to the sum, as we cannot bound the remaining

    The above algorithm produces a collection B of k-tuples of intervals such that we have bounds of
the form (a1 , . . . , ak ) ∈ b∈B [b−                    − +
                             S           +                                              Pn
                                    1 , b1 ] × · · · × [bk , bk ] for any candidate X =  i=1 ai ξi satisfying
P(X ≥ s) < p. Note that these bounds are rigorous bounds, and not merely approximations, as all the
functions of the ai ’s we are dealing with in this computation are monotone in the domains we consider
them over. For this reason, in the computation, we always bound ai by the upper bound or lower bound
of their interval, depending on whether the function is increasing or decreasing.
    The final step of our algorithm is to output the bounds on each ai which it can prove. These can then
be fed back into the start of the algorithm: if, for example, we have shown on one run of the algorithm
128                                                                                        Rademacher sums


that 0.24 ≤ a5 ≤ 0.26, then we never need to check values outside this range. This allows us to re-run
the algorithm with a larger value of d, which in turn allows us to produce more precise bounds without
an excessively long computation.
       The precise details of the operation of the code can be found in the code itself [71].


5.1.5      Open problems

Recall that X is the set of all Rademacher sums with variance 1. Keller and Klein [85] asked the follow-
ing.

Problem 5.1.10. Characterise the function F (x) = supX∈X P(X > x), defined for all x ∈ R.

       Note that F (x) = 1 − inf X∈X P(X ≥ −x), so Theorem 5.1.1 answers the question of Keller and
Klein for all negative x. Pinelis [107] gave asymptotic values of F , and some precise values of F have
been determined, but most of the behaviour of F in the positive range remains to be understood. It had
been previously conjectured that for every x, the sup in the definition of F (x) is attained for Rademacher
sums having all of their weights equal. However, this conjecture was disproved by Pinelis [108].
       While it seems that determining F (x) for all large x is beyond the reach of current techniques, it
appears that the function F stays moderately well-behaved for some values of x greater than 1. Indeed,
                                                                        √
Keller and Klein [85, Theorem 1.2] showed that F (x) = 1/4 for x ∈ [1, 2). We make the following
conjecture for larger values of x.
                               √
Conjecture 5.1.11. For x ∈ [ 2, 2], F (x) is characterised as follows.
                             √ √
    • F (x) = 1/8 for x ∈ [ 2, 3),
                              √
    • F (x) = 1/16 for x ∈ [ 3, 2),
       • F (2) = 9/256.

       In a different direction, one can also consider higher-dimensional variants of these problems. Indeed,
Dvořák and Klein [47] defined
                                         Od = inf {P(∥X∥2 ≥ 1)},
                                                X∈Xd

where Xd is the class of random variables that can be written of the form ni=1 vi ξi such that ξi are
                                                                         P

independent Rademacher variables and vi ∈ Rd are vectors such that ni=1 ∥vi ∥2 = 1. In this chapter
                                                                     P

we proved O1 = 7/32, but for general d, it is only known [47] that
                                             p
                                        1−    1 − 1/e2
                                                       ≤ Od ≤ 7/32.
                                               2

Besides the above problems, much remains to be understood in the case of higher-dimensional sums. In
the next section of this chapter, we consider higher-dimensional Rademacher sums, albeit in a different
setting to this section.
5.2. Reverse Littlewood–Offord problems                                                                   129


5.2     Reverse Littlewood–Offord problems

We now consider Rademacher sums weighted with vectors in Rd for some d ≥ 2. The full proofs of
results presented in this section can be found in the paper [81] from which they are drawn. We recall the
following conjecture of Erdős, which is the starting point of the work presented in this section and the
next.

Conjecture 1.4.3 (Erdős). Let x1 , . . . , xn be unit complex numbers. Then the number of sums ni=1 ξi xi
                                                                                               P
                         Pn                                     n
with ξi ∈ {−1, +1} and      i=1 ξi xi ≤ 1 is greater than c2 /n for some absolute constant c > 0.

    We moreover recall that this conjecture is false, as shown (working in R2 ) by taking an odd number
of copies of (1, 0) and of (0, 1), and that the following corrected version of the conjecture was proved by
Beck.

Theorem 1.4.4 (Beck). For any d ≥ 1, there is a constant cd > 0 depending only on d such that the
following holds. Let v1 , . . . , vn be vectors in Rd with ∥vi ∥ ≤ 1 for each 1 ≤ i ≤ n. If ξ1 , . . . , ξn are
independent Rademacher random variables, then

                                                                 √   cd

    Recall that, throughout this chapter, ∥ · ∥ denotes the Euclidean norm on Rd . After rediscovering
Beck’s result for d = 2 with an alternative proof, He, Juškevičius, Narayanan, and Spiro [69] conjectured
that the above theorem of Beck was not the only way to correct Conjecture 1.4.3.

Conjecture 5.2.1 (He, Juškevičius, Narayanan, and Spiro). There is a constant c > 0 such that, for every
n odd and unit vectors v1 , . . . , vn ∈ R2 , we have

                                                                    c
                                     P ∥ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn ∥ ≤ 1 ≥ .
                                                                     n

   In the rest of this section, we give three main results, culminating in a precise description of the
                                                               
phase transition of the function P ∥ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn ∥ ≤ r around r = 1. Our first main result is the
following approximate version of Conjecture 5.2.1.


5.2.1   Radius greater than 1

Our first result is an approximate form of Conjecture 5.2.1, holding for r > 1.

Theorem 1.4.5. For any δ > 0 there is a constant cδ > 0 such that, if n is odd and v1 , . . . , vn ∈ R2 are

Theorem 5.2.2 (Swanepoel). Let n be odd, and let v1 , . . . , vn ∈ R2 be unit vectors. Then there exist
signs η1 , . . . , ηn ∈ {−1, 1} such that

  The proof of Theorem 1.4.6 runs by first finding one particular allocation of signs (ηi )ni=1 such that
∥ ni=1 ηi vi ∥ < 1, and then showing that we can perturb this allocation of signs in an exponentially large

around this semicircle, then the choice of signs ηi = (−1)i+1 has the desired property.

5.2.3     Upper bound at r = 1

The final part of our proof of the “double-jump phase transition” exhibited by this problem is the follow-
ing construction.

Theorem 1.4.7. For every odd integer n, there exist unit vectors v1 , . . . , vn ∈ R2 such that


                                  P ∥ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn ∥ ≤ 1 = 2−⌊n/2⌋ .
                                                                
                                                                                                        (1.4.1)


   The existence of constructions like those in Theorem 1.4.7 is a delicate matter since the value of
                              
P ∥ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn ∥ ≤ 1 has to be atypically small. Indeed, n ≥ 2 and v1 , . . . , vn are selected
independently and uniformly at random from the circle S1 ⊆ R2 , then

                                                                        
                          Ev1 ,...,vn ∈S1 P ∥ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn ∥ ≤ 1 = 1/(n + 1),


a fact that traces back to the work of Rayleigh on ‘random flights’; see Bernardi [21] for a modern and
elementary proof.
5.2. Reverse Littlewood–Offord problems                                                                       133


       The key to the proof of Theorem 1.4.7 is the careful choice of the vectors v1 , v2 , . . . , vn . Indeed, we
let vn = (1, 0), and v2i−1 = v2i = (cos θi , sin θi ), where θi = ci for each i ≤ n/2 and with c a small
constant; c = 1/20 will suffice. Theorem 1.4.7 then follows from the following claim.

Claim 5.2.7. If ∥ ni=1 ηi vi ∥ ≤ 1, then, for all 1 ≤ i ≤ ⌊n/2⌋, we have η2i−1 = −η2i .
                 P


       With the above choice of vi in hand, Claim 5.2.7 then follows by assuming for contradiction that
η2k−1 = η2k for some (minimal) k, and some elementary computations. Indeed, as the arguments of the
points vi decay to zero so quickly, in essence the opposing signs means that v2i−1 and v2i cancel out for
i < k, and are too close to (1, 0) to be significant for i > k.


5.2.4      Double-jump phase transition

For a set of vectors V = {v1 , · · · , vn } ⊆ Rd , denote by σV the random variable


                                            σV := ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn ,


where ξ1 , . . . , ξn are independent Rademacher random variables. Consider the quantity

      The situation seems to be much more complex in higher dimensions. Indeed, it could be tempting

W = {w1 , . . . , wd+1 } centred at the origin such that for every i ∈ [n], we have vi = wj for some
j ∈ [d + 1]. Similarly we say that a set of vectors V = {v1 , . . . , vn } ⊆ Sd−1 is of orthogonal type if
there exists an orthogonal basis W = {w1 , . . . , wd } such that for every i ∈ [n], we have vi = wj for
some j ∈ [d].

or n ̸≡ d (mod 2).

   Even the problem of determining which set of vectors X of orthogonal or of simplicial type minimise

of simplicial type, we could merely show that they are less efficient than vectors of orthogonal type, as
in Theorem 5.2.11.



5.2.6     Open problems

In this section, we have outlined how Conjecture 1.4.3 of Erdős continues to give rise to a rich array of
intriguing phenomena that remain poorly understood.
    Recall that for a set of vectors V = {v1 , · · · , vn } ⊆ Rd , we denote by σV the random variable
σV := ξ1 v1 + · · · + ξn vn where ξ1 , . . . , ξn are independent Rademacher random variables. We recall the
following definition.

We now present several natural questions that arise from our work.
    Firstly, while we know that Fd,r (n) decays exponentially in n, the exact rate of growth remains
elusive. Indeed, while our approach in Theorem 1.4.6 leaves room for improvement, we cannot match

the upper bound in Theorem 1.4.7 at this moment. We leave it an open problem to determine whether

      Although the original Conjecture 1.4.3 of Erdős is false when n is odd, we have shown in Theo-
rem 1.4.5 that an approximate version holds. Equivalently, Theorem 1.4.5 shows that, for each δ > 0,

we obtain depends very poorly on δ. More precisely, we obtain cδ = Ω(δ 2 e−1/δ ) as δ approaches

other words, to determine the behaviour of F2,1+δ (n)/n for n odd and large, as a function of δ > 0.
                                                                                √
    Still in the two-dimensional case, we now consider the case where r = 2 and n may be even

Question 1.4.8 (Refined vector balancing). Let v1 , . . . , vn ∈ Rd be unit vectors with n ̸≡ d (mod 2). Is

      Nevertheless, Question 1.4.8 remains open and would provide a natural extension of the classical

5.3      Vector balancing

In this section, we make some progress towards Question 1.4.8, and outline a proof of the following
theorem.

Theorem 1.4.11. For every integer d there is ε = ε(d) > 0 such that, for any sequence v1 , . . . , vn ∈ Rd
of unit vectors with n ̸≡ d (mod 2), there are signs η1 , . . . , ηn ∈ {−1, +1} such that

                                             n
                                             X                 √
                                                    η i vi ≤    d − ε.                               (1.4.2)
                                             i=1


In particular, we may take ε = 2−100 d−80 .

      A full proof of Theorem 1.4.11 can be found in the paper [78]. For convenience of discussion, we
say that a sequence X = (v1 , . . . , vn ) of vectors in Rd is r-approximating for some r > 0 if, for every
{λi ∈ [−1, 1] : i ∈ [n]}, there are {ηi ∈ {−1, +1} : i ∈ [n]} such that

                                            n
                                            X                     2
                                                   (λi + ηi )xi       ≤ r.
                                             i=1


In other words, if Y is the set of signed sums of X, then every point in the convex hull of Y is within
square-distance r of some point of Y .
      With the above terminology, we may draw the following conclusion from Theorem 1.4.4.

Corollary 5.3.1. Any sequence of vectors v1 , . . . , vn in Rd with ∥vi ∥ ≤ 1 for all i is d-approximating.
5.3. Vector balancing                                                                                   141


    One of the key intermediate results in the proof of Theorem 1.4.11 is a stability version of Corol-
lary 5.3.1, as follows.

Lemma 5.3.2. There is ε0 > 0 such that, for all ε ∈ (0, ε0 ), there is ζ = ζ(ε, d) > 0 such that for all
sequences of unit vectors v1 , . . . , vd+1 ∈ Rd , either

    • (v1 , . . . , vd+1 ) is (d − ε)-approximating, or
    • for all distinct i, j, we have either |⟨vi , vj ⟩| < ζ or |⟨vi , vj ⟩| > 1 − ζ.

In particular, we may take ζ = 18 ε1/4 d4 .

    Indeed, Lemma 5.3.2 presents a dichotomy: either the sequence of vectors leads to better approxima-
tions than the worst case, or every pair of vectors in the sequence is almost parallel or almost orthogonal.
However, Lemma 5.3.2 only applies for sequences of length d + 1, so we also need a way to reduce from
the case of long sequences of vectors to shorter ones. This is achieved with the following “elimination”
lemma.

Lemma 5.3.3. Let V ⊆ Rd be finite, k be an integer, and W ⊆ V satisfy |W | ≥ k + 1 ≥ d + 1. If, for
all Y ⊆ W with |Y | = k, the set (V \ W ) ∪ Y is r-approximating, then V is also r-approximating.

    We will consider Lemma 5.3.3 as a means of “eliminating” vectors: if we assume that some set V is
not r-approximating, and W ⊆ V contains at least k + 1 elements for some k ≥ d, then we may find a
subset of V which is also not r-approximating consisting of all of V \ W and an (adversarially chosen)
subset of W of size k. This allows us to pass from a large set which is not r-approximating to a smaller
one, while preserving some of its structure.
    Indeed, if we are given a sequence of n vectors, and some pair u, w of vectors in the sequence are
ζ-oblique—that is, |⟨u, w⟩| ∈ (ζ, 1 − ζ)—then we may use Lemma 5.3.3 to reduce to the problem of
a sequence of d + 2 vectors, including u and w. Moreover, if there is a second ζ-oblique pair in the
sequence, then we may apply Lemma 5.3.3 again to reduce to the case of d + 1 vectors, including a
ζ-oblique pair, in which case we would be able to conclude from Lemma 5.3.2. Thus we may assume
that u, w is the only pair of ζ-oblique vectors in the sequence.
    In this case, let P be the plane through 0 containing u and v (noting that these vectors are not
parallel). We may then prove that all other vectors are almost orthogonal to the plane P , and thus apply
                                                            √
Corollary 5.3.1 to approximate them with an error of only d − 2, plus the components parallel to P .
As u and v are not too close to orthogonal, we can also produce a better approximation than the worst
case within P , and from this deduce that the sequence of vectors is (d − ε)-approximating.
    The remaining ingredient in the proof of Theorem 1.4.11 is the proof of Lemma 5.3.2, in which the
following lemma is a key ingredient.
142                                                                                               Rademacher sums


Lemma 5.3.4. Given unit vectors x1 , . . . , xd ∈ Rd and reals λ1 , . . . , λd ∈ [−1, 1], there are signs
                                                             √
ξ1 , . . . , ξd ∈ {−1, +1} such that ∥ di=1 (ξi + λi )xi ∥2 ≤ d. In particular, the vectors (x1 , . . . , xd ) are
                                      P

d-approximating. Moreover, the following both hold for all δ > 0.

      • If there are 1 ≤ i < j ≤ d such that |⟨xi , xj ⟩| ≥ δ, then the vectors (x1 , . . . , xd ) are (d − δ 2 )-
        approximating.
      • If |λi | > δ for some i, then the vectors (x1 , . . . , xd ) are (d − δ)-approximating.

      Indeed, Lemma 5.3.4 allows us to reduce to the case in which we have d + 1 vectors, consisting
of d vectors, x1 , . . . , xd , which are pairwise almost orthogonal, and one other vector, y, which is not
close to parallel to any xi . We may then replace x1 , . . . , xd with a genuine orthogonal basis e1 , . . . , ed
(with xi ≈ ei for all i) at the cost of proving a stronger approximation result. If we consider the convex
hull of the set of signed sums of e1 , . . . , ed , y, then Lemma 5.3.4 tells us that the only points which
are not immediately well-approximated by a signed sum are the centres of the hypercubes coming from
e1 , . . . , ed , i.e. the neighbourhoods of the points ±y. However, if we assume without loss of generality
that ⟨y, ei ⟩ > 0 for all i, then the point y (and its neighbourhood) is sufficiently well-approximated by
−y + e1 + · · · + ed , and likewise for −y. Thus the sequence of vectors is (d − ε)-approximating, as
required.
    Theorem 1.4.11 demonstrates that, for any dimension d, the probability P(∥ di=1 ξi vi ∥ ≤ r) is
                                                                                   P
                                                           √
sensitive to the parity of n, particularly for r just below d. However, our results are only a first step
in this direction, and there is much more to be understood. Indeed, as stated in the previous chapter,
Question 1.4.8 is still wide open.
      If an optimal upper bound for Question 1.4.8 were to be discovered, the obvious follow-up question
is to ask how many signed sums must lie at that distance or closer to the origin. In particular, it would
be of great interest as to whether there is a double-jump phase transition like that discovered in [81] (see
the previous section for more details on this phase transition).
      One indication that Question 1.4.8 may be difficult to prove, if true, is the complexity of what would
be the tight examples. Indeed, in the case of d = 3, n = 4, there is a large family of examples of
                                          √
v1 , v2 , v3 , v4 with min∥ 4i=1 ηi vi ∥ = 2: let v1 be parallel to v2 , and v3 be orthogonal to v4 . So long
                           P

as v1 + v2 cannot be well-approximated by ±v3 ± v4 , this is a tight example. This leads to examples for
larger values of n by adding pairs of vectors v2i−1 = v2i = vj for some j ∈ [4]. Thus, it would appear
that the application of a dichotomy results like Lemma 5.3.2 would be significantly more difficult.
      Nevertheless, we are hopeful that more progress could be made; the case of n = d + 1 would seem
to be a particularly appealing starting point.
Chapter 6


Generalisations of the bunkbed conjecture

The work presented in this chapter is based on a paper of which I am the sole author [75].



6.1    Introduction

This chapter concerns the bunkbed conjecture, which had stood as an open problem in percolation theory
since its introduction in 1985 (see [84, Remark 5]). As discussed in Section 1.5.1, the work presented in
this chapter has since been built on to produce a disproof of the bunkbed conjecture itself. We recall the
following key definitions.

Definition 1.5.1 (Bunkbed graph). For Q a graph, digraph, or hypergraph, we can define the bunkbed
structure Q̃ as follows. Take two copies, Q0 and Q1 , of Q, labelling the copy of vertex v and edge e in
Qi as v (i) and e(i) respectively. We will call such edges e(i) horizontal edges. E(Q̃) then consists of
E(Q0 ) ∪ E(Q1 ) along with an edge between v (0) and v (1) for each v ∈ V (Q), which will be called a
vertical edge. In the case of directed graphs, this vertical edge will be bidirectional. Q0 will be called
the lower bunk and Q1 the upper bunk of Q̃.

Definition 1.5.2 (Percolation model). If Q is a graph, digraph, or hypergraph, then a percolation model is
a family E(Q̃) is a family of subsets of E(Q̃). We perform percolation by selecting an element X ∈ E(Q̃)
uniformly at random, and then retain precisely those edges in X. For two vertices x, y ∈ V (Q̃), we write

                                      E(Q̃)                       E(Q̃)
                                 P(x −−−→ y)        or       P(x ←−→ y)


for the probability that there is a directed path of retained edges from x to y in the former, or a bidirec-
tional path in the latter case, in Q̃ under model E(Q̃). When Q is a hypergraph, a path in Q is a sequence
of vertices in which consecutive vertices are both members of some hyperedge in Q.

Definition 1.5.3 (Site percolation model). If G is a graph, then a percolation model E(G̃) is said to be a
site percolation model if, for every X ∈ E(G̃) there is some set U ⊆ V (G̃), which we shall refer to as
144                                                              Generalisations of the bunkbed conjecture


the set of open vertices, such that X consists of precisely those edges with both ends in U .

Definition 1.5.4 (Bunkbed conjecture for a general model E). Let Q be a graph, hypergraph, or digraph,
E(Q̃) be a model of percolation on Q̃ as defined above. The bunkbed conjecture for model E states that
for any Q, and any vertices u, v ∈ V (Q), we have

                                          E(Q̃)                 E(Q̃)
                                  P(u(0) −−−→ v (0) ) ≥ P(u(0) −−−→ v (1) ).


Let BBC(E(Q)) denote the above statement.

      This chapter is dedicated to the proof of Theorem 1.5.6, which we also restate here for convenience.

Theorem 1.5.6. The generalised bunkbed conjecture is false for site percolation, hypergraphs, and di-
graphs. More precisely, there are counterexamples to the statement BBC(E(Q)) for each of the models
S1 (G), S2 (G, T ), H4 (H, T ), H5 (H), D6 (D, T, F ), and D7 (D) to be discussed in Sections 6.3 and 6.4.

      We will prove Theorem 1.5.6 by producing a sequence of counterexamples. We now give an outline
of the rest of this chapter.


6.1.1     Chapter outline

Before diving into our results, we first describe in Section 6.2 the terminology we will use, and also state
and prove a simple lemma concerning a symmetry that we will often make use of.
      In Section 6.3 we then focus on the case of site percolation, and the main aim of this section is to
construct a counterexample to the bunkbed conjecture for site percolation. In pursuit of this goal, we will
need to work with a more complex model of site percolation first, and then translate our counterexample
from this setting to the general case.
      After dealing with site percolation, we then move on to directed graphs and hypergraphs. In Sec-
tion 6.4, we introduce the models of percolation that we will deal with, and then in Section 6.5 we build
the hypergraphs and digraphs which will serve as our counterexamples to various generalisations of the
bunkbed conjecture. Once this section is completed, we will have proved Theorem 1.5.6.
      Finally, in Section 6.6, we discuss potential directions for future work, and state several open prob-
lems.


6.2      Terminology and preliminary results

We now introduce some terminology which we shall use throughout the chapter, and then a lemma which
we will use on several occasions. We now define several useful notions. We will restrict our attention to
models with a particular symmetry that is convenient to work with, but to define this symmetry, we first
define the following function.
6.2. Terminology and preliminary results                                                                  145


Definition 6.2.1 (The flip function). For a graph, hypergraph, or digraph Q, and a set of vertices W ⊆
V (Q), we may define the flip function on W , fW : V (Q̃) ∪ E(Q̃) → V (Q̃) ∪ E(Q̃) as follows, for any
vertex u ∈ V (Q) and (horizontal) edge e ∈ E(Q).
                                                
                                                 u(1−i)
                                                
                                                           if u ∈ W,
                                 fW (u(i) ) =
                                                 u(i)
                                                
                                                           otherwise.
                                                
                                                 e(1−i)
                                                
                                                           if e ∈ E(Q[W ]),
                                 fW (e(i) ) =
                                                 e(i)
                                                
                                                           otherwise,

where Q[W ] is the sub-graph/hypergraph/digraph induced by W .

    Intuitively, the flip function fW flips vertices between the upper and lower bunks of Q̃ on W , and
flips edges between the bunks when they have all their vertices in W . We may note that the flip function
has no effect on vertical edges in Q̃. Finally, note that if P is a subgraph of Q̃, then it is not necessarily
the case that fW [P ] is a well-defined graph: fW [P ] may contain some edge e without containing one of
the vertices of e. For this reason, we will only ever consider flips of sets of vertices or sets of edges, and
never of a mix of vertices and edges.

Definition 6.2.2 (Symmetric percolation models). For a graph, hypergraph, or digraph Q, a set of edges
F ⊆ E(Q̃) and any set of vertices W ⊆ V (Q), the flip of F in W is the set fW [F ].
    Then, a subset A ⊆ E(Q̃) is said to be symmetric if for every F ∈ A and every W ⊆ V (Q), we have
fW [F ] ∈ A. Moreover, we will say that E(Q̃) is symmetric if A = E(Q̃) is symmetric as a subset of
itself.

    In particular, all bond percolation models considered here will be symmetric. However, the site
percolation models that we work with are in fact not symmetric by the above definition, and so we need
another, site-percolation-specific definition of symmetry.

Definition 6.2.3 (Symmetric site percolation models). Given a graph G and site percolation model E(G̃),
take some F ∈ E(G̃) and let U ⊆ V (G̃) be the corresponding set of open vertices. Then for any set
W ⊆ V (G), the site-flip of F in W is the set sfW (F ) of edges with both ends in the set fW [U ].
    A subset A ⊆ E(Q̃) is then said to be site-symmetric if for every F ∈ A and every W ⊆ V (G),
we have sfW (F ) ∈ E(Q̃). We will likewise say that E(Q̃) is itself site-symmetric if it is closed under
site-flips.

    We remark here that one may think of site percolation entirely in terms of sets of open vertices. A
site-flip on W then consists simply of flipping the positions of open vertices between the top and bottom
bunks in W , and leaving them unchanged elsewhere.
    In particular, all of the site percolation models we will work with are site-symmetric. Next, we
146                                                                Generalisations of the bunkbed conjecture


give some precise terminology for switching between paths in the bunkbed structure Q̃ and the original
structure Q.

Definition 6.2.4 (Shadows). Given a path P in the bunkbed structure Q̃, the shadow of P is defined to
be the path P ↓ in Q obtained by replacing vertices v (i) ∈ V (Q̃) with v ∈ V (Q), dropping any repeated
vertices which arise from vertical edges. For instance, in the example shown in Figure 6.1, the shadow
of the path w(0) , x(0) , x(1) , u(1) is w, x, u.
      We may similarly define the shadow W ↓ of an arbitrary set of vertices W ⊆ V (Q̃).

      Finally, we define a type of event which will allow us to use the defining property of symmetric
models.

Definition 6.2.5 (Posts, quasi-posts, and wall events). A vertex x ∈ V (Q) will be called a post in some
F ∈ E(Q̃) if the vertical edge x(0) x(1) is in F . Extending this, we call a vertex x a quasi-post in some
F ∈ E(Q̃) if either x is a post in F , or one can find a post y and paths P, P ′ in Q from x to y and y to x
                                                                                                            F
respectively such that every edge e in P or P ′ has both e(0) ∈ F and e(1) ∈ F (and so x(0) ←
                                                                                            → x(1) and
x behaves as if it were a post). Note that if Q is not directed then we may take P = P ′ .
      An event B ⊆ E(Q̃) is then said to be a (u, v)-wall event for vertices u, v ∈ V (Q) if B is either
                                                                     (i )     (i )          (i )           (i )   (i   )
symmetric or site-symmetric, and, for every F ∈ B, every path x1 1 , x2 2 , . . . , xk k with (xj j , xj+1
                                                                                                        j+1
                                                                                                            )
contained in some e ∈ F for all j, and with x1 = u and xk = v, has some j such that xj is a quasi-post.
      For instance, in the example shown in Figure 6.1, vertex z is a post, and vertices y and v are both
                                                              F
quasi-posts (but w is not, despite the fact that w(0) ←
                                                      → w(1) ), and thus F can be an element of a (u, v)-
wall event.

      Note that a quasi-post in a site percolation model is simply a post, as x ∈ V (G) being a quasi-post
requires that both x(0) and x(1) are open.

                                                    z (1)                                          v (1)

6.2. Terminology and preliminary results                                                                 147


    We now make two observations about quasi-posts, and then prove a simple lemma that we will make
use of on several occasions.

Observation 6.2.6. If v is a quasi-post in some F ∈ E(Q̃) for a (site-)symmetric model E(Q̃), then v
remains being a quasi-post after applying any (site-)flip to F .

Observation 6.2.7. If v is a quasi-post in some F ∈ E(Q̃) for a (site-)symmetric model E(Q̃), then
there is a path P from v (0) to v (1) consisting of edges in F such that every vertex P passes through is a
quasi-post, and every edge e ∈ P ↓ has e(0) ∈ F and e(1) ∈ F .

    Indeed, Observation 6.2.6 and Observation 6.2.7 both follow immediately from noting that the paths
P and P ′ in the definition of a quasi-post pass only through quasi-posts and are unaffected by any flip.

Lemma 6.2.8. Let Q be a graph, hypergraph, or digraph, and u, v ∈ V (Q) be two vertices, and let A
be a (u, v)-wall event. Then

                                       E(Q̃)                      E(Q̃)
                               P(u(0) −−−→ v (0) |A) = P(u(0) −−−→ v (1) |A).


Proof. We follow an argument similar to the ‘mirror argument’ used by Linusson [95, Lemma 2.3], but
in a slightly more general setting. Define the sets A(0) and A(1) by

                                                              F
                                      A(i) := {F ∈ A : u(0) −
                                                            → v (i) }.


We need to show that |A(0) | = |A(1) |. To this end, we demonstrate that there are injections in both
directions between these families of sets.
    Take some F ∈ A(i) , and define W to be the set of those vertices x ∈ V (Q) such that there is a walk
P in Q̃ satisfying all of the following.

    • There is some j ∈ {0, 1} such that P is a walk from x(j) to v (i) ,
    • all edges of P are in F , and
    • no vertex of P ↓ \ {v} is a quasi-post.

In the case when Q is a graph, W is thus the shadow of the connected component of v (i) in


                           Q̃ \ {w(j) : w ̸= v, w is a quasi-post, j ∈ {0, 1}}.


    In particular, it is immediate for any Q that all vertices of P except perhaps v (i) are in the same bunk
of Q̃, and every vertex y ∈ W has a path to v entirely contained in W . Furthermore, as A is a (u, v)-wall
                      / W ↓.
event, we have that u ∈
    It follows that there must be some path P in F from u(0) to v (i) such that P ↓ passes from outside of
W to inside W exactly once, with the final vertex x of P ↓ ∩ (V (Q) \ W ) being a quasi-post. We will
148                                                              Generalisations of the bunkbed conjecture


only make use of the fact that P is a walk. Define the set X as


                                  X := W ∪ {x ∈ V (Q) : x is a quasi-post}.


      Let F ′ := fX [F ] be the flip of F on X, and P ′ be fX [E(P )]. However, if P contains adjacent
quasi-posts outside of W , then P ′ need not be (the set of edges of) a walk. However, the only edges P ′
could be missing are vertical edges between x(0) and x(1) for x a quasi-post. In this case, we can apply
Observation 6.2.7 to insert paths between any such pair of vertices, producing a walk P ′′ from u(0) to
v (1−i) .
      Note that in the case that A is site-symmetric, every vertex in X adjacent to a vertex not in X must be
a post, so flips and site-flips in X coincide. Thus F ′ is in A(1−i) , as (u, v)-wall events are by definition
(site-)symmetric.
      Note that if we apply the same argument as above to F ′ , the existence of the walk P ′′ demonstrates
that we produce the same set W as we did for F , as by Observation 6.2.6, the set of quasi-posts is
unchanged by applying a flip to F . Thus the map sending F to F ′ is invertible, i.e. an injection, and so
|A(0) | = |A(1) |, as required.


      Throughout the chapter, we will refer to models as either ‘conditioned’ or ‘unconditioned’. While
this is not precise terminology, it should be taken to mean the following. In the unconditioned cases, our
model will be equivalent to one in which every edge (or vertex, in the case of site percolation) is retained
independently with probability 1/2. The conditioned cases then have further restrictions that move us
away from this situation. We now put our terminology and result to use in resolving the case of site
percolation.



6.3         Site percolation

The goal of this section is to show that the bunkbed conjecture is false for site percolation. We consider
the case of site percolation before moving on to the other structures (hypergraphs and digraphs) for two
purposes: for ease of notation, as we are in this section concerned only with ordinary graphs, and to
introduce our counterexamples in a more structured manner, as the results of Section 6.4 are built on top
of the results of this section. In the framework introduced in Sections 6.1 and 6.2, the site percolation we
consider comes in two flavours.


Model 6.3.1 (S1 (G); unconditioned site percolation). For an undirected graph G, let the set of open
vertices W ⊆ V (G̃) be chosen uniformly at random among all subsets of vertices. We then retain
precisely those edges with both endpoints in W .
6.3. Site percolation                                                                                        149


    In order to construct a counterexample to the bunkbed conjecture for site percolation, we first con-
sider a conditioned form of site percolation, in which we fix which vertices are to be posts. We also
condition further, insisting that among those v ∈ V (G) which are not posts, precisely one of the vertices
v (0) and v (1) is open.

Model 6.3.2 (S2 (G, T ); conditioned site percolation). For an undirected graph G and subset T ⊆ V (G),
a set of open vertices is selected uniformly at random from all sets of the following form. If v ∈ T , both
v (0) and v (1) are open, and for v ∈
                                    / T precisely one of v (0) and v (1) is open, and the other is closed.

    We now construct our counterexamples for site percolation, beginning with the case of conditioned
site percolation defined as Model 6.3.2.


6.3.1    Conditioned site percolation

We start by giving the construction of the counterexample to the bunkbed conjecture for model S2 (G, T )
as defined in Model 6.3.2. Indeed, this graph G2 is shown in Figure 6.2.

                                                     v1


                                        v2                            v5



                                             v3              v4



                                             v6
                                                           v7

                                               v9                      v8

Figure 6.2: The basic counterexample, G2 . Vertices v2 , v7 , v8 form the set T2 conditioned to have vertical
edges, and are shown highlighted with extra circles.

    Before proceeding to the proof that the graph G2 in Figure 6.2 is indeed a counterexample to the
bunkbed site percolation conjecture, we first make a brief remark about its discovery.

Remark 6.3.3. The vertices v8 and v9 in G2 serve as a gadget, forcing any vertex adjacent to v8 (in this
case, only v5 ) to be unreachable. More precisely, note that v9 has a unique neighbour, v6 , and consider
removing the vertices v8 and v9 to form a graph G′ , and making v6 the target vertex. We claim that
the site percolation bunkbed conjecture holding in G is equivalent to it holding in G′ with the added
                                                                (0)                                   (0)
constraint that no G-neighbour of v8 is reachable from v1 . Indeed, if there is a path from v1               to a
              (0)   (1)                                                  (0)   (0)       (1)
neighbour of v8 or v8 , then as v8 is a post, then there is a path from v1 to v8 and to v8 , and thus
150                                                               Generalisations of the bunkbed conjecture


to v9 , in whichever bunk it occupies. By symmetry of whether v9 is in the upper or lower bunk, these
situations may be disregarded, and so we are left to consider the case in which no neighbour of v8 can be
                 (0)
reached from v1 .

      The following result demonstrates that BBC(S2 (G2 , T2 )) is false.

Proposition 6.3.4. If G2 is the graph shown in Figure 6.2 with T2 := {v2 , v7 , v8 }, then we have

                         (0) S2 (G2 ,T2 )   (0)              (0) S2 (G2 ,T2 )   (1)
                       P(v1 ←−−−−−→ v9 ) = 12/64 < P(v1 ←−−−−−→ v9 ) = 13/64


Proof. We prove Proposition 6.3.4 by exhaustive enumeration of cases. A diagram of G2 is repeated
below for convenience. We first give three conditions to reduce the size of the space we must enumerate.

                                                           (0)
      • As we care about connection probability from v1 , both probabilities in question are 0 if v1 is
        present in the upper bunk.
      • As v9 is present in only one of the two bunks, we may condition on the rest of the graph, and then
        see in which of the upper or lower bunk v9 would be reachable.
      • By Remark 6.3.3, if v5 is in the same position (upper or lower) as v1 , then there is necessarily a
        path in G̃1 with shadow v1 → v5 → v8 → v9 , whether v9 is in the upper or lower bunk; the two
        probabilities in question are thus both equal to 1/2 in this case (after conditioning on v1 being in
        the lower bunk). Thus it suffices to consider the cases in which v5 is in the upper bunk.

      We may thus see that both probabilities in question are equal to 1/8 plus 1/4 times the respective
values they take upon conditioning that v1 is in the lower bunk and v5 is in the upper.
      There are three vertices left to consider: v3 , v4 , and v6 . We enumerate the eight possible configura-
tions of these vertices, and in which states v9 can be reached, in Figure 6.3.
      As can be seen, the number of configurations in which v9 is reached in the upper bunk exceeds the
number in which v9 is reached in the lower bunk, thus proving Proposition 6.3.4; the precise probabilities
arise as 1/8 + 5/64 and 1/8 + 4/64 respectively.



      We will use this graph to produce counterexamples to the bunkbed conjecture for the other models.
First, we remove the conditioning.


6.3.2     Unconditioned site percolation

We now use graph G2 to build a counterexample for Model 6.3.1. To prove this result, we shall go via
an intermediate model, which we will denote by S3 (G, T ).

Model 6.3.5 (S3 (G, T ); semi-conditioned site percolation). For an undirected graph G and subset T ⊆
V (G), define T± := {x(0) , x(1) : x ∈ T }. Let W ⊆ V (G̃) \ T± be chosen uniformly at random amongst
all such subsets. Then the vertices W ∪ T± are set to be open, and all other vertices are closed.

    Note that in model S3 (G, T ), vertices x ∈
                                              / T may or may not be posts, depending on whether or not
both x(0) and x(1) are open. We are now ready to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 6.3.6. There is a graph G1 such that BBC(S1 (G1 )) is false.

Proof. We construct G1 by taking G2 and blowing up each of the three vertices in T2 to an empty graph
on k vertices, for some k to be decided later.
    Let Uv2 , Uv7 , and Uv8 be the sets, each of k vertices, in G1 produced by the blow-ups of vertices
v2 , v7 , and v8 respectively. Let A be the event that for each v ∈ T2 , there is some x ∈ Uv such that x(0)
and x(1) are both open, so edge x(0) x(1) is retained.
    Note first that, conditional on A, for each v ∈ T2 , the set Uv behaves exactly the same way as the
post v in model S3 (G2 , T2 ). Thus for any x, y ∈ V (G2 ) \ T2 ⊆ V (G1 ),

                                       S1 (G1 )                  S3 (G2 ,T2 )
                             P(x(i) ←−−−→ y (j) |A) = P(x(i) ←−−−−−→ y (j) ).


    Next, let B be the event that some vertex x in V (G2 ) \ T2 ⊆ V (G1 ) has either both x(0) and x(1)
open (so vertical edge x(0) x(1) is retained), or neither are open. Then, noting that there is a unique path
in G2 from v1 to v9 avoiding T2 , we see that A ∩ B is a (v1 , v9 )-wall event for model S3 (G2 , T2 ): if x(0)
and x(1) are both open, then x is a post. If neither are open, then any path from v1 to v9 must avoid x,
and thus hit T2 . Therefore, by Lemma 6.2.8,

                             (0) S1 (G1 )     (1)            (0) S1 (G1 )       (0)
                         P(v1 ←−−−→ v9 |A, B) = P(v1 ←−−−→ v9 |A, B).

for all i, j ∈ {0, 1}. We therefore find that

6.4       Models

We consider a total of four further models in addition to those discussed in Section 6.3, two for each of
the cases of hypergraphs and directed graphs. Following the structure of the proofs in Section 6.3, in
each case we will consider first a conditioned model, and then an unconditioned version. In particular,
in all cases, the conditioned model on a structure Q (hypergraph or digraph) will have precisely one of
the (hyper)edges e(0) , e(1) retained for each edge e ∈ E(Q).
      We will first consider hypergraphs, and now define the models we will consider.
6.5. Construction of counterexamples                                                                  153


Model 6.4.1 (H4 (H, T ); conditioned hypergraphs). For a hypergraph H and subset T ⊆ V (H), let
H4 (H, T ) be the set of those F ⊆ E(H̃) containing precisely one of e(0) and e(1) for each e ∈ E(H),
and precisely those vertical edges v (0) v (1) for which v ∈ T .

    It was conjectured by Linusson [95] that a version of the bunkbed conjecture should also hold on
hypergraphs. In our notation, the conjecture is as follows.

Conjecture 6.4.2 ([95] Conjecture 2.7). For any hypergraph H and set T ⊆ V (H) of posts, BBC(H4 (H, T ))
holds. That is, for any vertices u, v ∈ V (H), we have

                                       H4 (H,T )                   H4 (H,T )
                              P(u(0) ←−−−−→ v (0) ) ≥ P(u(0) ←−−−−→ v (1) ).


    Note that Theorem 1.5.6, once proved, will disprove this conjecture. Similarly to in the site per-
colation case, we will use our results concerning Model 6.4.1 to prove a result about an unconditioned
model.

Model 6.4.3 (H5 (H); unconditioned hypergraphs). For a hypergraph H, let F ⊆ E(H̃) be chosen
uniformly at random amongst all such subsets. Keep precisely these edges, and discard the others.

    Finally, we consider digraphs, and, following the same pattern as before, have two models.

Model 6.4.4 (D6 (D, T, F ); conditioned digraphs). For a digraph D, subset T ⊆ V (D) of vertices, and
subset F ⊆ E(D) of (directed) edges, let D6 (D, T, F ) be the family of those subsets of E(D̃) containing
both e(0) and e(1) for all e ∈ F , exactly one of e(0) and e(1) for all e ∈ E(D) \ F , and precisely those
vertical edges v (0) v (1) for which v ∈ T .

    It was conjectured by Leander [94] that the bunkbed conjecture holds for acyclic directed graphs (in
the same sense as discussed here), and it was furthermore stated that this should broaden to hold for any
directed graph. The latter is disproved by Theorem 1.5.6, but the example we construct has many cycles,
and so it remains open whether the conjecture holds for acyclic directed graphs.
    We can now remove the conditioning from this model, but at the cost of working with multigraphs.

Model 6.4.5 (D7 (D); unconditioned digraphs). For a directed multigraph D, let F ⊆ E(D̃) be chosen
uniformly at random amongst all such subsets. Keep precisely these edges, and discard the others.

    As stated in T heorem 1.5.6, all of BBC(H4 (H, T )), BBC(H5 (H)), BBC(D6 (D, T, F )), and BBC(D7 (D))
are false for suitable hypergraphs H, digraphs D, subsets of vertices T and subsets of edges F . In the
next section, we give constructions of such graphs.


6.5      Construction of counterexamples

We now construct the counterexamples for our models of percolation on hypergraphs and digraphs.
154                                                                       Generalisations of the bunkbed conjecture


6.5.1     Hypergraphs

We first construct a hypergraph H4 and subset T4 ⊆ V (H4 ) of its vertices which contradicts the bunkbed
conjecture for model H4 (H4 , T4 ). In fact, a small modification of the hypergraph dual of G2 suffices,
and the resulting hypergraph H4 is shown in Figure 6.5.

                                                           u1



                                   u2                                             u3
                                                    u5           u6

                                   u4
                                                                u7
                                   u8                                             u9



                                                           u10

Figure 6.5: The basic hypergraph counterexample, H4 . All hyperedges have size 3, and shown by shaded
grey triangles. Vertices u2 , u7 , u9 are conditioned to have vertical edges, and are shown highlighted with
extra circles.

      While it is not too tedious to manually check that BBC(H4 (H4 , T4 )) is false, we now deduce this
from the previous results. Indeed, the hypergraph H4 is constructed in the following manner:

      • Let H ′ be the hypergraph dual of G2 ; that is, V (H ′ ) = E(G2 ), and for each v ∈ V (G2 ), we add a
        hyperedge ev to E(H ′ ), connected to the vertices of H ′ formed from those edges e ∈ E(G2 ) such
        that v ∈ e.
      • Collapse the edges in H ′ which came from posts in G2 down to single points to form H ′′ , and
        again condition these vertices to be posts, as shown in Figure 6.5. Let T4 = {u2 , u7 , u9 } be this
        set of posts.
      • Add two extra vertices to H ′′ , labelled u1 and u10 in edges ev1 and ev9 respectively, to form H4 .
        These will be the new start and end vertices for our percolation.

      The following claim then verifies that BBC(H4 (H4 , T4 )) is false.

Claim 6.5.1. For G2 and H4 as shown in Figure 6.2 and Figure 6.5 respectively, for all i, j ∈ {0, 1},

                                 (i) H4 (H4 ,T4 )    (j)             (i) S2 (G2 ,T2 )   (j)
                             P(u1 ←−−−−−→ u10 ) = P(v1 ←−−−−−→ v9 ).                                        (6.5.1)


Proof. Upon switching to the hypergraph dual H ′ of G2 , collapsing ev2 , ev7 , and ev9 down to points and
6.5. Construction of counterexamples                                                                                 155


conditioning that they be posts, and percolating on edges rather than vertices, the model H4 (H4 , T4 ) is
almost identical to S2 (G2 , T2 ). Indeed, for any x, y ∈ V (G2 ) \ T2 , we have

                                       S2 (G2 ,T2 )                   H4 (H4 ,T4 )
                                 P(x ←−−−−−→ y) = P(ex ←−−−−−→ ey ).


We then add vertices u1 and u10 to measure connections between ev1 and ev9 respectively within the
confines of model of BBC(H4 (H4 , T4 )).
    Thus it is immediate that equality (6.5.1) holds, and so BBC(H4 (H4 , T4 )) is false for T4 = {u2 , u7 , u9 }
and percolation from u1 to u10 .

    Our next task is to generalise the construction of H4 to produce a hypergraph H5 for which the
unconditioned statement BBC(H5 (H5 )) is false.
    We may prove this following similar lines to the case of site percolation; we attach gadgets to vertices
in T4 which force those vertices to act as if they were conditioned to be posts with probability close to
1. We then apply Lemma 6.2.8 to show that we are often in a situation equivalent to model H4 (H4 , T4 ).
We now present this argument in detail.

6.5.2     Directed graphs

Finally, we consider directed graphs. We produce the digraph D6 from hypergraph H4 , and then construct
