6.6      Concluding remarks and open problems

We have shown that the bunkbed conjecture does not hold when generalised to site percolation or hyper-
graphs. The situation with directed graphs is a little more complex, as we have only dealt with the case
of multigraphs with vertical edges which are bidirectional. To be precise, the directed edges v (0) v (1) and
v (1) v (0) are either both retained or both removed. In the interest of considering fully directed graphs
(wherein e.g. v (0) v (1) can be retained and v (1) v (0) removed simultaneously), we define the following
model:
158                                                             Generalisations of the bunkbed conjecture


Model 6.6.1 (D8 ; fully directed graphs). For a simple digraph D, construct the bunkbed graph D̃ by
adding both directed edges v (0) v (1) and v (1) v (0) for all v ∈ V (D). A subset of edges F ⊆ E(D̃) is
selected uniformly at random, and precisely the edges in F are retained.

      We conjecture that it is not necessary that our example in Section 6.5.2 has multiple edges and
undirected vertical edges.

Conjecture 6.6.2. There is a simple digraph D for which BBC(D8 , D) is false.

      We furthermore repeat the following question of Leander [94] in our context.

Question 6.6.3. Do BBC(D6 (D, T, F )) and BBC(D7 (D)) hold for all acyclic digraphs D?

      Since this chapter was released online as a paper, Question 6.6.3 has been resolved by Przybylowski
[110] by means of a counterexample, based on the graphs presented here.
      However, one case that does seem approachable, though was found by the author to be more difficult
than expected, is expressed by the following conjecture.

Conjecture 6.6.4. BBC(D7 (Tn )) holds for Tn , the transitive tournament on n vertices.

      We remark here that, while it may seem natural to apply the same techniques as used for the (undi-
rected) complete graph (e.g. in [40, 118]), these do not directly apply, in short because Tn does not have
the same symmetries as Kn .
      It would be interesting to consider whether the bunkbed conjecture holds after restricting to various
classes of graphs, with a view towards ascertaining whether or not the bunkbed conjecture failing for site
percolation is in general the exception or the rule. Indeed, one may ask the following (perhaps optimistic)
question.

Question 6.6.5. What is the probability that the assertion in the bunkbed conjecture for site percolation
BBC(S1 (G)) holds for G ∼ G(n, p) the Erdős-Rényi random graph for n large? To what extent does
this probability depend on the behaviour of p as a function of n?

      There are of course many other classes of graph one could consider; graphs of diameter 2 (or simply
of radius 1), and highly symmetric graphs (e.g. those with transitive automorphism group) would seem
to be natural candidates for investigation.
      Returning now to the original bunkbed conjecture BBC(E(G)), as was discussed in Section 1.5.1, the
work presented here has since been used by Gladkov, Pak and Zimin [63] to construct a counterexample
to the bunkbed conjecture. This has constituted a rapid reversal of the direction of the previous literature,
which had been almost entirely dedicated to attempts to prove that the bunkbed conjecture true.
      Now that the conjecture is known to be false in general, it is not immediately clear what the next
direction for work in the area should be. One question to ask is for which graphs the bunkbed conjecture
6.6. Concluding remarks and open problems                                                             159


is in fact true; there are several highly symmetric graphs for which the conjecture is still unknown. Grid
graphs are raised as a case of interest in [63], and we add to this the following conjecture.

Conjecture 6.6.6. The bunkbed conjecture BBC(E0 (Qd , T )) holds for the graph Qd and any subset
T ⊆ v(Qd ) and any integer d, where Qd is the hypercube of dimension d.

    Moreover, even the simpler version of Conjecture 6.6.6, wherein one does not condition on a set
T of posts and takes a subset of the edges of Qd uniformly at random is—to the best of the author’s
knowledge—also open. Indeed, this is equivalent to the appealing statement that the connection proba-
bility in uniform bond percolation on the hypercube is decreasing in the distance.
    A natural generalisation of Conjecture 6.6.6, which also encompasses many other cases of interest,
is the following question.

Question 6.6.7. Does the bunkbed conjecture BBC(E0 (G, T )) hold for any Cayley graph G and subset
T ⊆ V (G)?

    While Question 6.6.7 appears natural, it seems (as appears to be the case with many problems in this
area) that a solution would require some significant new ideas.
Chapter 7


Long cycles in percolated vertex expanders

The contents of this chapter is based on a paper of which I am the sole author [76].



7.1    Introduction

This chapter is concerned with finding long cycles within percolated graphs with certain vertex-expansion
properties. We first recall the following definition, which is the vertex-expansion condition we work with,
introduced by Collares, Diskin, Erde, and Krivelevich [38].

Definition 1.6.2. A graph G is an ({k}, d)-vertex-expander if every S ⊆ V (G) with |S| = k has
|N (S)| ≥ dk.

   We also recall the main theorem of this chapter, which resolves a conjecture of [38].

Theorem 1.6.3. Fix d > 1 and ε ≥ ε0 (d) > 0 such that ε ≤ 1/8, let k ∈ N satisfy k ≤ ε−2 d, and let
the graph G be an ({k}, d)-vertex-expander. Then, if p = (1 + ε)/d, then Gp contains a cycle of length
at least Ωε (kd) with probability at least 1 − exp(−Ωε (k/d)).

   The rest of this chapter is mostly dedicated to the proof of Theorem 1.6.3. We will in fact prove the
slightly more general Theorem 7.2.1, which contains more precise information about the dependencies
and constants involved. Where relevant, we use the same notation as [38]. For a broad introduction to
random graphs, we refer to the textbooks [22, 58].
   Our proof of Theorem 1.6.3 will be outlined in Section 7.1.1. We state one standard probabilis-
tic result that we need in Section 7.1.2. Then, in Section 7.2, we state a more technical version of
Theorem 1.6.3 containing all of the dependencies we will prove. In Section 7.2.1 we then provide a
description of the modified DFS algorithm we will employ, along with a collection of terminology and
notation which we will use in our proof. The proof of Theorem 1.6.3 is contained in Section 7.3. Finally,
in Section 7.4 we suggest some directions for future work.
7.1. Introduction                                                                                      161


7.1.1   Proof outline

As is the case with much of the work in this area, the proof of Theorem 1.6.3 runs by careful analysis of
a modified depth-first search (DFS) algorithm. The precise details of the algorithm will be discussed in
Section 7.2.1, but the idea of the proof is as follows.
    We will use sprinkling, writing p = 1 − (1 − p1 )(1 − p2 ) with p1 ≈ p large enough so that dp1 > 1,
but also far enough from p that p2 is not too much smaller than p. We expose edges with probability p1
for running the DFS, and then expose edges in a second round with probability p2 . This second round
will reveal a few more edges, with which we are likely to find a long cycle. Indeed, we seek an edge xy
of G such that there is a long path between x and y in Gp1 ; in fact, we find many of these edges, so that
it is likely that one of them is present in Gp2 .
    The standard DFS algorithm processes vertices of the graph Gp in some order, querying whether
edges in G are also present in Gp one at a time. The set F of edges which the DFS algorithm has found
to lie in Gp is a forest and, as the algorithm runs, it keeps track of a stack A, which is a path in Gp .
Following the techniques of [38], we know that A will likely grow at least to the scale of dk, the same
scale at which we seek a cycle. Indeed, we call an edge xy of G long if there is a path of length at least
αkd from x to y in Gp1 for some constant α. We will show that, under the assumption that there are few
long edges, the path A is likely to grow at (at least) a constant rate (i.e. roughly a constant proportion
of the vertices in F are in A), which will yield a contradiction as the path cannot continue growing
indefinitely inside the finite graph G.
    To be more precise, we will process the vertices in “blocks” of k vertices, and the fact that the path
continues growing will follow by an inductive argument, which we now outline. While the path continues
growing in the above sense, we will be able to effectively bound the number of vertices close (in Gp1 )
to the end of the path, and show that the final block has few neighbours (in Gp1 ) amongst the processed
vertices in previous blocks (to avoid producing a long edge). Thus many neighbours (in G) of the final
block of k vertices of the path will have to be in the unexplored set U , and there will be enough of these
that, with high probability, many of them are also neighbours in Gp1 .
    However, we have no control over the size of the graph G, which may in particular be far larger than
k. Thus we cannot hope that, for each block, the set of consecutive queries in the DFS algorithm made
while that block was being processed will have roughly the expected number of positive answers, even as
k grows. To deal with the situations in which we get far fewer positive responses to queries than expected
(resulting in a “bad” block), we reserve a collection of “safe” vertices, which are spread far apart along
A. When we run into a bad block, we abandon the end of the path, and return to a safe vertex, restarting
the DFS algorithm from there. We show that the probability of a block being bad is exponentially small,
and so the number of bad blocks is far smaller than the number of safe vertices, and so the algorithm can
continue.
162                                                            Long cycles in percolated vertex expanders


7.1.2     Concentration inequalities

We will use the following Chernoff-type bound on the tail of the binomial distribution. For more details,
see e.g. [58, Section 27.4].

Theorem 7.1.1. If X is a binomial random variable with mean µ > 0, then for every 0 ≤ t ≤ µ/2,

                                                            t2 
                                     P(|X − µ| > t) < 2 exp −     .
                                                              3µ


7.2      Technical result and algorithm

We will in fact deduce Theorem 1.6.3 from the following result, which contains explicit dependencies
and constants.

Theorem 7.2.1. Fix positive real numbers d > 1 and ε ∈ [300d−1/2 , 1/8] such that this interval is
nonempty, and let k ∈ N satisfy k ≥ ε−2 d. Let G be a graph on at least k vertices such that every
S ⊆ V (G) with |S| = k satisfies |N (S)| ≥ kd and let p = (1 + 3ε)/d. Then, the probability that Gp
contains a cycle of length at least ε2 kd/100 is at least 1 − 2 exp(−ε2 k/200d).

      It is immediate that Theorem 7.2.1 implies Theorem 1.6.3. Indeed, other than replacing ε by 3ε
(which is for notational convenience), Theorem 1.6.3 can be deduced from Theorem 7.2.1 by simply
hiding the various constants in asymptotic notation.
      We now present the DFS algorithm which we will use to produce a long cycle along with a number
of definitions which we will use in our analysis of the algorithm.


7.2.1     Depth-first search algorithm

We use a modified DFS algorithm in our proof. While DFS algorithms are standard, our modifications
are significant enough that we will fully describe the algorithm. When appropriate, we align our notation
with that in [38].
      The DFS algorithm tracks five sets which update over time, as follows.

      • W (t), the processed vertices,
      • A(t), the active vertices,
      • U (t), the unprocessed vertices,
      • S(t), the safe vertices, and
      • T (t), the trashed vertices.

      We assume throughout the proof that there is a total order on the vertex set V of G, and so we may
talk about considering vertices “in order”. Both A and S are treated as stacks, following a last-in first-out
protocol. We will refer to the vertex most recently added to A as the head or top of A. Throughout the
7.2. Technical result and algorithm                                                                           163


discussion of the algorithm, we will say that we query an edge e of G to mean that we reveal whether or
not e is present in Gp . If e is in Gp , we will say that the query gave a positive result, or, if e is not present
in Gp , that it gave a negative result. When the algorithm starts, at t = 0, all vertices are unprocessed.
Formally,
                            W (0) = A(0) = S(0) = T (0) = ∅          and    U (0) = V.

The DFS process is then run in steps indexed by t until both A(t) and U (t) are empty. We define our
algorithm in two parts, and, as A(t) and W (t) may be updated by both the first part and the second part
of the algorithm, we first produce Â(t+1) and Ŵ (t+1), and then in the second part produce the updated
sets A(t + 1) and W (t + 1). If unspecified, the values of the sets W , A, U , S, and T carry over from step
t to t + 1 unchanged. Step t + 1 of the basic algorithm is as follows; we will add some further conditions
in due course.


    • If A(t) is empty and U (t) is non-empty, then move the first vertex v from U (t) to A(t), i.e.
       Â(t + 1) = A(t) ∪ {v} and U (t + 1) = A(t) \ {v}. Set Ŵ (t + 1) = W (t).
    • Otherwise, let v be the top vertex of A(t). For each neighbour u of v in U (t) in turn, query the
       edge uv if it has not already been queried, and continue until either a query returns a positive result
       or all edges from v to U (t) have been queried.
          – If uv is found to exist for some u ∈ U (t), then move u from U to the top of A, so U (t + 1) =
             U (t) \ {u} and Â(t + 1) = A(t) ∪ {u}. Set Ŵ (t + 1) = W (t).
          – If all edges are queried and no positive result is found, then move v from A to W , i.e. put
             Â(t + 1) = A(t) \ {v} and Ŵ (t + 1) = W (t) ∪ {v}.


    When running queries, for ease of analysis we will use the following method of sampling. Let Xi,j be
independent Bernoulli random variables with parameter p for all i, j ∈ N. Sample an infinite sequence
X1,1 , X1,2 , . . . which will be used to determine responses to queries in steps 1, 2, . . . , t1 (noting that
there may be multiple queries per step), and then another infinite sequence X2,1 , X2,2 , . . . for queries in
steps t1 + 1, t1 + 2, . . . , t2 , and so on.
    We will refer to the vertices processed at times in the range [ti−1 + 1, ti ] as a block, and let Bi be the
set of vertices processed during this time interval. Say that block Bi is good if it – or, more precisely, the
infinite sequence of random variables Xi,1 , Xi,2 , . . . – satisfies the following property.

Definition 7.2.2. For s ≥ 1, let Yi,s be the number of Xi,1 , Xi,2 , . . . , Xi,s which equal 1. Then a block
Bi is good if, for all s ∈ [dk/2, dk], we have


                                       (1 + ε)d−1 s ≤ Yi,s ≤ (1 + 3ε)d−1 s


A block is bad if it is not good.
164                                                              Long cycles in percolated vertex expanders


      It is worth noting here that whether or not a block is good or bad does not depend at all on the state
of the graph when the block is processed, as it depends only on the infinite sequence of random variables
Xi,1 , Xi,2 , . . . , which are chosen entirely independently of G. For example, it is entirely possible a
priori that zero queries are performed while processing block Bi (if none of the top k vertices in A have
any un-queried edges to U , though we will in due course show that this is, with high probability, never
the case), and we would still deem the block good or bad according to Definition 7.2.2 by looking at
random variables whose values are never used. It is also possible that far more than dk queries will
be made while processing a block Bi , but, as each positive query leads to a vertex being added to A,
controlling only the first dk queries is sufficient to show that A continues to grow. The range of values
[dk/2, dk] in Definition 7.2.2 arise from those required to prove the results that we will use (in particular
in Lemma 7.3.4).
      Let F (t) be the graph on V with edge set consisting of all edges which have been queried with
positive results in steps up to and including t.
      We may note that, for all times t, the set A(t) is a path in F (t), which is a forest. This fact will be
used in the final two update rules of the algorithm, which we may note also preserve the fact that A is a
path and F a forest. As A also has a notion of a “top” coming from the algorithm, it is in fact linearly
ordered, and so we may consider the set of all vertices above a given vertex in A. We will also consider
the graph F (t) − A(t), which consists of the forest F (t) with all edges in the path A(t) removed.
      We are now ready to define the final steps of our modified DFS algorithm.

      • If t = t2ik for an integer i, and the previous 2k blocks have all been good, then let x be the first
        vertex in B(2i−1)k , and let y be the vertex in Â(t + 1) closest to x in F (t) by graph distance.
        If there is no path from x to Â(t + 1), then the algorithm stops and fails. Add y to S, i.e. set
        S(t + 1) = S(t) ∪ {y}. Set A(t + 1) = Â(t + 1) and W (t + 1) = Ŵ (t + 1).
      • If t = tj for an integer j, and the block Bj just processed was bad, then let y be the top vertex in
        S(t), which is in Â(t + 1). If either S(t) is empty or the top vertex of S(t) is not in Â(t + 1), then
        the algorithm stops and fails. Let H be the set of vertices in Â(t + 1) which are above y, and let H ′
        consist of H along with, for each z ∈ H, all vertices in the connected component of z in the graph
        F (t) − Â(t + 1). Set S(t + 1) = S(t) \ {y}, T (t + 1) = T (t) ∪ H ′ , A(t + 1) = Â(t + 1) \ H ′ ,
        and W (t + 1) = Ŵ (t + 1) \ H ′ .

      We will prove in due course that, with high probability, the algorithm does not fail and that, at all
times t we have S(t) ⊆ A(t). We will refer to any vertex in S(t) for any t as safe, even if we are
considering a time at which the vertex has already been removed from S.
      Say that a vertex v is processed at time t if v ∈
                                                      / W (t − 1) and v ∈ W (t). Let ti be the first time at
which precisely ik vertices have been processed (noting that only one vertex can be processed at each
time step).
7.3. Proof of main result                                                                                165


    Before proceeding, we make a few simple observations about the algorithm.

Observation 7.2.3. The following points hold deterministically for all t.

      • There are no edges of Gp between W (t) and U (t), and
      • Any path in F (t) from a vertex of T (t) to a vertex of A(t) ∪ W (t) must pass through a safe vertex.

    We close this section with some further definitions, which we will use in Section 7.3.
    We say that x ∈ A(t) ∪ W (t) is a W -descendant at time t of y ∈ A(t) if x, y are in the same
component of the graph F (t) − A(t).
    The height at time t of a vertex x ∈ A(t)∪W (t) is, letting y ∈ A(t) be such that x is a W -descendant
of y at time t, the number of vertices in A(t) beneath y.
    Every vertex v ∈ A(t) ∪ W (t) is added to the stack A at some unique time. Let Cj be the set of
those vertices v which were added to A between times tj−1 + 1 and tj .
    At time t, two vertices x, y in the connected component of F (t) containing A(t) are connected by a
unique path Px,y (t) (as F (t) is a forest); write x ∼t y if this path exists. We will define the distance ρ
between x and y to be the length of this path:


                              ρ(x, y) = min{|Px,y (t)| : t ≥ 0 and x ∼t y}.


By convention, we take the minimum of the empty set to be infinite. When we refer to the “distance”
between two vertices, we mean distance in the sense of ρ(x, y). Note that, as A(t) ∪ W (t) ∪ T (t) is a
forest, as soon as x and y are connected by a path, this will be the unique path between them for the rest
of time, and so as soon as there is a path from x to y at a time t, this is the shortest path between these
points in A(τ ) ∪ W (τ ) ∪ T (τ ) for all τ ≥ t.
    Finally, define aj := |A(tj )| to be the height of the stack A at time tj , and let hj be the topmost
vertex in A(tj ).


7.3      Proof of main result

The proof of Theorem 7.2.1 is performed via a series of lemmas. We will carry several assumptions
through many of these lemmas, so we introduce the following notation for ease of referencing.

(Ψi ) Either block i is bad or ai − ai−1 ≥ εk/4.
 (Φ) There are at most εk long edges in G and for all s we have Zs ≤ se−αk/2 .
(Ξi ) For all j with k − 1 ≤ j ≤ i, the height of the stack A satisfies aj ≥ k + 1.

Throughout this section, we assume all bounds from Theorem 7.2.1 hold. In particular,

      • 300d−1/2 ≤ ε ≤ 1/8, and
166                                                                  Long cycles in percolated vertex expanders


      • k ≥ ε−2 d.


      Note that if ρ(x, y) ≥ s is finite and Gp also contains an edge connecting x and y, then Gp has a
cycle of length at least s. Indeed, call an edge xy of G long if there is a time t at which ρ(t; x, y) is both
finite and at least αkd where
                                                   α := ε2 /100.

      If we can find many long edges of G then we can apply sprinkling and with high probability one of
these edges will be added to our percolated graph, giving us a long cycle.
      Before working through the lemmas in turn, we record the following simple bound which we will
use on several occasions.


Lemma 7.3.1. The following bound holds.


                                                 exp(2αk) > 8k.                                         (7.3.1)

                                                      2αk 1−1/c 1/c c

                              2αk 1−1/c   ε−2+4/c 3001−1/c   82−4/c 3001−1/c
                                        ≥                  ≥                 .

Using c = 4, the above is at least 81/c if and only if 8(3003/4 ) > 200(81/4 ), which can be checked to

Lemma 7.3.2. Let Ej be the event that block j is good. Then P(Ej ) ≥ 1 − dk exp(−ε2 k/12), and Ej
is independent of all the events E1 , E2 , . . . , Ej−1 and Ej+1 , Ej+2 , . . . .


Proof. Firstly, independence follows from the fact that whether block Bj is good or bad is determined
entirely by the infinite sequence of independent random variables Xj,1 , Xj,2 , . . . , and each block has its
own, independent, sequence of random variables.
      It remains to prove the given bound for fixed j. Fix s ∈ [dk/2, dk], and observe that Yj,s = Xj,1 +
Xj,2 + · · · + Xj,s is a binomial random variable. Noting that E[Yj,s ] = p1 s = (1 + 2ε)d−1 s, we may
7.3. Proof of main result                                                                          167


apply Chernoff’s inequality (Theorem 7.1.1) to find that


              1 − P (1 + ε)d−1 s ≤ Yj,s ≤ (1 + 3ε)d−1 s = P |Yj,s − E[Yj,s ]| > εd−1 s

bound.



    We now deduce from Lemma 7.3.2 that, with high probability, there are very few bad blocks.


Lemma 7.3.3. Let Zs be the number of the first s blocks which are bad. Then, with probability at least
1 − exp(−αk/2), for all s we have
                                            Zs ≤ se−2αk .

                            dk exp(−ε2 k/12) = dk exp(−8αk) < exp(−4αk),

s0 := exp(3αk), and note that


              P(Zs0 = 0) ≥ (1 − exp(−4αk))s0 ≥ 1 − s0 exp(−4αk) = 1 − exp(−αk),


where the second inequality follows from Bernoulli’s inequality. Thus with probability at least 1 −

(Theorem 7.1.1) to obtain

Lemma 7.3.4. Assume that Φ holds and block i is good. Then, if there are at most εdk/2 vertices
x ∈ F (ti ) such that there is some y ∈ Bi with ρ(x, y) ≤ αdk, then ai − ai−1 ≥ εk/4, i.e. Ψi holds.

Proof. Due to the expansion of G, we know that there is a set X ⊆ V \ Bi of vertices such that |X| = dk
and every vertex in X is adjacent in G to some vertex of Bi . As we know by our assumption Φ that there
are at most εk long edges in G, we must have that


                                      |X ∩ U (ti )| ≥ (d − εd/2 − ε)k.


As all vertices in Bi are by definition processed at times before ti , we know that Bi ⊆ W (ti ), and
thus every edge from Bℓ to X ∩ U (ti ) has been queried by the DFS algorithm at a time between ti−1
and ti , and these queries have returned negative. Thus, between times ti−1 and ti , there were at least
(d−εd/2−ε)k ∈ [dk/2, dk] negative responses to queries and so, in particular, at least that many queries
in total. Due to Definition 7.2.2, there must therefore have also been at least (1 + ε)(1 − ε/2 − εd−1 )k
positive responses to queries.
      Noting that every positive response leads to the path A growing by one vertex, we see that


                                 ai − ai−1 ≥ (1 + ε)(1 − ε/2 − εd−1 )k − k.                           (7.3.2)


It thus suffices to prove that the right-hand side of (7.3.2) is at least εk/4. We first note that ε/2+εd−1 ≤
2ε/3, which follows from d ≥ 300ε−2 . Thus


                              ai − ai−1 ≥ (1 + ε)(1 − 2ε/3)k − k ≥ εk/4,


so long as ε ≤ 1/8, which holds by assumption.

      We now use Lemma 7.3.4 to deduce the desired result—that Ψi holds for all i—under the assumption
that there are no bad blocks. This allows our argument to “get off the ground,” so to speak, as we can
ensure that a safe vertex has been produced before we need to consider the possibility of a bad block.
7.3. Proof of main result                                                                                  169


Indeed, at times during the processing of the first few blocks, the stack A may become empty, and the
algorithm may need to “restart”. However, as will be shown in due course, under the assumption Φ, the
stack A is never empty once a safe vertex has been created.

Lemma 7.3.5. If blocks B1 , B2 , . . . , Bi are all good and there are at most εk long edges in G, then for
all j ≤ i, we have aj − aj−1 ≥ εk/4.

Proof. We inductively apply Lemma 7.3.4, and thus it suffices to prove that there are at most εdk/2
vertices of F (ti ) within a distance αdk of some vertex of Bi . The base case is vacuous, and so assume
that Ψ1 , Ψ2 , . . . , Ψi−1 all hold As exactly k vertices are removed from A while Bi is being processed,
we know that, if x ∈ F (ti−1 ) has ρ(x, hi−1 ) ≥ αdk + k, then x cannot be within distance αdk of any
vertex of Bi . Thus we count the number of vertices in F (ti−1 ) which are within distance (αd + 1)k of
hi−1 , and then add on the contribution from F (ti ) \ F (ti−1 ).
    We know that, for any j ≤ i − 1, the distance from x ∈ Bj to hi−1 is at least (i − 1 − j)εk/4 due to
the induction hypothesis. Thus the only vertices in W (ti−1 ) which can be within distance of αdk + k of
hi−1 are in Bi−1 , Bi−2 , . . . , Bi−4αdε−1 −1 , and there are (4αdε−1 + 1)k vertices in these blocks. There
are at most αdk + k vertices in A(ti−1 ) within a distance αdk + k of hi−1 , as A(ti−1 ) is a path and hi−1
is its head. Finally, note that if |Ci | ≥ (1 + ε/4)k, then as |Bi | = k, we must have ai − ai−1 ≥ εk/4.
Thus we can assume that A(ti ) \ A(ti−1 ), which is contained in Ci , has at most (1 + ε/4)k vertices.
    Adding this all together, we can upper-bound the number of vertices within a distance αdk of some
vertex of Bi by

                                                             4             ε2          ε   
           (4αdε−1 + 1)k + αdk + k + (1 + ε/4)k =                   εd +       d + d−1 + + 2 k.
                                                             100           100          4

Applying the inequality d−1 ≤ ε2 /300 and its consequence εd ≥ 300ε−1 ≥ 300, the above can easily be
seen to be bounded above by εdk/2, as required to apply Lemma 7.3.4. Thus the induction is complete
and the lemma is proved.


Observation 7.3.6. For integers i ≥ j, assume that Bi , Bi+1 , . . . , Bj are good and Ψi , Ψi+1 , . . . , Ψj−1
all hold. Moreover assume that ai−1 ≥ k + 1, and let x ∈ A(ti−1 ) be such that ρ(x, hi−1 ) ≥ k + 1.
Then x ∈ A(tj ) and, in particular, the stack A is never empty at any time t with ti−1 ≤ t ≤ tj .

    Indeed, by definition a block can only remove k vertices from the stack A while it is being processed,
and our assumptions that Ψi , Ψi+1 , . . . , Ψj−1 hold and the blocks are good imply that ai−1 ≤ ai ≤
ai+1 ≤ · · · ≤ aj−1 .

Lemma 7.3.7. Assume that Ξi−1 and Ψi hold, that |S(ti−1 )| ≥ 1, and that the topmost safe vertex in
s ∈ S(ti−1 ) is at distance at least k + 1 from both ends of A. Then Ξi holds.
170                                                                Long cycles in percolated vertex expanders


Proof. We know from Ξi−1 and Observation 7.3.6 that the stack A is not empty at any time between ti−1
and ti , so it suffices to prove that ai ≥ k + 1.
      If the block Bi is good, then we know from our assumption Ψi that ai ≥ ai−1 + γk > k + 1, as
required.
      If the block Bi is bad, then we know from our assumptions on s that s is not removed from A while
block Bi is being processed, and that it has height at least k + 1, so ai ≥ k + 1, as required.


Lemma 7.3.8. Let ℓ be an integer, assume that Ψ1 , Ψ2 , . . . , Ψ(2ℓ+1)k and Ξ2(ℓ+1)k−1 all hold, and as-
sume that blocks B2ℓk , B2ℓk+1 , . . . , B(2ℓ+1)k , . . . , B2(ℓ+1)k are all good. Let s ∈ S(ti ) be the safe vertex
created after block B2(ℓ+1)k was processed, so s has a W -descendant u ∈ B(2ℓ+1)k . Then s was added
to A while block B(2ℓ+1)k−j was being processed for some j with 0 ≤ j ≤ 4ε−1 .

Proof. The hypothesis Ξ2(ℓ+1)k−1 along with Observation 7.3.6 means that the stack A was not empty
at any time between when the algorithm began to process B(2ℓ+1)k and when it finished processing
B2(ℓ+1)k . Therefore, the vertices in B(2ℓ+1)k are in the same connected component of F (t2(ℓ+1)k ) as A,
and so the algorithm does not fail in its attempt to produce a safe vertex s.
      There is some j such that s ∈ Cj , and we may note that, as u is a W -descendant of s, we must
have j ≥ 0. We first claim that j < k. Indeed, it suffices to prove a(2ℓ+1)k−1 − a2ℓk > k, due to
Observation 7.3.6 and that fact that, as s is safe, all blocks B2ℓk , B2ℓk+1 , . . . , B2(ℓ+1)k are good. As we
know that Ψ2ℓk , Ψ2ℓk+1 , . . . , Ψ(2ℓ+1)k all hold, we have that a(2ℓ+1)k−1 − a2ℓk ≥ ε(k − 1)k/4, and so it
suffices that ε(k − 1) > 4, which holds due to our lower bound k ≥ ε−2 d ≥ 300ε−4 .
      Thus 0 ≤ j < k, and so all the blocks B(2ℓ+1)k−j , B(2ℓ+1)k−j+1 , . . . , B(2ℓ+1)k are good. Therefore,
our assumptions Ψ(2ℓ+1)k−j , Ψ(2ℓ+1)k−j+1 , . . . , Ψ(2ℓ+1)k mean that a(2ℓ+1)k − a(2ℓ+1)k−j ≤ k implies
that j ≤ 4ε−1 , as required.


Lemma 7.3.9. Assume that Ψ1 , Ψ2 , . . . , Ψi−1 , Φ, and Ξi−1 all hold and that |S(ti−1 )| ≥ 1. Then,
for all s ∈ S(ti−1 ) and all times t with ti−1 ≤ t ≤ ti , the vertex s is at distance at least k + 1
from both ends of A(t), and so, in particular, is never moved to W (t). Moreover, for all safe vertices
u, v ∈ S(ti−1 ) ∪ T (ti−1 ), we have that ρ(u, v) ≥ εk 2 /4.

Proof. We prove this lemma by induction on i. In fact, we also prove that, for all j < i and every safe
vertex s ∈ S(tj ), we have ρ(s, hj ) ≥ εk 2 /4. The base case is vacuous as there are no safe vertices when
the algorithm starts, and so we prove the inductive step.
      Firstly, if the block Bj is bad, then the head hj is a safe vertex which has just been removed from S.
As εk 2 /4 > k, this safe vertex was not removed from A while Bj was being processed. The inductive
hypothesis implies that all vertices in S(tj ) are at distance at least εk 2 /4 from hj , as is required for the
induction to continue.
7.3. Proof of main result                                                                                  171


    If instead Bj is good, then let s ∈ S(tj−1 ) be the safe vertex in S nearest to the top of the stack. As,
again, εk 2 /4 > k, by Observation 7.3.6 the vertex s is not removed from the stack while Bj is processed.
Moreover, if j < i, then we have assumed that Ψj holds, so aj > aj−1 , and thus s is still at least distance
εk 2 /4 from the top of the stack after Bj is processed, as required.

    Finally, we must deal with the case of a new safe vertex s being created. We know by Lemma 7.3.8
that s ∈ C(2ℓ+1)k−j for some j with 0 ≤ j ≤ 4ε−1 . This immediately implies that s is not at the top of
the stack when it is created, and so we must show that s is at distance at least εk 2 /4 from the top of the
stack h2(ℓ+1)k . We know that blocks B(2ℓ+1)k , B(2ℓ+1)k+1 , . . . , B2(ℓ+1)k are good, and so the assump-
tions Ψ(2ℓ+1)k , Ψ(2ℓ+1)k+1 , . . . , Ψ2(ℓ+1)k imply that ρ(s, h2(ℓ+1)k ) ≥ εk 2 /4, as required. In particular,
4ε−1 < k, and so there is some non-zero number of good blocks B2ℓk , B2ℓk+1 , . . . , B(2ℓ+1)k−j . The
assumptions Ψ2ℓk , Ψ2ℓk+1 , . . . , Ψ(2ℓ+1)k−j then imply that the height of s is at least ((k − j)ε/4 − 1)k,
and so we must show that (k − j)ε/4 > 2. We know that j ≤ 4ε−1 , and so it is sufficient that kε > 12,
which holds.

    It remains to show that s is at distance at least εk 2 /4 from any other safe vertices in S(t2(ℓ+1)k ).
Indeed, by Observation 7.3.6, our assumptions Ψi , and the fact that all blocks surrounding the safe
vertex are good, it suffices to prove that ((2ℓ + 1)k − 4ε−1 − 2ℓk)k ≥ k. This reduces to k ≥ 4ε−1 + 1,
which is true due to our assumed lower bounds on k.



Lemma 7.3.10. Assuming that S(t) ∩ W (t) is empty for all t ≤ ti , we have that

                                                        j i k
                                           |S(ti )| ≥           − 2Zi .
                                                         2k

Moreover, if we assume Φ holds and let b be minimal such that block Bb is bad, then b > 4k and
|S(t)| ≥ 1 for all t ≥ tb .


Proof. We consider when vertices are added to or removed from S(ti ). If the blocks B2jk+1 , B2jk+2 , . . . , B(2j+1)k , . . .
are all good, then a safe vertex is created, and if any of these are bad, then a safe vertex is not created,
and one safe vertex is removed from S for every bad block. In particular, each bad block can use up one
safe vertex and prevent at most one safe vertex from being created, from which the claimed inequality
follows immediately.

    Finally, we must deduce the “moreover” part of the statement. First, we know by Lemma 7.3.3 that
b ≥ exp(2αk) > 4k (due to k ≥ 300ε−4 and ε ≥ 1/8), and so there are at least two safe vertices
produced before the first bad block. It thus remains to show that, for i ≥ exp(2αk), we have ⌊i/(2k)⌋ −
2Zi ≥ 1.

    Now let i = λ exp(2αk) for some λ ≥ 1. Applying our assumption Φ, and using the fact from
172                                                              Long cycles in percolated vertex expanders


Lemma 7.3.1 that exp(2αk) > 8k, we find that

                 j i k             j λ exp(2αk) k            3 exp(2αk)
                         − 2Zi =                    − 2λ ≥              − 2 ≥ 3k − 2 > 1,
                   2k                   2k                   4    2k

as required.


Lemma 7.3.11. Assuming that Ψ1 , Ψ2 , . . . , Ψi−1 , Φ, and Ξi−1 all hold, for all safe vertices s ∈ S(ti ),
there are at most εdk/4 vertices x ∈ A(ti ) ∪ W (ti ) ∪ T (ti ) satisfying ρ(s, x) ≤ (1 + αd)k.

Proof. We first show that we only need to consider vertices in A(ti ) ∪ W (ti ). Indeed, we know from
Observation 7.2.3 that any path from T (ti ) to Bi passes through a safe vertex q ∈ T (ti ) (and note that
 / S(ti )). Due to Lemma 7.3.9 and the fact that εk 2 /4 > (1 + αd)k (which easily follows from k ≥
q∈
ε−2 d), there are no other safe vertices within distance (1 + αd)k of s – in particular, ρ(s, q) ≥ (1 + αd)k
– and thus no vertices of T (ti ) within this distance. It thus suffices to prove that there are at most εkd/4
vertices of A(ti ) ∪ W (ti ) within distance (1 + αd)k of s.
      Assume that s was deemed safe after the processing of block B2(ℓ+1)k . Then the height of s at time ti
is between a(2ℓ+1)k−1 − k and a(2ℓ+1)k + k. To upper-bound the number of vertices which might be close
to s, we count the number of blocks in which any of their vertices are close to s, and add a contribution of
k vertices for all such blocks. We then add on the number of vertices in A close to s. Indeed, we bound
the number of blocks which contain a vertex with height at time ti between a(2ℓ+1)k−1 − 2k − αdk and
a(2ℓ+1)k + 2k + αdk.
      By symmetry, we focus on the lower bound. Block Bt can only contain a vertex sufficiently close
to s if at + k ≥ a(2ℓ+1)k−1 − 2k − αdk, i.e. a(2ℓ+1)k−1 − at ≤ (3 + αd)k. Note that, if we take
t = 2ℓk, then a(2ℓ+1)k−1 − a2ℓk ≥ ε(k − 1)k/4 as all blocks in question are good. This is in turn greater
than (3 + αd)k (as k ≥ ε−2 d and d ≥ 4), and so we need only to consider t > 2ℓk, and all blocks in
question are good, and the relevant Ψ-assumptions hold. Thus, letting t = (2ℓ + 1)k − j, we find that
a(2ℓ+1)k−1 − at ≥ ε(j − 1)k/4. Therefore ε(j − 1)k/4 ≤ (3 + αd)k, which implies that


                              j ≤ 4(3 + αd)ε−1 + 1 ≤ 8αdε−1 + 1 ≤ εd/10.


Thus at most εd/5 blocks can have a vertex within distance (1+αd)k of s. Finally, at most 2(1+αd)k+1
vertices of A can be within distance (1 + αd)k of s, as A is a path, and 2(1 + αd)k + 1 ≤ εdk/50 (as
follows from our bounds on ε and d). All in all, there can indeed be at most εdk/4 vertices within a
distance (1 + αd)k of s, as required.


Lemma 7.3.12. Assuming Ψ1 , Ψ2 , . . . , Ψi−1 , Φ, and Ξi−1 all hold, there are at most |Ci | + 3εdk/8
vertices x ∈ A(ti ) ∪ W (ti ) ∪ T (ti ) such that there is some y ∈ Bi with ρ(x, y) ≤ αdk.
7.3. Proof of main result                                                                                 173


Proof. If no block B1 , B2 , . . . , Bi is bad, then we are done by Lemma 7.3.5. Thus assume that there is
some bad block Bj with j ≤ i, and let j be maximal with this property. Let s be the safe vertex which
was removed from S(tj−1 ) (i.e. s was the vertex “backtracked” to after the bad block). Note that, due to
hypotheses Ψj+1 , Ψj+2 , . . . , Ψi−1 and Observation 7.3.6, only the top k vertices below s (inclusive) in
A(tj ) can be removed from A at times between tj and ti . Thus no vertex at distance more than (1 + αd)k
from s in F (tj−1 ) can ever be within distance αdk in F (t) of the top of A(t) for t ≥ tj . We know from
Lemma 7.3.11 that there are at most εdk/4 vertices within distance (1 + αd)k of s, so it remains to count
vertices in Cj+1 , Cj+2 , . . . , Ci which are close to s.
    By an argument identical to those made in the proof of Lemma 7.3.11, we know that there are at
most εdk/8 vertices within a distance (1 + αd)k > αdk of hi−1 which were added to A at times after tj
(and there could be far fewer if e.g. i is not much larger than j). Finally, all vertices in A(ti ) \ A(ti−1 )
are contained in Ci , and so the result follows.


    With the above lemmas in hand, we can finally prove Theorem 7.2.1.


Proof of Theorem 7.2.1. Assume first that there are more than εk long edges in G. Then, applying
sprinkling with p2 ≥ ε/d, we find that the probability that no long cycle is created is

                                                    ε εk       −ε2 k 
                                                1−         ≤ exp          .
                                                     d             d

We will deduce a contradiction by assuming that Φ holds, and then showing that the stack A grows in-
definitely, and thus in fact there must be at least εk long edges in G. We know from Lemma 7.3.3 that,
assuming that there are at most εk long edges in G, Φ holds with probability at least 1 − exp(−αk/2).
Thus, once we have deduced a contradiction, we will know that there is a long cycle in Gp with proba-
bility at least
                                      −αk               −ε2 k                −αk 
                           1 − exp               − exp               ≥ 1 − 2 exp        ,
                                        2                    d                     2d
    as required. Thus assume for contradiction that Φ holds.
    We will derive a contradiction from our assumption that there is no long cycle by proving that, for
                     i
all i, |S(ti )| ≥ ⌊ 4k ⌋. This would mean that S (and by extension the stack A) grows indefinitely, which
is impossible in a finite graph G.
    The key inductive claim is that Ψi and Ξi hold for all i. By Lemma 7.3.5, if b is the index of the first
bad block, then we know that Ψ1 , Ψ2 , . . . , Ψb−1 all hold (and if there is no bad block, then we know that
all Ψi hold). Then Lemma 7.3.10 tells us that b > 4k and at all times t ≥ tb , the set S(t) of safe vertices is
never empty. In particular, the algorithm will never fail due to no safe vertex being available. Moreover,
we know that, for all j with k − 1 ≤ j < b, Ψ1 , Ψ2 , . . . , Ψj all hold, and so |A(tj )| ≥ (k − 1)εk/4,
which is greater than k + 1 (as can be easily deduced from k ≥ ε−2 d and ε ≤ 1/8). In particular, Ξb−1
174                                                              Long cycles in percolated vertex expanders


holds. Note that, if Ξi−1 holds, then the stack A(t) is connected for all times t with tb−1 ≤ t ≤ ti . In
particular, the algorithm does not fail due to block B(2ℓ+1)k being disconnected from A when trying to
form a safe vertex.
      Assume that Ψ1 , Ψ2 , . . . , Ψi−1 and Ξi−1 all hold. Then, by Lemma 7.3.12, there are at most |Ci | +
3εdk/4 vertices within distance αdk of some vertex of Bi .
      If block i is bad, then Ψi holds immediately, and we know that a safe vertex is available. If block i is
good and |Ci | ≤ εdk/8, then Lemma 7.3.4 tells us that Ψi holds. On the other hand, if |Ci | ≥ (1+ε/4)k,
then we know from Observation 7.3.6 that Ψi holds. Thus, to show that Ψi holds, it is sufficient that
εdk/8 ≥ (1 + ε/4)k. Indeed, 1 + ε/4 < 2 and εd ≥ 600, so this does indeed go through, and Ψi holds.
Finally, by Lemma 7.3.7, with the required bounds on distances between safe vertices and the ends of
the stack coming from Lemma 7.3.9, Ξi also holds.
      This completes the induction, and so Ψi and Ξi hold for all i. However, Lemma 7.3.10 then implies
                i                                                                         i
that |Si | ≥ ⌊ 2k ⌋ − 2Zi , and our assumption Φ implies that this is in turn at least ⌊ 2k ⌋ − 2i exp(−2αk).
                                         i      i      i
Then by Lemma 7.3.1, this is at least ⌊ 2k ⌋ − 4k ≥ ⌊ 4k ⌋ − 1, and so the stack A grows without bound.
This contradiction concludes the proof of Theorem 7.2.1.


7.4      Concluding remarks

We have demonstrated the existence of long cycles in percolated expanders with probability just above
the critical threshold for having a large component. A question that remains is how small ε > 0 can be
while percolation with probability p = (1 + ε)/d still results in long paths or cycles. Indeed, as stated in
the introduction, Theorem 1.6.1 of [38] gives that, with high probability, Gp has a path of length Ω(dk)
when ε > Cd−1 for a sufficiently large constant C.

Question 7.4.1. Fix d > 1, let k ∈ N, and let the graph G be an ({k}, d)-vertex-expander. What (for
fixed d and k → ∞) is the minimal order of magnitude of ε0 = ε0 (d) > 0 such that, for all ε > ε0 and
p = (1 + ε)/d, the percolated graph Gp with high probability has a path/cycle of length Ωε (dk)?

      In a different direction, much remains to be understood about what more complex structures occur
with high probability in Gp . Note that, due to the fact that ε is bounded from below by a function of
d while k (and thus also |G|) is sent to infinity, it is not unreasonable to expect more complex struc-
ture to emerge in Gp . In particular, we make the following conjecture, which would be a significant
strengthening of Theorem 1.6.3.

Conjecture 7.4.2. Fix d ∈ N and let H be a graph of maximum degree 3. Let ε ≥ ε0 (d) = d−O(1) ,
let k ∈ N be sufficiently large in terms of d and ε, and let G be an ({k}, d)-vertex-expander. Then, for
p = (1 + ε)/d, the percolated graph Gp contains a subdivision of H with high probability.

      It is plausible that the maximum degree of H in Conjecture 7.4.2 could even be taken as large as d.
